--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This is the title page of the Latin edition of Jan van Ruusbroec's "The Spiritual Espousals," published in Paris. The translation maintains the formal structure of a 16th-century legal and theological title page.</meta>

¶ By the devout and venerable father <term>John Ruysbroeck</term><note>original: "Ioānis Ruſberi." Jan van Ruusbroec (1293–1381) was a Flemish mystic and one of the most important spiritual writers of the late Middle Ages.</note>, priest and canon of the <term>Augustinian</term><note>original: "obſeruantiæ beati Auguſtini." Referring to the Canons Regular of St. Augustine, the religious order to which Ruusbroec belonged at the Groenendaal Priory.</note> observance: Three books on the **Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage**.

¶ The first book concerns the adornment of the moral and active life.

¶ The second book concerns the adornment of the spiritual and <term>affective</term><note>original: "affectiuæ." In mystical theology, this refers to the "affections" or the movement of the heart and will toward God, rather than just intellectual understanding.</note> life.

¶ The third book concerns the adornment of the <term>super-essential</term><note>original: "ſuperessētialis." A term used to describe a state of being that exists beyond or above the created essence, specifically the soul's union with God's own essence.</note> and contemplative life.

¶ For sale at the workshop of <term>Henri Estienne</term><note>original: "Henrici Stephani." Estienne (1460–1520) was a famous humanist printer in Paris and the founder of the Estienne printing dynasty.</note>, printer, located in the district of the School of Decrees <note>original: "Scholæ Decretorum." This refers to the Faculty of Canon Law at the University of Paris.</note>.

<summary>This is the title page for the Latin translation of Jan van Ruusbroec's "The Spiritual Espousals," detailing the three-part structure of the work covering active, spiritual, and contemplative life. It identifies the author as an Augustinian canon and the printer as the renowned Henri Estienne of Paris.</summary>

<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, The Spiritual Espousals, Henri Estienne, mysticism, Augustinian Canons, contemplative life, active life, super-essential life, Paris printing</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues from the title page of Jan van Ruusbroec's "The Spiritual Espousals." It contains the beginning of the introductory epistle by the humanist scholar Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples (Jacobus Faber Stapulensis) to his friend Raymond. This preface establishes the theological and monastic context for why Ruusbroec's mystical writings are being published.</meta>

# ¶ JACQUES LEFÈVRE TO HIS DEAR RAYMOND, GREETINGS.

<margin><unclear>Peter</unclear> <note>original: "Petrus." This marginal note appears to be a name, possibly a reader or owner's mark.</note> <image-desc>A small sketch of a manicule (pointing hand) towards the text</image-desc></margin>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' containing a stag standing amidst stylized foliage.</image-desc>Since every state of the particular <term>religious orders</term><note>original: "religionum." Here referring specifically to monastic and mendicant orders like the Carthusians or Celestines rather than "religions" in the modern global sense.</note>, dearest Raymond, is a certain flight from the world and an approach to things beyond the world—a forsaking of the many and the divided, and a clinging to the One—it follows that each order possesses as much truth as it cleaves to things beyond the world, and to that One which is above all oneness. In this union and cleaving consists true contemplation and the sweetest fruit of the contemplative life. This fruit is known by few, as it is achieved not without secret <term>infusions</term><note>original: "immissionibus." In mystical theology, this refers to the "pouring in" of divine grace or knowledge directly into the soul.</note> and divine visitations. Through these, as far as is permitted to mortal man, the spiritual state is unveiled. 

For the blessed souls who have already cast off the garment of mortality live the spiritual state perfectly; those, however, who have not yet cast it off can indeed live in it, but not yet perfectly—not of themselves, but through a certain divine arrival. "I go," says the Savior, "and I come to you" <note>original: "Vado... & venio ad vos." A reference to the Gospel of John 14:18 or 14:28, often interpreted by mystics as the internal "coming" of Christ to the soul.</note>. And I believe there is no particular religious order in which there are not, or have not been, some who have perceived something of this sweetness, especially those that are more solitary, withdrawn, and strict. 

Therefore, I would not believe the <term>Carthusians</term><note>A monastic order known for extreme solitude and silence, founded by St. Bruno in 1084.</note> and <term>Celestines</term><note>A branch of the Benedictines founded by Peter of Morrone (later Pope Celestine V) that emphasized asceticism.</note> to hold the lowest places in this sublimity of the contemplative life; and rightly so, both because of their greater solitude and the strictness of their life, and because of their most watchful providence in defending their state from falling away. On this account, they also love the books of contemplative men most of all. This is not because books make contemplatives most perfect, but because they prepare and incite beginners; God, however, completes the rest. For in a higher way, a heavenly ray and a certain divine encounter make men contemplatives. 

Nevertheless, the Celestines of <note>Lefèvre lists several monastic houses in France.</note> Mantes, Sens, Marcoussis, and Paris, and the Carthusians of Saint-Omer, Mont-Dieu, Bourgfontaine, and the "Green Valley" of the Parisian solitude <note>original: "vallis viridis." Referring to the Carthusian monastery of Vauvert in Paris, located near the current Jardin du Luxembourg.</note>, have frequently shared most of these kinds of books with us, and they continue to share them most kindly every day as need requires; among whom...

<summary>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples writes to Raymond, praising the contemplative life found within strict monastic orders like the Carthusians and Celestines. He argues that while books help beginners prepare for divine union, true contemplation is a gift from God, and he acknowledges the various monasteries that provided the manuscripts for this publication.</summary>
<keywords>Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples, Raymond, contemplation, monasticism, Carthusians, Celestines, mystical union, Jan van Ruusbroec, religious life, Paris monasteries</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory letter by Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples to his friend Raymond. Having established the nature of the contemplative life, Lefèvre now addresses specific criticisms leveled against the author, Jan van Ruusbroec, by the famous theologian Jean Gerson.</meta>

<sig>A ij</sig>

<margin>book</margin>
You have avidly read the book shared with you regarding the adornment of the <term>spiritual marriage</term><note>original: "spiritualium nuptiarum." This refers to Ruusbroec's most famous work, *The Spiritual Espousals*, which describes the soul's journey toward union with God.</note>, which Jan van Ruusbroec—a devout and religious father of the <term>Augustinian Canons Regular</term><note>original: "canonicę obseruationis beati Augustini." A branch of the Augustinian order known for its strict adherence to the Rule.</note>—composed some time ago, around the era of <term>Boniface IX</term><note>Pope from 1389 to 1404. Ruusbroec actually died in 1381, but Lefèvre is providing a general historical timeframe.</note>.
<margin>mar-riage and other wed-dings</margin>
However, it troubled you while reading that <term>Gerson</term><note>Jean Gerson (1363–1429), the influential Chancellor of the University of Paris, who famously criticized Ruusbroec's mysticism for potentially leading to pantheistic errors.</note>—a devout man of grave authority, who bore a religious mind beneath a secular habit—judged the author of that work to be insufficiently learned, even rebuking him in certain matters as one in error. This should have moved you but little. For there is only one who finds fault, while a great crowd of holy men defends him. This fact cannot escape you if you read the devout works of <term>Thomas à Kempis</term><note>original: "Ioānis Quempiſij." Referring to Thomas à Kempis, the probable author of *The Imitation of Christ* and a fellow member of the Devotio Moderna movement that revered Ruusbroec.</note>, a man of blameless life.
<margin>By these things committed [to those] who believe [the] father [to the] brothers</margin>
Gerson, in the judgment of many, was both devout and possessed of much secular learning; and this man [Ruusbroec] was likewise devout and possessed of much spiritual learning. And both are good and to be revered: the latter in the cloister, the former in the world.

But indeed, that this book was first published in the native speech and the <term>vernacular tongue</term><note>original: "vernacula lĩgua." Ruusbroec wrote in Middle Dutch so that his teachings would be accessible to those without a university education in Latin.</note> is not sufficient proof that the author was of little learning. For even the most learned man can publish books in the vernacular—perhaps far better than an unlearned one.
<margin>It is difficult</margin>
And the grammarians who read this will judge the author to be remarkably elegant for that time; the rhetoricians will find him eloquent; the philosophers will see him as one skilled in the secrets of nature; the astrologers as a knower of the times; the physicians as a master of diseases and health; and the theologians as a master of divine matters.

<image-desc>A small decorative manicule (pointing hand) points to the next paragraph.</image-desc>

I think, therefore, that this happened to Gerson (an upright man indeed): that he happened upon a corrupt manuscript. This has happened to me elsewhere. But when I came upon a true and corrected copy, having previously judged the author unlearned, I changed my opinion and began to admire him. By a similar reasoning, I think he [Gerson] was mistaken. And later <term>Trithemius</term><note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a famous polymath and abbot who likely repeated Gerson's criticisms in his bibliographical catalogs.</note> followed him through his own writings, as one who wrote similar things based on hearsay. For neither the one nor the other was of such an unfair mind or such malignant judgment that (if they had seen the true volume) they would have ever accused the man of having little learning. Read, therefore, with confidence what Gerson himself would have read with confidence and even with delight. Farewell, progressing in piety.

->At Paris. On the <term>Nones of August</term><note>August 5th, 1512.</note><-

<summary>Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples defends Jan van Ruusbroec against the historical criticisms of Jean Gerson, arguing that Gerson's accusations of "unlearnedness" and heresy were based on faulty manuscripts or a misunderstanding of Ruusbroec's use of the vernacular.</summary>
<keywords>Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples, Jan van Ruusbroec, Jean Gerson, Thomas à Kempis, Johannes Trithemius, spiritual marriage, vernacular theology, manuscript errors, defense of mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the table of contents to the actual beginning of the manuscript. It features the introductory epistle (preface) from the author, Jan van Ruusbroec, to his fellow monks. He explains the reason for translating his work from the local vernacular (Middle Dutch) into Latin.</meta>

# THE EPISTLE OF JOHN RUUSBROEC, Priest, to the brothers of Groenendael <note>original: "Capella Rosana." This refers to the monastery of Groenendael (Green Valley) in the Sonian Forest near Brussels, where Ruusbroec was prior.</note> in Flanders, concerning the interpretation of his book *On the Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage*.

<margin>

V.
I.
I.

</margin>

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'E' depicting a figure, likely a monk or saint, standing in a landscape with foliage and clouds.</image-desc> You have requested, most dear brothers, with humble and devout letters, that I interpret for you into the Latin tongue our little book *On the Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage*, which we formerly wrote in the Brabantine dialect of Middle Dutch <note>original: "teutonico Brabantino."</note>. Although (as you have written) because of the differences between the dialects of northern Flanders and Brabant, you cannot fully grasp its flavor; yet, because of a certain proximity of speech, you perceive from it a faint scent of such sweetness that you in no way doubt that in it is shown not only all holiness, but indeed the perfection of all holiness, the fulfillment of all perfection, and the end of all fulfillment. There is indeed a great multitude of sweetness hidden for those who hunger, and it is worthy of being brought to light by a Latin translation (as you asked), or rather by the light of any tongue—if indeed it were as you believe. Certainly, you should have been careful lest, perhaps emulating only the fragrant mandrakes with Rachel <note>A biblical reference to Genesis 30. In medieval exegesis, Rachel (representing the contemplative life) seeking mandrakes was often interpreted as seeking the "sweetness" or "fragrance" of spiritual gifts.</note>, you seemed to look for a knot in a bulrush <note>original: "in scirpo nodum." A common Latin proverb meaning to look for a difficulty where none exists, or to find a problem in something that is perfectly smooth and simple.</note>.

But what shall we say to these things? First of all, we give thanks to JESUS CHRIST, who by a share of apostolic grace has granted us to be the "good scent" of Himself <note>original: "bonum sui nos esse dedit odorem." A reference to 2 Corinthians 2:15: "For we are the sweet aroma of Christ to God."</note>—if not in every place, at least in yours. For the rest, even if it is not as you believe, yet because you believe, let it be done to you. Indeed, a wonderful faith shall be reaped. Since this is the case, I wish that we could speak in the tongues of all nations, so that we might be able to reveal the grace of such great perfection (as you wrote) to all peoples. Surely the labor would be small if compensated by the gain of such common salvation. Therefore, we have yielded to your petitions; rather, we have obeyed the command of charity, and that very book of which you wrote...

<summary>This page contains the opening of an introductory letter by Jan van Ruusbroec, responding to a request from Flemish monks to translate his famous work into Latin because they struggled with the Brabantian dialect of the original Middle Dutch.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, Groenendael, Flanders, Brabant, Latin translation, Spiritual Marriage, monastic correspondence, Middle Dutch</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory epistle to the brothers of Groenendael and begins the "Author's Preface" to the main work. The translator (likely William Jordaens or Laurentius Surius, though Ruusbroec speaks in the first person here) discusses the philosophy of translation before transitioning into the famous opening of the treatise based on the Parable of the Ten Virgins.</meta>

->EPISTLE<-

...we have translated into Latin; or rather, we have clothed the meaning of the book in Latin garments. For this reason, perhaps, because it is dressed in a foreign habit, it may be held as either changed, or less lovable, by those skilled in both languages. However, according to your Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the patron saint of translators, who famously advocated for translating "sense for sense" rather than "word for word."</note>, the best method of translation is where the property of the language into which one translates is observed (if such a thing can be done).

Indeed, brothers, do not let the dissonance of the "garments" <note>The "garments" refers to the Latin language vs. the original Middle Dutch.</note> dismay you, provided that the same harmony of meaning resonates for you. But also, most dear ones, let it not weary you to grant an exchange of your prayers for our labor; so that we too, having become followers of its scent, may deserve at some time to be sharers with you—you who wrote that you have already smelled the fragrance of this work beneath the husks of our letters.

## ¶ THE AUTHOR’S PREFACE to his book on the 
## Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage.

<image-desc>Woodcut decorative initial 'E' with floral scrollwork within a double-lined square border.</image-desc> **Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him.** <note>original: "Ecce sponsus venit exite obviam ei." A quotation from the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25:6, which forms the structural foundation of this entire work.</note> Wake up, you wise virgins, who for a long time (while the bridegroom delayed) have all drowsed and likewise slept. For now at midnight (that is, in the deep night), while faith makes its journey and hastens toward the brightness of the heavenly homeland, the cry of the Gospel grows louder every day, saying: "Behold, the bridegroom comes." Arise quickly; take oil in your vessels; adorn your lamps; and go out to meet the bridegroom. 

You ask, who is this bridegroom? He is surely the one to whom the apostle betrothed you, to present you as a chaste virgin <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 11:2.</note>. He is truly beautiful in form beyond the sons of men, coming forth from the royal court of the virginal womb like a bridegroom proceeding from his chamber. And if he is only a bridegroom because he has a bride, who then is the bride of this bridegroom? It is indeed human nature, created after the image and likeness of God.

<summary>The author concludes his introductory letter by defending his translation style and then begins the preface by quoting the Parable of the Ten Virgins to introduce Christ as the Bridegroom of human nature.</summary>
<keywords>St. Jerome, translation theory, Spiritual Marriage, Bridegroom, Bride, human nature, Parable of the Ten Virgins, Matthew 25:6, Groenendael</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological narrative following the opening citation of Matthew 25:6. It establishes the "history of the Bride" (humanity), moving from her creation in Paradise to her fall into the "region of dissimilarity," and finally to her redemption through the Incarnation of the Son of God.</meta>

->PREFACE 2<-

->a.ij.<-

...by its very Creator <note>original: "conditore"</note> in a higher place on earth, full of all pleasantness, riches, and delights—namely, in the earthly paradise—with all other creatures on earth subject to her. And her Creator had adorned her with grace and instructed her in heavenly **discipline**, having indeed given her a mandate by which, through obedience, she might have deserved to be indissolubly joined to her Bridegroom in a bond of perpetual faith, with every occasion for punishment, guilt, or misery removed. 

And behold, a **tyrant**—the prince of darkness, indeed, by whose envy death entered into the world—clothed in the form of a **cunning serpent**, first overthrew feminine weakness with shrewd persuasion; and finally, aided by feminine charms, he subjugated the masculine stronghold. <note>The author uses the traditional medieval allegory where the serpent targets Eve (feminine weakness) to eventually conquer Adam (the masculine stronghold/reason).</note>

Therefore that tyrant snatched away the **bride of God**, seduced by deceitful counsel; and he drove her far from her homeland into an unknown region, subdued by poverty, misery, and captivity, as if she were never to return. But when the **fullness of time** <note>original: "plenitudo temporis," a biblical reference to the moment of Christ's birth (Galatians 4:4).</note> had come, in which God, having pitied the misery of his beloved, had arranged by his hidden counsel to set her free: he sent his Son into the temple of the virginal womb, from whose purest blood he joined her to himself in a personal union by an indissoluble covenant. 

Of this nuptial celebration, the Archangel **Gabriel** was the messenger, and the **Holy Spirit** was the celebrant, while the undefiled Virgin provided her consent. Thus, therefore, the Son of God, the Bridegroom of his faithful, personally assumed our nature. Rising from on high, he visited us in the **region of dissimilarity** <note>original: "regione dissimilitudinis." A concept from St. Augustine and St. Bernard of Clairvaux describing the soul's state of being lost and unlike God after the Fall.</note>; he faithfully instructed us in heavenly disciplines; with **athletic sweat** <note>A reference to the physical and spiritual struggle of Christ's Passion, compared here to the labor of an athlete.</note> he manfully conquered our enemies; having achieved a full trophy (the sting of death being overcome), he turned back our captivity **like a torrent in the south.** <note>A quotation from Psalm 126:4, symbolizing a sudden and powerful restoration of fortune.</note>

He redeemed us with his precious blood; he restored us to liberty by his **baptism**; he endowed us with his **sacraments** and graces, so that finally, full of virtues, we might run to meet him in the hall of glory—

<summary>This page presents a salvation-history allegory, depicting humanity as a bride who fell from Paradise into the captivity of the devil, only to be rescued and "wedded" by Christ through the Incarnation.</summary>
<keywords>humanity as bride, The Fall, Incarnation, Archangel Gabriel, region of dissimilarity, redemption, spiritual marriage</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page concludes the theological introduction regarding the soul's union with God and transitions into the formal preface of the work. It establishes the structure of the treatise based on the biblical cry, "Behold, the bridegroom comes."</meta>

### PREFACE.

...and may we eternally enjoy his glorious embraces. We, however, while we yet wander in the body <note>original: "peregrinamur in corpore," a reference to 2 Corinthians 5:6.</note>, let us listen to the gospel cry, which is spoken in the word of God: "**Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him**." <note>original: "ecce sponsus venit exite obviam ei." This verse from Matthew 25:6 serves as the foundation for the entire book.</note> In the words of this cry, the heavenly proclaimer sets forth four things for us to note. First, in this word of pointing, "**behold**," he excites our vision as if with a finger <note>original: "digitaliter." In medieval Latin, this refers to pointing with the finger (digitus) to focus attention.</note> and, in a way, gives a command to see. Whoever, therefore, persisting in their blindness, does not even care to raise an eyelid at the sound of this voice, is deservedly already sentenced to eternal darkness. Secondly, he shows us the saving and visible coming of the bridegroom, saying: "**the bridegroom comes**." Thirdly, he teaches and commands what we ought to do once the bridegroom is seen to be coming, saying: "**go out**." Fourthly, he suggests to us the benefit of our "going out" and the glorious fruit of our good labors, adding: "**to meet him**"—that is, meeting **CHRIST** the highest King with the affections of love, clinging to him in the embraces of enjoyment, and penetrating into him by the grace of union, so that two may become one in spirit. <note>original: "duo in spiritu uno," a reference to the mystical state where the soul is united to God while maintaining its distinct essence.</note> And because the wise virgins did not presume to meet the bridegroom except with adorned lamps, lest they suffer rejection along with the foolish virgins <note>The OCR reads "tuís" (yours), but the context of the parable of the Ten Virgins indicates "fatuís" (foolish).</note>, we too shall learn to adorn our lamps (that is, our lives). We shall explain the aforementioned words concerning the **threefold adornment of life**, seeking the help of the Holy Trinity, so that we may finally be able to say with that proverb of Solomon: "*Behold, I have described it to you in a threefold way.*" <note>Proverbs 22:20.</note> In the first book, therefore, we shall deal with the adornment of the **moral active life**, which is necessary for all who are to be saved. In the second, we shall discourse more extensively on the adornment of the **spiritual and affective life**, which many of the faithful attain with the cooperation of God's grace. In the third, we shall touch upon the adornment of the **super-intellectual and contemplative life**, which is utterly **God-like** <term>deiform: from "deiformis," describing a soul that has been transformed by grace to reflect God's own nature</term>; to which, because of its excellence, few attain.

<summary>Jan van Ruusbroec introduces the structural framework of his treatise, using the parable of the Ten Virgins to divide the spiritual journey into three distinct stages: the active, spiritual, and contemplative lives.</summary>
<keywords>Active Life, Spiritual Life, Contemplative Life, Jan van Ruusbroec, Mysticism, Parable of the Ten Virgins, Deiformity, Spiritual Marriage</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general prologue to the first book of the treatise, focusing on the "active life." It establishes the physiological and theological requirements for "seeing" God, using the metaphor of physical sight to explain spiritual enlightenment.</meta>

# ¶ BY JOHN RUUSBROEC, priest and canon of the observance of Blessed Augustine: the first book of the Spiritual Espousals. On the adornment of the moral and active life.

## ¶ Three things necessary for the vision of the active life. Chapter 1.

<image-desc>A decorative historiated initial 'C' featuring a stag standing in a wooded landscape, framed by a border of foliage.</image-desc>

Christ, therefore—the power and wisdom of God <note>original: "dei virtus et sapientia," a common title for Christ based on 1 Corinthians 1:24.</note>, the true light illuminating every person coming into this world—has, from the beginning of the human condition, inspired this adverb of pointing, "**behold**," <note>original: "ecce"</note> into human hearts for a rational vision of Himself, calling forth the creature capable of receiving Him. Therefore, a spiritual vision is necessary for us, lest we be punished with eternal blindness. For the existence of this vision, just as for bodily vision, three things are proved to be necessary. 

For the perfection of bodily vision, first, light is required, illuminating the medium of vision—that is, the air. Second, the will of the viewer is required, allowing the visible image to be impressed upon their eyes. Third, the purity of the eyes is required, so that the images of material things can be impressed upon them intentionally or spiritually <note>In medieval philosophy, "spiritually" here means the "image" or "form" of an object enters the eye without the physical matter of the object itself.</note>. For if any of these are removed, bodily vision is removed. 

In this way, for the supernatural vision through which a person is justified <note>The Latin "iustificatur ipsius" is likely a printer's error for "iustificatur ipse" (the person is justified) or "iustificatur anima" (the soul is justified).</note>, and from which the active life begins, three things are necessary: namely, the pouring in of the light of grace, the free turning of the will toward God—which is otherwise called the movement of free will toward God—and a pure cleansing of the conscience, which is otherwise called the movement of free will against sin. 

Now, the grace of God is twofold: namely, <term>grace freely given</term> <note>original: "gratia gratis data." This refers to a gift from God that does not necessarily make the receiver holy, but helps lead them or others toward salvation.</note> or prevenient grace, and <term>grace that makes one pleasing</term> <note>original: "gratia gratum faciens." Also known as sanctifying grace, which transforms the soul and makes it right with God.</note>. Prevenient grace is offered to everyone in common, even to Jews and Gentiles. Since the Spirit of God fills the whole world, the sound of prevenient grace has gone out into all the earth... <note>The text breaks off here at the end of the page; the full word is "prevenientis."</note>

<summary>Ruusbroec begins his first book by comparing spiritual vision to physical sight, identifying three requirements for spiritual awakening: divine grace, a willing heart, and a clean conscience.</summary>
<keywords>active life, spiritual vision, three requirements, prevenient grace, sanctifying grace, free will, justification, John Ruusbroec</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual vision from the previous page, moving from the philosophical requirements of sight to the universal call of Christ. It transitions into an agricultural metaphor describing how grace works upon the human soul like a graft upon a tree.</meta>

->ON THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-

...running, and into the ends of the earth his words and works [reach],
so that there is no one who may hide himself from his heat. Accordingly, the
veil of excuse has been taken away from all: from the moment the voice
of the evangelical thunder in the wheel <note>original: "euuangelici tonitrui i rota," a mystical reference to the preaching of the Gospel as a cosmic force, often associated with the "wheels" in the vision of the prophet Ezekiel.</note> was heard saying: come to
me all you who labor with the difficulty of doing good works, and are
burdened with the loads of sins. I will refresh you with the wine of
penance, healing the wounds of free will <term>free will: the human capacity to choose, which Ruusbroec views as "wounded" or weakened by sin and requiring the "medicine" of repentance</term>, and with the milk of the
beauty of grace, comforting the tender infancy of your faith:
until **CHRIST** be formed in you.
Come therefore, buy without silver and without any exchange
wine and milk: offering only the will of a believing obedience
to all. The church of **CHRIST** stands open: the
grace of the washing at the baptismal font <note>original: "batismi luter," referring to the stone basin or vessel used for the ritual of baptism.</note> stands waiting before the doors
for those who hope. The lamps of graces are not under a bushel basket: but
are set out for all upon the lampstand. The table of the
sacraments is set before all. Let each one ask in faith
for what he desires, hesitating not at all. For God is no
respecter of persons: he who wishes every person
to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth;
who makes his sun rise over the good and the bad: and
rains over the just and the unjust. It must be noted, however,
that although the sun commonly casts the rays of its light
over all trees, and the rain equally waters them
all: yet some bear no fruit, and some bear wild and
useless fruit for human use. For this reason,
wise farmers of old, pruning the branches of wild and
sterile trees, graft in shoots of good
fruit, so that in their own time they might bear useful fruit.
Indeed, the fertile and fruit-bearing shoot arisen from the heavenly
paradise is the light of sanctifying grace <term>sanctifying grace: the Latin "gratia gratum faciens" refers to the specific gift of God that makes the soul "pleasing" or "holy" and capable of spiritual life</term>,
without which any work born is tasteless and useless...

<summary>This page describes Christ's universal invitation to grace, comparing the Church's sacraments to a public feast and a lampstand. It introduces a central metaphor where the human soul is a tree that must be "grafted" with the shoot of sanctifying grace to produce good fruit.</summary>
<keywords>free will, baptism, sanctifying grace, grafting metaphor, universal salvation, repentance, John Ruusbroec</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual vision and grace, specifically moving into the agricultural metaphor of "grafting" grace into the soul. It follows the previous page's assertion that God offers salvation to all, leaving no one with an excuse for remaining in sin.</meta>

# I

<center>a iiij</center>

...is held as nothing at all. This little branch <note>original: "furculus," a small branch or shoot used in grafting. Ruusbroec uses this to describe how grace is joined to the "wild" soul.</note> of grace is indeed offered to all, but it is not grafted into everyone; for many are unwilling to prune away the wild and barren branches of their own tree—namely, their unbelief and their disobedience to the heavenly commands. But in order that this little branch may be grafted into our wild hearts, three things (which we touched upon earlier regarding vision) are required: specifically, the prevenience of grace <term>prevenient grace: the theological concept of grace that "comes before" human action, prompting the soul to turn toward God</term>, the free turning of the will toward God, and the cleansing of one's conscience.

Prevenient grace—because it is given by the Father of lights, from whom every best gift and every perfect gift (as Scripture testifies) descends, and who gives to all abundantly without reproaching—is ready for all (as we have said before). But not all equally apply their **free will** <term>free will: Latin "liberum arbitrium," the human capacity to choose between good and evil, which must cooperate with divine grace</term>, nor do they cleanse their conscience. Because of this, it happens that the little branch of *subsequent grace* is not grafted into their minds; it is from and through this subsequent grace that the fruit of eternal life would be brought forth.

Now, prevenient grace is exhibited in two ways: from the outside and from the inside.

From the outside, it rouses human sleepiness in many ways: specifically, through the scourging of physical illness, the loss of worldly goods and even friends, the occasion of scandals, the exhortation of preachers <note>original: "prędictorū," literally "the aforesaid," likely referring to the preachers or the "evangelical thunder" mentioned on the previous page.</note>, the consideration of the examples of the saints, conversation with devout persons, and admiration for the beauty of created things; so that by understanding the invisible things of God—that is, his **wisdom** and **goodness**—through the things that have been made, one may finally be moved to the love of their Creator.

From the inside, however, prevenient grace pricks human hearts through meditation on the Lord’s passion, the remembrance of God’s benefits, the loathing of one’s own sins, the consideration of the brevity of this present life, and [the fear] of hellish punishment...

<vocab>grace, graft, free will, wisdom, meditation, conscience, of sins, of the passion, of the creator</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the "grafting" of divine grace into the human soul, noting that while grace is offered to everyone, it requires a cooperative free will and a clean conscience to take root. Ruusbroec describes how God uses both external trials (like illness or nature) and internal reflections (like the passion of Christ) to wake the soul from its "sleepiness."</summary>

<keywords>prevenient grace, free will, grafting metaphor, spiritual awakening, cleansing of conscience, divine wisdom, suffering of Christ, John Ruusbroec</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of how grace interacts with the human soul. The previous page established that God offers grace to everyone like a "branch" to be grafted, but it requires human cooperation (free will and a clean conscience) to take root.</meta>

->### ON THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-

...and by the contemplation of heavenly glory, to notice how the goodness and patience of God, mercifully sparing those who sin, waits for their repentance. Also, from the higher spark of reason called **synteresis** <term>synteresis: the innate "spark" or conscience in the human soul that remains uncorrupted by sin and preserves a basic knowledge of moral law</term>—by which the human intellect is joined to the angelic intelligences in the order of creation—there is born in man a natural and indelible inclination toward the good and a detestation of evil. In these moments, according to the proportion and the needs of each, God sometimes so moves the hearts of his elect that, struck with awe and rebuked by their own consciences, they collect themselves and meditate on the purpose of a better life.

This work is carried out by **prevenient grace** <term>prevenient grace: from the Latin "praevenire" (to come before); the grace that moves a person toward God before they have even decided to seek Him</term> to prepare for the introduction of **subsequent** and **sanctifying grace** <term>sanctifying grace: also known as "gratia gratum faciens," this is the divine gift that heals human nature and makes the soul pleasing to God</term>. For when the soul—in this state of ceasing from wicked acts and desires, struck with the fear of God and trembling with a conscience aware of its own guilt—ponders what it ought to do, there is born within it a natural displeasure with sins and a natural benevolence toward God. At this point, the prevenient grace, having reached the height of its power, brings the soul to a standstill.

Thus, with a heart that is **contrite** <note>original: "attrito corde," referring to "attrition," a sorrow for sin born out of fear of punishment which prepares the way for true "contrition" or sorrow born of love</note> and doing everything within its power, yet unable to progress further by its own strength, the completion of the work is at last attributed to the divine goodness. And behold! The most brilliant light of **sanctifying grace**, like the splendor of a sunbeam, is poured into the soul thus humbled—unhoped for and earned by no preceding merits. For with this light, which no human merits can reach, God pours Himself in. This is the hidden operation of God in the soul, illuminating and stirring both the cognitive and the emotional powers of the soul. This is the completion of prevenient grace and the beginning of subsequent grace. And this is the **supernatural light** which we said before is required for supernatural vision. In accordance with this supernatural vision...

<summary>This page describes the transition from "prevenient grace" (which stirs the conscience) to "sanctifying grace" (which transforms the soul). It highlights "synteresis" as the natural human spark that allows us to recognize the good even before we are saved.</summary>

<keywords>synteresis, prevenient grace, sanctifying grace, subsequent grace, supernatural light, attrition, repentance, intellect, free will</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the process of justification. The previous page discussed prevenient grace and the natural inclination toward the good (synteresis), leading to a state where the soul is ready to receive sanctifying grace.</meta>

# I

...required: that is the movement of free will toward God. The second, I say, is not a matter of chronological time but of nature <note>The author distinguishes between things happening "in time" (one after another) and "in nature" (happening simultaneously, but with one being the logical cause of the other)</note>. For at the same moment grace is poured into the soul, the free will is suddenly and instantaneously moved toward God. And from that very union of the soul with God, **charity** <term>charity: from the Latin "charitas," referring to the divine love that connects the soul to God</term> is born. For where God and the soul are joined by the "glue of love," there God simultaneously pours the light of grace into the soul beyond the constraints of time. 

And the soul, by the power of that infused light, is suddenly—and in an instant—moved toward God; and from the meeting of both (namely, of God and the soul), charity is generated, which is the bond and the knot of love between them. From this charity is born the third thing which we said was required for supernatural vision: namely, the **cleansing of the conscience**, which is achieved through the movement of free will against sin. 

For these three concur in **justification** <term>justification: the process by which a person is moved from a state of sin to a state of grace or "rightness" with God</term> in such a way that neither the first nor the second—nor indeed the third—allows itself to be absent for even a single moment. For charity, generated from the two (as we said before), allows no stain of sin to remain in the conscience. By its existence alone, it "covers a multitude of sins" and transforms **unformed attrition** into **contrition** <term>attrition vs. contrition: attrition is sorrow for sin born of fear of punishment; contrition is "perfect" sorrow born of love for God. Charity is the "form" that turns the former into the latter</term> through itself—that is, through charity having been formed.

This, therefore, is the order of the justification of the sinful soul: first (as we said), in an order of nature rather than time, grace is infused; through its mediation, free will is moved toward God; from that union, charity is born; and from that, finally, perfect contrition is generated. 

Once contrition is born, it soon pours tears and sorrow into the bilge-water <term>sentinam: literally the "bilge" or "sink" of a ship; used here as a metaphor for the accumulated filth and dregs of sin within the human conscience</term> of the conscience; and with a hatred of sin and a firm purpose to do good, the soul hastens to vomit out the poison of its vices through a naked, simple, and sincere confession, and to wipe away the stains of its guilt through voluntary satisfaction. In this way, the justified sinner soon sings: "I am ready and I am not troubled" <note>original: "paratus sum et non sum turbatus," a quote from Psalm 118:60 (Vulgate)</note> so that...

<summary>This page describes the instantaneous and simultaneous process of justification, where grace is infused, free will moves toward God, and charity is born to cleanse the soul. It explains how divine love transforms a sinner's fear-based sorrow (attrition) into love-based repentance (contrition).</summary>

<keywords>justification, grace, free will, charity, contrition, attrition, confession, cleansing of conscience, divine union</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preparation of the soul for divine union. The previous page concluded the analysis of how charity and a cleansed conscience are necessary to achieve "supernatural vision," which allows the soul to see God's movement.</meta>

...keep your commandments, O Lord. Yet, from the remembrance of former filth and especially of the offense against God, the person becomes vile in their own eyes; and everything they do, when looked at through the lens of divine majesty, they consider worthless. Thus it happens that through the judgment of **humility**, daily destroying the **old man** <note>original: *veterem hominem*, a biblical concept referring to the sinful, unregenerate nature that must be cast off to live a spiritual life</note> along with his deeds, the person is renewed from day to day by good works, and the **eyes of the heart** grow young again. By this process, it finally happens that when the cry of **divine visitation** <term>visitation: a moment of divine presence or intervention in the soul</term> is made—even suddenly and unexpectedly—saying *behold, look*, there is no difficulty or delay in seeing. This is because the light of grace is not lacking (which carries and reveals the visible object), nor is the healthy eye of a **cleansed conscience** lacking (which receives the visible form). Furthermore, the rectitude of free will is present, reflecting the eye of the conscience back toward God. Let these remarks, therefore, suffice concerning the word "behold."

### ¶ ON THE THREEFOLD ADVENT OF CHRIST. Chapter II.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring intricate foliate and vine scrollwork.</image-desc>
With the eye of the heart finally cleansed, see what you may see: *the bridegroom*, he says, *comes*. It should be noted that among the **Grammarians** <note>The author refers to the rules of Latin grammar where the word *venit* can mean "he comes" (present tense) or "he came" (perfect tense). In manuscripts and early printing, these were often written identically.</note> this word *comes* <note>original: *venit*</note>, when taken by itself, belongs to two tenses: namely, the past and the present. And yet, in this passage, the Lord speaks in a parable about the **future**, which is made clearly evident by the exclusion of the **foolish virgins** <note>A reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25, where the "bridegroom" arrives in the future for the wedding feast.</note>. For in the present wedding feast <note>Likely referring to the Church on earth</note>, the wicked are admitted alongside the good; indeed, no one is excluded to such an extent that some are even compelled to enter by royal command. But there, in that future state, none shall enter with the bridegroom to the wedding except those who have been prepared. It must be noted, therefore, that a **threefold advent** <term>advent: from the Latin *adventus*, meaning "coming" or "arrival," specifically referring to the stages of Christ's appearance to humanity</term> of our bridegroom can be observed here. In the first advent: he was made man for the sake of men. In the second: according to the proportion of hearts...

## ¶ ON THE THREE THINGS to be considered in the advents and acts of CHRIST. Chapter III.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'V' decorated with vine and leaf scrollwork.</image-desc>
Truly, in these three advents of CHRIST and in all his works, three things occur to us for consideration: namely, concerning his indwelling dispositions and his visible actions. For the cause of the creation of intellectual and rational creatures—namely, the angel and the human—is the immense **goodness of God** <note>original: *bonitas dei*</note>: so that he might communicate and show the riches of his goodness to a creature made capable of receiving it. This was done so that the creature might love the goodness it saw, be united to the goodness it loved, and, once united, enjoy it perpetually. And the angel, indeed, by a single movement of **free will** <term>free will: *liberum arbitrium*, the capacity of a rational being to choose its own path or orientation toward God</term>, attained the goal of its creation; but man, in this present life, rises step by step through many movements toward an **enigmatic vision** <note>original: *aenigmaticam visionem*, a reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12; in this life we see God "darkly" as through a blurred mirror, rather than clearly</note> and a slight foretaste, aided by **grace** <term>grace: *gratia*, the supernatural assistance given by God to humans for their salvation</term>. But in the future, having been made equal to the angels, he will be inebriated by the abundance of the house of God, and will be given to drink from the torrent of divine pleasure, and will see God face to face just as he is. Furthermore, the cause of the Lord's **incarnation** <term>incarnation: the theological mystery of God becoming human in the person of Jesus</term> was the inestimable love of **charity** <note>original: *charitas*, specifically the selfless, divine love God has for creation</note>, by which God so loved the world that he gave his only begotten son; and also the miserable condition of necessity, by which the human race, beset by countless calamities due to the **guilt of original sin** <note>original: *reatus originalis culpae*, the inherited state of separation from God resulting from the Fall of Man</note>, was unable to make satisfaction by itself. Indeed, the causes of the works of CHRIST which he performed in this world, according to both his divinity and his humanity, are fourfold:

### ¶ Concerning the Twofold Humility of CHRIST. Chapter IV.

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'G' featuring a seated figure, likely a saint or scholar, surrounded by intricate floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>A twofold humility is found in CHRIST according to his twofold nature: namely, the most worthy humbling of his divinity and the most perfect humility of his humanity. For the divine majesty of CHRIST demonstrates his humility to us by a double sign. First, by the condescension <note>original: *dignatione*</note> of assuming our nature—which was subject to the curse of eternal damnation—by taking the form of a servant in personal unity: so that both the good and the evil may truly be able to call CHRIST their brother, and he, in turn, is not at all ashamed to call them brothers. Second, by the choice of his mother; because he chose as his mother not a king's daughter, but a poor, simple woman <note>original: *pauperem mulierculam*</note>—a virgin, yet most pure: so that through the humility of divine condescension, a fragile woman might be called the parent of the creator <term>parent of the creator: *creatoris genitrix*, a reference to the title 'Theotokos' or 'Mother of God'</term> of heaven and earth and of all things that are in them. And to such an extent do God and man communicate their properties <term>properties: *idiomata*, referring to the 'Communication of Idioms,' a theological concept where the attributes of both the divine and human natures are assigned to the single person of Jesus</term> to each other in CHRIST, that what was done through the man is truly said to have been done by God, and again, the works of the divinity are truthfully predicated of the man. 

Moreover, the most perfect humility of the humanity of CHRIST is made known by evident signs. For since Scripture testifies that the Word was made flesh, full of grace and truth, there is no doubt that in the sight of the Father's majesty, the humble soul of CHRIST was bowed and submissive with all veneration and reverence, which is the truest proof of humility. Because of this, whatever he did, he did for the praise and glory of the Father, nowhere seeking his own glory. For humility subjected him to the legal ceremonies and commands: namely, to circumcision, to the offering in the temple, to the price of redemption <note>referring to the Jewish custom of "redeeming" a firstborn son</note>, and to the paying of the tribute tax. In humility he descended with his parents from Jerusalem to Nazareth and was subject to them, no doubt attending to their service and following- <note>The text ends mid-word on the Latin *obsecundans* or *obsequendo*, meaning 'following' or 'obeying'.</note>

<summary>This page defines Christ's humility as being "twofold," existing in both His divine nature (through the Incarnation and the choice of a humble mother) and His human nature (through His obedience to religious laws and His parents).</summary>

In humility, for the mystery of his apostleship <note>original: *apostolatus sui mysterium*</note>, he chose the poor and the ignoble: among whom he was not as one to whom service is rendered, but as one who serves. Washing their feet, girded with a linen towel, he most kindly came to the aid of all necessities, both of the soul and the body, and displayed himself to all as if he were the servant of the whole world. He even willed to be baptized among the "generation of vipers" <note>A reference to Matthew 3:7, where John the Baptist addresses the Pharisees and Sadducees; Christ’s humility is shown by submitting to baptism alongside sinners</note>. These, therefore, are for us the truest arguments and examples of the humility of **CHRIST**.

### ¶ Concerning the Charity of CHRIST. Chapter V.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring scrolling acanthus leaf motifs.</image-desc>
**S**econd among the virtues of **CHRIST** to be treated, we have taken up **Charity** <term>Charity: *Charitas*, meaning divine, sacrificial love rather than mere almsgiving</term>, the mother of virtues. The Charity of **CHRIST** had raised the intellectual powers of his soul into the suspension of that same comprehension and the stillness <note>original: *silatio*, a variant of *silentio*</note> of that same fruition <note>The state of enjoying God's presence</note> in which he now exults, fully glorified in heaven. Charity caused his heart to continually ascend like a pillar of smoke <note>original: *virgulam fumi*; a common mystical reference to Song of Solomon 3:6</note> from the spices of paternal praise, love, and veneration. It persuaded him to pray for human necessities, and to do whatever he did for the honor of the Father. Finally, Charity made him condescend to human infirmities of both body and soul: healing bodies through miracles, and souls through his words, character, and examples. "Learn from me," he said, "for I am meek and humble of heart" <note>Matthew 11:29</note>; and "I have given you an example, so that as I have done, you also should do" <note>John 13:15</note>. He fed the multitude, exhausted after three days, with miraculous loaves in the desert. He made the deaf hear and the mute speak. He enlightened the blind. He drove out demons. He raised the lame. He cleansed the lepers. He raised the dead. But these works of divine condescension are to be believed as having been performed not so much **historically** upon bodies as **spiritually** within the minds of men. What more? The excellence of the Charity of **CHRIST** is unsearchable to us. Because...

### Concerning the patience of CHRIST. Chapter VI.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop cap for the letter 'S', featuring intricate vine and leaf scrollwork.</image-desc>
**B**ut now let us treat a little more extensively the third virtue of **CHRIST**, namely **patience**. For she <note>The author personifies Patience as a lady or queen</note> made our bridegroom distinguished with wonderful ornaments, from the very moment of his birth until the death of the cross, and from the sole of his foot even to the top of his head. For having been born in an inn, he was soon wrapped in cheap rags <note>original: *pannis vilibus*</note> and laid in a manger. On the eighth day, with the shedding of blood, he was circumcised. By the fear of a threatening sword, he was driven into Egyptian exile. He was bent in obedience to his parents. He endured hunger, thirst, and the labor of vigils. And after a forty-day fast, he was relentlessly beset <note>original: *improbe perurgetur*</note> by diabolical temptations. Traveling through all Judea from place to place, preaching the gospel of grace, and healing the sick: he groaned, grew weary from labors, and was troubled by the shouts and the persistent demands <note>original: *importunitatibus*</note> of the crowds flowing around him. He was afflicted by the injuries of the Jews, distressed by rebukes, torn apart by slanders, mocked by derision, attacked by traps, and provoked by poisonous hatreds. At the end, he was sold, betrayed, captured, and accused by false witnesses. He was beaten, spat upon, mocked, struck with fists <note>original: *colaphizatur*</note>, and presented to the judge as one guilty of death and deserving of punishment. He was condemned, bound, scourged, and crowned with thorns; and with a murderer <note>Barabbas</note> released, he was called to be crucified by the voices of all. Carrying the cross for himself, he was led to death; he was nailed by hands and feet. He was given gall-mixed vinegar to drink. He was saturated with insults. His sense of taste was fed with the bitterness of gall; his sense of smell with the stench of spittle <note>original: *screarum*</note>; his sense of hearing with the reproach of those insulting him; his sense of sight with the mocking distortion of ghost-like <note>original: *larualium*, meaning spectral or demonic-looking</note> faces, and with the groans and affliction of his mother's grief. So...

[...convic]ted, he is tortured by the pain of many wounds, by the stretching that loosens the joints of his body, and by the piercing harshness of the cold. Above all things, however, the blind stubbornness of the Jews <note>original: *iudæorum obduratio*</note> tortured his holy soul, which was filled with the highest love. He suffered the frustration of his bitter passion regarding many things past, present, and future, and especially the lack of gratitude—or the very minimal gratitude—of the many who were to be washed in his own blood. He also endured the blasphemy of his sworn enemies who reproached him with the shames of his own passion. Behold, our bridegroom, thus affected, seemed to the wicked Jews to have neither beauty nor comeliness <note>A reference to the "Suffering Servant" in Isaiah 53:2</note>.

But in truth, he showed to us the signs of **patience**, offering himself to us as a most excellent mirror of suffering. Beyond all these kinds of passion, his divinity withdrew the influx of consolation and grace from his human sensitivity; and in the danger of that so powerful a storm, he was steered by the rudder of **patience** alone. As if wondering at this, he himself cried out: "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" <note>original: *Deus meus, deus meus quare dereliquisti me*; Christ's fourth cry from the cross</note>. Truly, unmindful of his own pain, he prayed for his crucifiers, saying: "Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do" <note>original: *Pater ignosce illis quia nesciunt quid faciunt*</note>; and he was heard because of his reverence. For perhaps those who were ignorant of what they were doing afterwards obtained mercy.

Behold our Jesus, crowned with a triple diadem: namely, of **humility**, **charity**, and **patience**. With these, he did not crown us first, but crowned himself from the beginning. These virtues he did not cast aside even unto death; rather, he clothed himself with radiant gems that illuminate the whole world, and having been crowned with them, he ascended the cross. And opening his side, he not only paid our debts, but also, from the treasure of his heart, he bestowed the gifts <term>donativa: originally a gift given by Roman emperors to their soldiers; here used for divine grace</term> of the sacraments and of graces upon those wishing to serve as soldiers in his name.

Indeed, after the resurrection, crowned most fully with glory and honor, he ascended into heaven by his own power; he sits at the right hand of the Father; he lives and reigns forever and ever.

### ¶ ON THE SECOND COMING OF CHRIST: by which he
### flows into souls through grace. Chapter VII.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring scrolling acanthus leaves within a square border.</image-desc> The second coming of CHRIST <note>original: *Secundus CHRISTI adventus*</note> is carried out invisibly, while daily, through the grace of a new visitation, he flows into the minds of his chosen ones. Intending to say a few things about this advent, let us set forth its causes and its manner. Therefore, a fourfold cause gives birth to the daily infusion of new grace and the flowing in of divine visitation into the hearts of the faithful: namely, divine mercy, our own need, the overflowing of divine bounty, and the pious desire of our poverty. Moreover, let us investigate by a visible example how the seedlings of these four virtues grow and how the gifts of grace are heaped up. For when the sun passes through its highest solstice point <note>The "solstitial vertex" refers to the sun's highest point in the sky, providing maximum light and heat.</note> and pours the rays of its heat into a deep valley enclosed on both sides by high mountains, that valley receives three benefits that are not granted so abundantly to flatter plains: indeed, it grows bright from the reflection of the mountains, it grows warm from the influence of the solar rays, and from this, thirdly, it becomes rich with fertile fruitfulness. That deep valley is the humble heart. For the just man, residing in the depth of his own smallness <note>original: *puvitatis*, a contraction of *parvitatis*, meaning insignificance or lowliness</note>, already noticing that he neither is nor can do anything by himself, nor finally has anything which he did not receive—to such an extent that even in the grace already received he notices he cannot make progress by himself—and observing that he offends in many things, fails in many acts of virtue, and neglectfully passes over many others: then he digs for himself the deep valley of humility. Therefore, placed in the valley of humility and need, truly acknowledging his own poverty, he cries out for the mercy of divine goodness, laying open his need and [appealing to] divine generosity...

<summary>This page transitions from Christ's historical life to His spiritual "Second Coming" within the soul. The author uses the metaphor of a sun-warmed valley to explain how humility allows a person to receive divine grace, light, and spiritual fruitfulness.</summary>
<keywords>Second Coming, grace, humility, spiritual growth, solar metaphor, divine mercy, poverty of spirit, visitation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the metaphor established on the previous page, where the soul is compared to a valley situated between mountains, awaiting the "sunlight" of divine grace.</meta>

->[inter]appeals. And because in this case the soul contemplates the excellence of divine majesty and the deficiency of its own purity: it is made into a humble and deep valley.<-

**CHRIST**, therefore, the sun of justice and mercy, residing at the solstitial vertex <note>The highest point of the sun's path; here a metaphor for the highest divinity.</note> (that is, in the equality of the Father and the Holy Spirit) at the Father’s right hand, and always condescending to the needs of the humble who cry out to him: pours the rays of his grace into that valley of the humbled heart. Immediately those two mountains, higher than all the heavens, swell up: namely, the twin desires. One is the desire of always praising the Lord and serving him peacefully with all reverence. The other is the desire of possessing virtues more perfectly. These mountains, I say, are higher than heaven: because they touch the mercy of divine goodness immediately with their peaks and knock upon the munificence of divine bounty with great cries. Thus it happens that, once the soul has been made capable of more ample grace in this way, the generous giver—who gives to all abundantly—enriches the little valley of its humility (enclosed by these high mountains) with a triple gift, just like the sun. 

For indeed, the humble and humbled soul is more brightly illuminated by the infusion of new grace; it is more fervently inflamed by the love of charity. From which, finally, it is more richly endowed with the perfection of virtues and the wealth of good works. This, therefore, is the cause, this the manner, and this the <term>dedication festival</term><note>original: *encenia*; historically refers to the feast of the dedication of a church, here used for the soul's renewal.</note> of the internal visitation of our spouse. 

Besides this, there is another manner of daily infusion of new grace: namely, as often as anyone humbly, placing no obstacle <note>original: *obicem*; a spiritual barrier or block.</note> to grace, receives any <term>sacrament</term><note>A religious rite regarded as a visible sign of inward divine grace.</note> of the church. Moreover, the obstacles to sacramental graces are: as in <term>baptism</term><note>The rite of initiation into the Christian faith.</note>, a pretense of faith; in <term>penance</term><note>The sacrament involving confession and repentance.</note>, a lack of contrition; in the receiving of the <term>eucharist</term><note>The sacrament of Holy Communion.</note>, a purpose of sinning mortally and a sinister intention; and in the others, something similar. This is, therefore, the fruitful and daily present advent of **CHRIST** our spouse, which we must greatly attend to and long for with frequent desires.

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the soul as a humble valley, explaining how Christ's grace "warms" the soul into virtuous fruitfulness. It also defines the "daily advent" of Christ through the sacraments, provided the believer does not block grace through insincerity or lack of repentance.</summary>

<keywords>humility, Sun of Justice, divine grace, sacraments, baptism, penance, eucharist, contrition, spiritual growth</keywords>

...so that we, who are unable to stand or move forward by our own power, may be strengthened by the grace of his frequent visitation.

### ¶ Concerning the coming of CHRIST for judgment. Chapter VIII.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial 'T' decorated with stylized foliage and scrolls.</image-desc>
**T**he third and still future coming of **CHRIST** will be fulfilled in the final judgment; and in the deaths <note>original: *obitibus*</note> of individuals, it is in some way anticipated. Concerning this coming, five things are to be considered: namely, the cause, the manner, the witnesses, the accuser, and the judge. The cause of this coming is threefold: namely, the fitness <note>original: *congratuitas*; referring here to the appropriate or graceful timing of the event.</note> of the time, the necessity of the case, and the equity of the judge. The fitness of the time refers specifically to the day of each person's death and the day of the general judgment. For to every soul, at its creation and its entry into the body <note>original: *incorporatione*</note>, a certain boundary has been established by God, known to him alone, which the soul can in no way pass over; so that, leaving the body, it may present itself before the divine sight. The necessity of the case is this: that the soul must give an account to the Truth that sees all things, for every word it has spoken and every deed it has performed. Furthermore, the equity of the judge brings about this coming for this reason: because it is just that an irrevocable sentence be delivered by immutable equity and infallible truth. Now, **CHRIST** is inflexible justice and infallible truth, to whom every hidden thing is open; for he is the Son of God, to whom the Father has also given all judgment. Therefore **CHRIST** judges, because he is wisdom knowing all things, power <note>original: *virtus*; in this context, it refers to divine strength or miraculous power.</note> capable of all things, and justice rendering a correct verdict. For these three causes, a specific judgment <note>Often called "Particular Judgment" in theology, occurring immediately after death.</note> is conducted at the deaths of individuals, and a general judgment is expected on the last day. The manner of this coming is to render to each person, according to their own merits and following the rule of equity, either rewards or punishment. For he will render to the just the reward of their labors, for each of their works directed toward God with a right in—

<sig>b ij</sig>

<vocab>The Coming of Christ, the final judgment, death, the soul, justice, wisdom, power, merit</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the "Third Coming" of Christ as the Judge, explaining that judgment occurs both individually at the moment of death and universally at the end of time. It outlines the theological reasons why Christ is the perfectly equitable judge of human words and deeds.</summary>
<keywords>Last Judgment, Particular Judgment, Christ the Judge, Divine Justice, soul and body, account of deeds, equity, afterlife</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the "equity of the judge" and the rewards or punishments of the Final Judgment, before transitioning into Chapter IX, which categorizes the five groups of people present at the end of time.</meta>

DE OR. NVP. SP. <note>original: *De Ornatu Nuptiarum Spiritualium*; referring to "The Adornment of Spiritual Marriage" by Jan van Ruusbroec.</note>

intentions being directed to Him: giving himself as an infinite good for a reward. Nor do we say that any human effort, however great, can ever worthily deserve such a great good; rather, it is through God cooperating with human merits that—once works are performed through the power of the Godhead—the divinity itself bestows justice as a reward of fitness <note>original: *congruitatis*; a theological term referring to a reward given out of God's kindness rather than a strict legal debt.</note>. Those, however, whom he sentences by just judgment to the eternal fire, he does so because they chose the transitory and mutable after despising the eternal and immutable good; and turning away from divine honor by their own will, they clung finally to created things. The witnesses of this examination are the angels and each person's own conscience; the accuser is the devil; the infallible judge is **JESUS CHRIST**, to whom be all power, praise, and honor forever and ever.

## ¶ Concerning the five kinds of people who must stand before the judgment of CHRIST. Chapter IX.

<image-desc>A square decorative woodcut initial 'Q' with intricate floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>
**F**ive kinds of people will stand before the judgment of **CHRIST**; for (as the apostle says) we shall all stand before the judgment seat of **CHRIST**. The first and worst kind is the crowd of false Christians dying in mortal sins without contrition and repentance <note>original: *poenitentia*</note>, and rotting like beasts in their own dung. For these, because they either despised the sacraments of the Church or used them in vain and improperly despite the death of **CHRIST**, and applied no effort to works of mercy or the Lord's commandments, shall be condemned by God's just judgment to the depths of hell <note>original: *tartari*; referring to Tartarus, a classical term for the deepest abyss of punishment.</note>. The second kind is the whole army of unbelievers <note>original: *infidelium*</note>. Although these will appear at the judgment, they have already been judged because they did not believe. For whoever does not believe is already judged; and whoever does not love **CHRIST** remains in death. However, they will be tortured less in the underworld than the damned Christians; for to whom m[ore is given, more is required].

<summary>This page concludes the description of the Final Judgment's justice, where God rewards the faithful by "fitness" rather than debt. It then introduces a classification of five types of people to be judged, detailing the grim fate of "false Christians" and unbelievers.</summary>
<keywords>Final Judgment, Merit of Congruity, Jesus Christ as Judge, False Christians, Unbelievers, Contrition, Punishment</keywords>

->DISCOURSE IV.<-
b iii

...committed less, less is demanded of him; and the servant who did not know his lord’s will shall be beaten with fewer lashes <note>original: *plagis paucioribus vapulabit*; a reference to the Gospel of Luke 12:48 regarding degrees of punishment based on one's level of knowledge and responsibility.</note>. The third kind is the crowd of penitents who, departing this life before their prescribed penance is completed, are freed through the <margin>purgatorial fire</margin> purgatorial fire. The fourth kind is the number of the good or the blessed who faithfully observe the Lord’s commandments, or who satisfy their lapses through prescribed penance <note>original: *pacta poenitentia*</note>, persisting in works of charity and mercy. Being fully cleansed in this life, they fly upward at once without the need for purgatory. The fifth kind is the society of the perfect. These exceed mere "bodily training"—which is of little use—through the piety of their minds <note>A reference to 1 Timothy 4:8, contrasting physical discipline with spiritual godliness.</note>. They dwell in the heavens while still remaining on earth; in them, through a reciprocal charity, God makes a lovely and mutually indwelling home, and they in God. Indeed, they differ from the heavenly host only by the intervening shadow of time and mortality. For as soon as the veil of the body is laid aside, they enjoy full blessedness in the blink of an eye. These will not come into judgment to be judged themselves, but to sit beside **CHRIST** as He sits on His throne of majesty, judging the twelve tribes of Israel. Then, death—the last of all things—will be destroyed, and every temporal punishment, even that of purgatory, shall have an eternal end. Those blessed by the Father shall then seek the heights with their King, rejoicing forever in the clearest vision, the firmest understanding, and the sweetest enjoyment of the divine essence. This, therefore, is the third advent <term>advent<gloss>from the Latin 'adventus', meaning 'coming' or 'arrival'</gloss></term> of **CHRIST**, which we experience either individually at death or all together at the final judgment.

...reflecting the eyes of the mind <note>The author uses the "eyes of the mind" to describe spiritual insight or contemplation.</note>, so that we may be His true ministers, let us follow His footsteps; let us emulate from the heart perfect habits of virtue outwardly, but inwardly, let us strive for humility, charity, and true patience. ## ¶ CONCERNING THE GOING OUT by which one goes out to meet the bridegroom. Chapter XI.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'M' decorated with scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Once the eyes of the heart have been cleansed through charity and the clearing of the conscience, and once the visible thing—that is, the triple advent <note>The "triple advent" refers to Christ's coming in the flesh (Incarnation), His coming into the soul through grace, and His coming at the Final Judgment.</note> of the bridegroom—has been shown, He suggests what we ought to do, saying: "Go out." For through grace and charity, when our hearts are illuminated and we attend to the visible and glorious advent of **CHRIST**, the highest God, a new light of justice arises—that is, the desire to imitate **CHRIST** in diligent habits. Therefore, **CHRIST**, crying out, says: "Go out." This "going out" is completed by three paths: for we are commanded to go out toward God, toward ourselves, and toward our neighbors. Lest we wander in these departures through devious turnings: let us follow charity and justice as our renowned leaders. For charity, like a riverbed derived from the sea of eternal love, always strives to return to the place from which it went out, though it is never separated from its origin by the continuity of its essence. Justice, moreover, is a stream generated from the very wide riverbed of charity. It gathers all the precious pearls of virtues and habits, with which it is fitting for the queen of God (that is, <gloss>the rational soul</gloss> the rational soul) to be adorned. Both, however—namely, charity and justice—give birth to a third virtue...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "going out" to meet the bridegroom. ...that is, humility: which is the most solid foundation of the moral kingdom of the soul. These three virtues <note>The author is referring to Charity, Justice, and Humility.</note> sustain the entire structure of all the other virtues. For Charity always places man before the sight of God's immense goodness—from which charity itself originated—so that, aided by His grace, he may live more honorably for God, stand with a firmer step, and grow more extensively in the virtues. Justice, moreover, makes man present to the eternal truth; so that he, being open to the truth, may receive an increase of light from it, and, aided by the face of God, may perfect all the virtues without error. Humility, indeed, represents man to the gaze of the divine majesty: so that, always considering his own smallness <note>original: "paruitati"</note>, he may cling with all confidence to the divine power and never swell up with any self-esteem.

### ¶ CONCERNING HUMILITY, which is the beginning of the genealogy of the virtues written below. Chapter XII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'E' within a square frame, embellished with floral motifs and vine-like scrollwork.</image-desc>

And because we have laid humility as the foundation for the virtues, desiring to weave a catalog, or rather a genealogy of the virtues <note>The "genealogy of virtues" is a traditional way of describing how one spiritual quality gives birth to another.</note>, let us start first with humility. Humility is a profound bowing of the heart in the presence of the divine majesty. For when Justice commands this, the heart of the lover is soon filled with humble and devout inclinations by the instinct of charity. For the humble lover of **CHRIST**, observing that one so powerful, so exalted, and so rich in Himself, served such a vile, such a small, and such a poor little man <note>original: "homunculo"; a diminutive used to emphasize human insignificance compared to God.</note> so faithfully and in such great poverty, there is born in his heart such reverence and such veneration for the divine majesty that he is unable to demonstrate it either with words or signs. For he exhibits honor and reverence to the divine greatness...

# ¶ CONCERNING THE GOING OUT by which one goes out to meet the bridegroom. Chapter II.

### ¶ CONCERNING HUMILITY, which is the beginning of the <term>genealogy of the virtues</term> written below. Chapter XII.
