<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XII on the virtue of Humility. The previous page ended mid-word, defining humility as an exhibition of the soul toward God and others.</meta>

<header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...biting <note>Completing the word "exhibiting" from the previous page; original Latin: *exhibere*.</note> oneself outwardly indeed with humble services, and inwardly with reverent bows. This is the first act, and the most fitting for humility, the sweetest for charity, and the most rightful for equity <note>original: "aequitati," referring to the justice or fairness of giving God what is His due.</note>. Indeed, the humble heart of a true lover cannot pay sufficient honors to God and His most noble humanity according to its own desire, nor can it sufficiently disparage itself. From this it happens that in the showing of divine honor and in humble services, it always complains that it falls short and is negligent. Nor does it show humble and worthy reverence only to God, but also to Holy Mother Church, her sacred mysteries, and her ministers. It is thereafter frugal in food, drink, and clothing; moderate in words, responses, and gestures; prompt in humble services and deeds; and in all its conduct, it is neither feigned nor adorned. In both mental and physical exercise, and in the sight of both God and men, it is graced with such humility that it gives no one cause for offense. Thus, when humility is established as the foundation of all virtues, pride—the beginning and root of all evils—is destroyed. What more is there to say? With what praises shall I exalt you, sweet humility, dear to God, pleasing to angels, and lovable to men? Indeed, through you, the manifold snare of the devil, the world, and sin is broken. Through you, your possessor is made fit and well-ordered within himself, and is established in the proper realm of the virtues. Through you, the gate of heaven is opened to your followers; and for your sake, the Most High inclines the ears of His most excellent majesty to their prayers. Through you, those made into deep valleys <note>A common mystical metaphor: as water flows to the lowest point, God’s grace fills the "lowly" or humble soul rather than the "mountains" of the proud.</note> are filled with the flowing streams of grace; and so that no threatening storm may shake them, they are rooted and founded in **CHRIST**, the solid rock. In Him, every building constructed in humility—even if the winds of temptation blow and the rivers of vice flow—shall never be moved. For it is founded upon the firmest foundation.

# ¶ ON OBEDIENCE. Chapter XIII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial M featuring a central face surrounded by foliate patterns.</image-desc>

**M**oreover, humility, acting as a mother, gives birth to her first daughter, obedience. For no one is truly and heartily obedient except the humble person; nor is true humility ever proven except through obedience. Obedience is, furthermore, a flexibility of a submitted will and an untiring readiness of the mind <note>original: "animus," referring here to the seat of intention and courage.</note> toward every good thing. Obedience indeed inserts all things into their proper order; for it subjects the flesh to the spirit, and the spirit to its Creator. Thus, with God governing the spirit, and the spirit governing the senses, a **living, holy sacrifice, pleasing to God**, and a **rational service** <note>A reference to Romans 12:1, where the believer is urged to present their body as a "reasonable service" or "spiritual worship."</note> may be offered. Nor does obedience merely subject a person to the mandates of God and the divine will; it also submits them to the ecclesiastical <term>sacraments</term> <note>the sacred rites of the Church</note>, precepts, commands, counsels, dogmas, scriptures, and all established customs. It makes a person subject to **every human creature** for God’s sake. It makes one prompt and cheerful in giving counsel, aid, and service—both physical and spiritual—as one recognizes what is appropriate for each person, while always keeping the rule of discretion <note>The "rule of discretion" is a classic monastic concept meaning the ability to discern the right measure or middle path in all things.</note>. Through obedience, that venomous viper—disobedience, the daughter of pride—is killed. And humility, like a mother through her offspring, is made known as the "fringes" of her good deeds are widened <note>original: "dilatatis bonorum actuum fimbrijs"; an allusion to the tassels or fringes on garments mentioned in the Gospels, here symbolizing the visible extension of virtue through action.</note>. Obedience ensures that reverence and authority are shown to superiors, and that condescension <note>In this context, "condescension" refers to the graciousness of a superior stooping down to help an inferior, rather than the modern negative sense.</note> and kindness are shown to subjects; it binds everyone together in continual peace. It makes the one who loves it worthy of imitation to those below, sociable to equals, lovable to superiors, acceptable to God, and capable of being exalted by Him and enriched by the eternal gifts of grace.

# ¶ ON THE ABANDONMENT OF ONE'S OWN WILL. Chapter XIIII.

<summary>This page defines obedience as the primary "daughter" and proof of humility, explaining how it creates order by subjecting the flesh to the spirit and the spirit to God. It emphasizes that obedience applies not just to divine law, but to Church authority and social duty.</summary>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XIII on the virtue of obedience. The previous page established obedience as the daughter of humility, describing it as the submission of the spirit to its Creator and the flesh to the spirit. This page explores how obedience leads to the total renunciation of the self-will and introduces the next virtue, patience.</meta>

<header>ON THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'O' depicting a pelican in her piety (a bird feeding her young from her own breast) set within a circular medallion and framed by ornate scrollwork and foliage.</image-desc>

**O**bedience, moreover, gives birth to the abdication <note>original: "abdicationem," meaning a formal renunciation or giving up</note> of one's own will. For it is, in a way, palpable to the touch: no one can truly pour their own will into the will of another unless they have been weaned from the breasts of mother obedience. Without this, even if one's own will remains in control, a person might still try to conform themselves to the wills of others in external actions. However, the abdication of one's own will prevents its possessor from adopting any special way of living that is unlike the lives and teachings of the saints. Instead, in all of their conduct <note>original: "conversatione," referring to one's entire manner of living and social interaction</note>, it urges them to look to the honor of God, to obey the Lord’s and the Church’s precepts, and—while preserving the rule of discretion—to harmonize with the customs of those with whom they live. Through this renunciation of self-will in both acting and suffering, all the material and every occasion for pride perishes and is nullified. Humility is then perfected in the highest degree; God's will exercises full dominion, and the human will is so united to the divine will that it cannot want or desire anything to the contrary. One who abdicates their own will in this manner "strips off the old man with his deeds" and is "clothed with the new man who is created according to God," <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul’s letters (Ephesians 4 and Colossians 3), describing the transformation of the self through faith.</note> renewed in the justice and holiness of the divine will. Of such people, Truth speaks in the Gospel: **Blessed are the poor in spirit** <note>original: "Beati pauperes spiritu"</note>—that is, those who have renounced their own will—**for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.**

### ¶ ON PATIENCE. Chapter XV

<image-desc>A decorative square woodcut initial 'A' featuring symmetrical foliate patterns and intertwining stems within a border.</image-desc>

**A**bdication of one's own will gives birth to patience. For no one is able to be perfectly patient in all things unless they have so subjected their own will to the divine will that they are also subject to **every human creature** for God’s sake, in all things that are lawful, honest, and useful. Patience, however, is the peaceful endurance of all things that happen, whether by God’s permission or by the infliction of other creatures.

<meta>catchword: pa (patientia)</meta>

<summary>This page describes how obedience leads to the "abdication of the will," a state where the individual's desires are perfectly aligned with God's. It concludes by introducing patience as the natural result of this self-surrender.</summary>
<keywords>self-will, renunciation, obedience, humility, new man, patience, spiritual transformation, Jan van Ruysbroeck</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the virtues of the soul. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how the abdication of self-will allows the individual to "strip off" worldly attachments and put on the virtue of patience. This page explores Patience (Chapter XV, concluding), Meekness (Chapter XVI), and begins the section on Kindness (Chapter XVII).</meta>

...tience, which is such that it is hardly saddened by whatever might happen to it: whether the loss of worldly goods or friends, the onset of illness or scandals, or even life, death, purgatory, the devil, or hell. This is because a person of true love <note>original: "charitatis," referring to the theological virtue of charity or selfless divine love</note> submits himself above all things to the divine will. Since his own conscience does not accuse him of mortal sin, he finds it good to endure whatever God has commanded, whether it be a temporary trial or an eternal one. Through patience, a person is wonderfully adorned; he is strongly armed against the spirit of anger—which often shakes the soul and leaves it open to various temptations.

## ON MEEKNESS. Cap. XVI.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'P' featuring intricate floral and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
Patience, moreover, gives birth to meekness <note>original: "mititatem," meaning gentleness or mildness of spirit</note>. For no one is truly meek in the face of adversity unless patience supports them. Meekness promises its possessor peace in all things, for "the meek shall inherit the earth and shall delight in the abundance of peace" <note>A reference to Psalm 37:11 and the Beatitudes</note>. The meek person lightly endures harsh words, frightening physical hardships, the furious works of anger, and easily disregards <note>original: "floxifacit," literally "to value at a straw" or treat as worthless</note> every injury while preserving peace of heart. Indeed, meekness is nothing other than tranquility found in true patience. Through meekness, the "irascible power" <note>The "iraſcibil̃," a term in medieval psychology referring to the part of the soul that responds to threats or obstacles with anger or courage</note> is immobilized with a kind of silence; the "concupiscible power" <note>The "cōcupiſcibilis," the part of the soul associated with desires and appetites</note> is elevated by virtues; the rational part, noticing this, rejoices; and the conscience is wonderfully calmed by the taste of such sweetness. Finally, through meekness, the tyranny of anger is utterly destroyed, as the Spirit of God rests upon the humble and the quiet. Of such people, the Truth <note>A common medieval title for Jesus Christ, who called himself "the Way, the Truth, and the Life"</note> says: *Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth*, which means the quiet possession of their own nature and the tranquil enjoyment of worldly things.

## ON KINDNESS. Cap. XVII.

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'M' adorned with leafy flourishes and botanical details.</image-desc>
Meekness, finally, gives birth to kindness <note>original: "benignitatem," implying a generous and helpful disposition toward others</note>. For it is clearly known to all that only the meek are truly kind. Kindness [soothes] the minds of the angry with a mild, human—

...[facili]ty <note>The word fragment "tate" completes "facilitate" from the previous page, meaning ease or gentleness.</note>, with sweet affability and pious acts, it strives to soften <note>original: "emolire," to mollify or make tender</note> where hope promises an effect. Through kindness <note>original: "benignitate"</note>, the fruitfulness of love <note>original: "charitatis," referring to the theological virtue of selfless divine love</note> grows strong. For the human heart filled with kindness is like a lamp full of oil: it shines with good examples for those who wander; it anoints the desolate and the sad with soothing words; it heals with pious speech and service those wounded by the dart of anger; and it sets minds rich in virtue ablaze with the fire of love. It does not waste away like brittle glass through hatred or the envy of others, but hardens with the metallic firmness of brotherly affection.

## ¶ ON COMPASSION.
### Ch. XVII

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap letter B decorated with floral and leaf motifs.</image-desc>Kindness, moreover, gives birth to compassion; for only pious minds stoop down to the needs of all with kind compassion. Compassion is a pious stirring of the heart toward every need. Compassion makes the pious soldier of CHRIST suffer with his King, who was so cruelly slain. It impresses upon his mind the so-necessary cause of His death, His so pious and prompt will to suffer, the harsh words, the harsher lashings, and the harshest torments of the cross. The bitter remembrance of these things strikes the pious heart with tearful compassion, and crucifies it together with CHRIST the spouse in sorrowful love.

Compassion urges one to pay attention to their own negligences and defects: their lukewarmness <note>original: "teporem"</note> and laziness, their loss of time, and the great poverty of virtues and perfected character caused by such self-neglect. Through the consideration of these things, a person takes pity on their own soul with a saving compassion, laboring from then on with all their strength to please God. Finally, compassion encourages one to weigh the many errors of neighbors—namely, the slighting <note>original: "parui-pensionem," literally a small-weighing or low estimation</note> of God and eternal happiness, and the [forgetting] of divine benefi...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Compassion, specifically focusing on the neighbor's spiritual and physical plight. It completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the "needs of neighbors."</meta>

<header>I</header>

...neighbors, <note>The fragment "orum" completes "proximorum" (neighbors) from the previous page.</note> both in their actions and in their sufferings; their ingratitude, their great poverty in the knowledge, possession, and practice of virtues; and on the other hand, their great cleverness in worldly wickedness and craftiness, their diligent search for temporal gains, and their non-existent or weak will and haste to seek the happiness of eternal goods. By the consideration of these things, and by the sting of compassion, the pious mind is pricked and inflamed with a desire for the salvation of all. 

Compassion, therefore, is mercifully moved by the physical needs of all: noticing in others hunger, thirst, cold, nakedness, illness, great want, manifold injuries, and contempt. In others, it notices the loss of temporal passions <note>original: "passionum," here referring to things one undergoes or suffers, like misfortunes</note> and friends, the corruption of honor and good reputation, the disturbance of peace and quiet, and the harsh outcomes of the innumerable accidents that weigh down human nature. 

The attention to these things tortures the kind soul with pious compassion. But above all, a source of grief is the impatience of those so afflicted; for they not only fail to accumulate rewards of merit in their adversities, but moreover, through their lack of endurance, they deserve eternal punishments. This indeed is the work <note>original: "negocium," meaning the duty or business</note> of compassion. 

Furthermore, through compassion, the third beast of mortal crimes—namely, envy—is overthrown; because compassion wounds through piety and strikes with an exceedingly sweet wound the need for the common charity <note>original: "charitatis," the selfless, divine love for others</note> of all. Until this aforementioned progeny of virtues reigns, no dross of negligence or sluggishness can be removed. For compassion alone, above all others, flows from its office from the Giver of all virtues: so that it may pity and grieve for the needs of all with a compassionate heart <note>original: "piis visceribus," literally "pious bowels," the traditional seat of deep mercy</note>. 

For this reason, Truth <note>Referring to Christ</note> also promises, saying: *Blessed are they who mourn: for they shall be comforted.* <note>Matthew 5:4</note> For those things which they now sow in tears and compassion, they shall reap in the exultation of eternal happiness.

## ¶ ON GENEROSITY. ### Chapter XII

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'E' featuring scrolling foliage and a central flower within a square border.</image-desc>

**From** compassion and mercy, generosity <note>original: "largitas," meaning a bountiful or liberal giving that flows from the heart</note> is born. For no one is truly generous through the supernatural favor of common charity except the person who is first merciful. Although some may give many things to specific, favored individuals, they are actually void of charity and supernatural generosity. Generosity is, in fact, the liberal outpouring of a heart moved by charitable compassion. 

Indeed, when the mind is wounded by pious compassion and remembers the torments of the Lord’s passion, it pours itself out with such generosity in return for such wondrous charity <note>The author suggests that reflecting on Christ's sacrifice inspires an equal desire to give of oneself.</note> that for every single moment <note>original: "articulis," referring to the specific points or events of the crucifixion</note> of so bitter a martyrdom, it lovingly repays Christ (who suffered for its sake) with praise, honor, and the reverence of sorrowing gratitude. It joyfully submits both body and soul in humble and devout subjection. 

Furthermore, as the wretched soul reflects on the benefits of goodness bestowed upon it and its own negligence or slowness to respond, it soon flows back upward with its whole spirit toward the divine generosity, clemency, faithfulness, and benevolence, offering perpetual servitude with a lavish will. Then, considering the various errors and injustices of neighbors, it turns the stream of its own generosity back to the source from which it began: it appeals to the munificence of divine goodness with pious cries, asking that God might take pity on those vanishing into vanities, open the fountain of His gift-flowing generosity and the infusion of His grace, and lead those who are erring back to the path of truth. 

Finally, the possessor of this flowing generosity, observing such manifold physical needs among neighbors, ministers, gives, and lends according to what each requires; and according to their own means, they console each person—always preserving the rule of discretion <note>original: "saluo discretionis canone," referring to the spiritual gift of discernment to give wisely rather than recklessly</note>. Through this virtue of generosity, the seven works of mercy <note>A traditional list of acts found in Matthew 25: feeding the hungry, giving drink to the thirsty, clothing the naked, sheltering the homeless, visiting the sick, visiting the imprisoned, and burying the dead.</note> are performed: by the rich indeed, through their services and resources; by the po...

<vocab>generosity, compassion, mercy, Christ, seven works of mercy, liberal outpouring</vocab>

<summary>This chapter defines generosity as a natural outflow of compassion, specifically triggered by reflecting on Christ's Passion and the needs of others. It emphasizes that true generosity must be rooted in supernatural charity and practiced with discretion.</summary>
<keywords>generosity, largitas, Passion of Christ, works of mercy, compassion, discretion, charity, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Generosity (largitas) from the previous page, specifically concluding how this virtue is perfected through pious works and a willing spirit. It then transitions into the next virtue in the sequence: Diligence or Zeal (strennuitas).</meta>

...with pious works, indeed, through holy favors and good intentions, by which undoubtedly the true virtue of generosity is perfected in them. Through this generosity of the soul, virtues are multiplied and the powers of the soul are adorned: for the generous person is always joyful in spirit, solicitous in heart, profuse in affection, and commonly benevolent in all pious acts. Finally, a bountiful mind <note>original: "dapsilis animus," suggesting a spirit that is feast-like or lavish in its giving</note> that does not love earthly things—even if it is stripped of physical resources—is nevertheless rich in will and like unto God. This is because the vital sense of the soul is nourished by the sweet breath of abundant charity, by which it has perfectly crushed within itself the fourth beast of the mortal sins: avarice <note>In medieval theology, the seven deadly sins were often envisioned as beasts; avarice (greed) is here listed as the fourth.</note>. Regarding such people, **CHRIST** says in the Gospel: *Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy*—namely, on that day when the Son of Man sits upon the throne of his majesty and his sweet-speaking voice is heard caressing the sheep on his right hand, saying: "Come, you blessed of my Father, possess the kingdom prepared for you because of your mercy since the foundation of the world."

### On Diligence and Zeal. Chapter XX.

<image-desc>Ornamental decorative initial L with floral motifs inside a square woodcut border.</image-desc>

**Generosity** thereafter gives birth to supernatural diligence and zeal <note>original: "strennuitas," a term implying vigorous, active, and eager effort in spiritual life</note> in studious works. No one, however, attains this unless they are a merciful person, overflowing with inward bounty. This zeal is a restless alacrity of the heart for exercising the works of virtue; it is an untiring emulator of the life of **CHRIST** and the holy fathers. Driven by the stimulus of this zeal, the lavish man desires to surrender <note>original: "mancipare," a legal term meaning to formally transfer ownership; here it refers to giving one's entire being over to God.</note> his body and soul, his existence and life, his strengths and resources to divine praise and honors. The zeal of reason and discretion keeps watch: it ensures that virtues are rightly performed in both heart and body. Through supernatural zeal, the powers of the soul are polished for the reception of divine influence and are rendered more vigorous for acts of virtue; the conscience from these...

From compassion and mercy, indeed,
**generosity** is born. For no one, except
the merciful, is supernaturally generous
through the favor of common charity <note>original: "charitas," referring here to divine love or "agape," rather than just modern "charity" in the sense of giving money</note>. 
Even if one might give many things to 
specific favored persons, it is not 
through charity or any supernatural 
generosity. Now, generosity is the liberal outpouring of a heart moved 
by charitable compassion. The mind, wounded by pious compassion, 
remembers the torments of the Lord’s passion: it pours itself out with 
such generosity in return for such wondrous love <note>original: "vicissitudine tam mirande charitatis," suggesting a reciprocal exchange where the soul tries to match God's love</note> that, for 
every single moment <note>original: "articulis," which can mean both moments in time and the physical joints of the body, likely referring here to the specific stages of Christ's suffering</note> of that bitter martyrdom, it lovingly 
repays **CHRIST**, who suffered for it, with praise, honor, and the 
reverence of a grieving gratitude; and it joyfully submits both body 
and soul in humble and devoted subjection. Recalling also the 
benefits of goodness bestowed upon its own wretched soul, and 
the negligence of its own slowness, it soon flows back upward 
with its whole spirit toward divine generosity and clemency, 
faithfulness and benevolence, offering perpetual service with a 
lavish will. Considering thereafter the various errors and injustices 
of neighbors, it soon reflects the stream of its own generosity 
back to the place from which it came <note>This refers to God, the source of all goodness; the soul sends its love back to God on behalf of others</note>: it appeals to the 
bounty of divine goodness with pious cries, so that He might 
take pity on those fading away in vanities and open the fountain 
of His gift-flowing generosity and the infusion of His grace, 
leading the erring back to the path of truth. Finally, the possessor 
of such overflowing compassion, observing the many-layered 
physical needs among neighbors, administers, gives, and lends 
according to what each person requires; and according to his own 
means, he consoles each individual (keeping the rule of discretion 
intact). By this virtue of generosity, the seven works of mercy <note>The Seven Corporal Works of Mercy are a traditional list in Christian ethics: feeding the hungry, giving drink to the thirsty, clothing the naked, sheltering the homeless, visiting the sick, visiting the imprisoned, and burying the dead.</note> 
are practiced: by the wealthy, indeed, through service and resources; 
by the poor—

### ¶ On Diligence and Strenuousness. Chapter XX.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'L' featuring an ornate letter entwined with scrolling foliage and floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>

Generosity, therefore, gives birth to supernatural **diligence** and **strenuousness** <term>strennuitas: a state of active, vigorous effort or zeal in the spiritual life, the direct opposite of sloth (acedia)</term> in diligent works: which no one, however, attains unless he is a merciful man, overflowing with inward bounty. Now, **strenuousness** is an unquiet alacrity of the heart for exercising the works of the virtues, an indefatigable imitator of the life of **CHRIST** and the holy fathers. Driven by the sting of this strenuousness, the bountiful man desires to surrender body and soul, being and living, strength and resources, to divine praises and honors. The strenuousness of reason and discernment keeps watch: and it ensures that virtues are rightly performed in both heart and body. Through supernatural strenuousness, the powers of the soul are refined for the reception of divine influence, and they are rendered more vigorous for the acts of the virtues; the conscience from these...

Not only is the torment of dullness struck down through the virtue of **strenuousness**, but even bodily heaviness and natural laziness are sometimes shaken off. Concerning this kind of virtue, Truth <note>referring to Christ or the personification of Divine Truth</note> says:

This will occur then, namely, when the glory of God shall appear, and according to the measure of charity and justice, the desire for glory in each of the blessed shall be filled.

## ¶ On Temperance and Sobriety. Chapter X.

<image-desc>A large square decorative initial 'S' at the beginning of the chapter. The letter is stylized with foliate patterns and curls, set against a background of intricate floral and leafy vines.</image-desc>
**Strenuousness** <note>or spiritual diligence</note> moreover gives birth to **temperance** and **sobriety**: in which no one is so powerful as the diligent and strenuous governor of both body and soul according to the rule of justice. **Sobriety** governs the intellectual as well as the animal and bodily powers from all superfluity, not wishing to know or to savor things that are not permitted, but to be wise unto sobriety <note>original: "sapere ad sobrietatem," a reference to Romans 12:3, meaning to have sound judgment and avoid intellectual pride</note>. 

Indeed, the nature of God is incomprehensible; it exceeds every creature, even the heavenly ones, because any creature can only comprehend that which is created. But even if He exists not only above and outside, but also within every creature by essence, presence, and power, nevertheless every created capacity is too narrow to comprehend the divinity. However, so that the intellectual creature—which alone is capable of receiving the deity—may know by seeing and enjoy by loving, it is necessary for it to be raised above itself by divine light and to be drawn into God by the sweetness of love. Thus, it may comprehend God according to its own capacity. He, therefore, who would wish to know what God is, and would become a searcher of His majesty, being overwhelmed by glory, would perhaps turn into madness <note>an allusion to Proverbs 25:27: "the searcher of majesty shall be overwhelmed by glory," warning against over-curious speculation into the nature of God</note>. Con[cerning]...

<summary>This page describes how the virtue of Strenuousness (Diligence) overcomes Sloth and leads to Temperance. It emphasizes that while God's nature is incomprehensible to the finite mind, the soul can experience Him through divine light and love rather than through intellectual over-reaching.</summary>
<keywords>Strenuousness, Diligence, Sloth, Acedia, Temperance, Sobriety, Incomprehensibility of God, Divine Light, Spiritual Marriage, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sobriety from Chapter X. Having established that Sobriety governs the mind and body, the author now distinguishes between spiritual sobriety—the humble acceptance of faith's mysteries—and physical sobriety, which regulates the "animal" appetites of the body.</meta>

...for every created light is surpassed by the face-to-face vision of the divine essence. For although nature, scripture, and every creature testify *that* He exists, His **essence** <note>original: "quidditas," literally "whatness," a scholastic term for the inner nature or definition of a thing</note> is hidden from all by its own excellence. The articles of faith likewise are not to be debated by the investigation of reason, but simply believed with firm assertion. For while we are in the body, we are only able to see **through a glass darkly** <note>original: "per speculum & in ænigmate," a reference to 1 Corinthians 13:12</note>. 

Finally, the obscurities of the Scriptures dictated by the Holy Spirit are not to be twisted to suit private opinion, but must be understood healthily according to the lives and teachings of **CHRIST** and the holy fathers, and faithfully explained. Therefore, from the Scriptures and all creatures, let only that be taken which is proven in any way to be beneficial for salvation; and thus the work of **spiritual sobriety** will be completed.

Furthermore, sobriety must be maintained in the bodily senses, and the **animal powers** <note>original: "bestiales potentię," referring to the irrational or instinctive part of the human soul that shares traits with animals, such as hunger or reproduction</note> must be restrained by the governance of reason, lest, of course, the beast-like appetite pour itself out excessively upon food and drink. Instead, let the hungry man take nourishment just as a sick man takes medicine: namely, so that he may preserve or restore bodily health for the service of God. 

Sobriety must also be observed in words and deeds, in speech and in silence, in foods and drinks, in labors and in rest, according to the traditions of the ecclesiastical institution and the examples of the holy fathers. Indeed, through sobriety of the spirit, faith is guarded firmly and steadily, the intellect is purified, reason is quieted for the understanding of truth, the will is easily bent toward works of virtue according to the pleasure of the divine will, unshaken peace of heart is preserved, and the conscience is untangled from all the knots of scrupulosity. In all these things, stable peace is possessed both in oneself and in God. Also, through bodily sobriety, the health of the body and the **balanced temperament** <note>original: "eucrasia," a term from ancient and medieval medicine meaning the perfect, healthy balance of the four humors</note> of nature is preserved; and [social] conduct...

<summary>This page defines sobriety as both an intellectual humility that accepts the limits of human reason regarding God and a physical discipline that treats food as medicine for God's service. It argues that such restraint leads to inner peace, a clear conscience, and physical health.</summary>
<keywords>sobriety, essence of God, faith vs reason, biblical interpretation, animal appetites, eucrasia, spiritual discipline, peace of heart</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sobriety from Chapter XX. It concludes the benefits of this virtue—specifically its role in fostering peace and defeating gluttony—before transitioning into Chapter XXI, which defines Chastity as the offspring of mental and physical Sobriety.</meta>

...conduct is made beautiful and reputation grows. Thus it happens that the **sober man** has peace within himself and with his neighbor, because through the virtue of sobriety, by drawing all people to his own grace, he becomes pleasing to everyone.

But he also conquers, binds, and slays that savage beast, **gluttony**, which is the sixth in the order of mortal sins. Regarding the virtue of this type of man, Truth also says: **Blessed are the peacemakers: for they shall be called the children of God** <note>original: "beati pacifici: quoniam filii dei vocabuntur," quoting Matthew 5:9. Here, "Truth" refers to Christ speaking in the Gospels.</note>, as they are made similar to the Son of God. For He destined peace for men of good will by the angelic voice, preached peace to all through Himself, and when about to go to the Father, He left peace behind for His disciples. Those who create peace through sobriety and temperance He will make heirs of His Father's kingdom, to reign peacefully with Him through eternal ages.

## ¶ On Chastity. Chapter XXI.

<image-desc>A large ornamental initial P featuring intricate scrolling floral and foliage patterns within a square border.</image-desc>Furthermore, sobriety of mind and body gives birth to **chastity**; indeed, no one perfectly possesses this unless they are someone who guards the sobriety of both mind and body. Moreover, chastity is: to adhere <note>original: "adhærere"</note> to no creature with an inclined desire, but to God alone. For creatures are to be used, but God alone is to be enjoyed <note>This reflects a famous distinction by St. Augustine: *uti* (to use things as tools) versus *frui* (to enjoy God as the ultimate end).</note>. 

Mental chastity joins its possessor to God above sense and intellect, and above all the infusible gifts of the soul. Since whatever a creature is able to comprehend, chastity strives to transcend that very thing, and to rest in the only **incomprehensible Good**. 

For this reason, the **Sacrament of the Body of CHRIST** <note>original: "sacramentum corporis Christi," referring to the Eucharist or Holy Communion</note> must be received, not for the sake of the delight of spiritual flavor, nor for the appetite of desire, nor for the feeling of sweetness, nor for the increase of peace or tranquility—not, in short, for any other cause than for the honor of God, and so that one may have a more fruitful progress in the virtues. Indeed, mental chastity commands that this be observed in the highest degree. 

**Chastity of the heart**, however, is this: when urged by the temptations of the flesh and stimulated by natural impulses, to flee to God with an upright will and total confidence, as if to the safest refuge of chastity, and perpetually to [adhere] to Him...

<summary>This page concludes the treatment of Sobriety as a path to peace and a weapon against gluttony, then defines Chastity as a total orientation toward God. It specifically warns that even holy acts, like receiving the Eucharist, should be done for God's honor rather than for personal emotional or spiritual satisfaction.</summary>
<keywords>Sobriety, Peacemakers, Gluttony, Chastity, Eucharist, Mental Chastity, Heartfelt Chastity, Jan van Ruusbroec, Temperance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the virtue of Chastity from Chapter XXI, explaining its spiritual benefits and how it defeats the vice of Lust. It then transitions into Chapter XXIII (noting a skip in chapter numbering or a variation in the manuscript's sequence), which introduces Justice as the guardian of the virtues against the "three enemies": the devil, the world, and the flesh.</meta>

...to present a most pure will for adhering <note>The word "adhering" (original: *adherendi*) continues from the previous page's final sentence.</note> to God. For to offer consent to sin, or to the desires of the flesh, or to the beastly pleasures of nature, is to be separated from God in no small measure. Furthermore, **chastity of the body** is to keep oneself untouched by any acts of the world which conscience has marked as obscene, or which are prohibited by heavenly mandates, or are contrary to the divine will. 

Through this triple form of chastity, the seventh beast of the mortal sins—**lust** <note>original: *luxuria*. While often translated as "luxury" in modern English, in this moral context, it specifically refers to lechery or sexual excess.</note>—is utterly exterminated. It guards, directs, and governs the external senses; it restrains, corrects, and shapes the beastly appetite. It is the firmest closure of the heart against the assault of earthly things, and at the same time, the widest opening toward heavenly things and the influx of all truth, and the most beautiful adornment of the whole spiritual life. 

To the possessors of this chastity, Truth <note>A common medieval shorthand for Christ, based on John 14:6.</note> promises in the Gospel: **"Blessed,"** He says, **"are the pure in heart, for they shall see God"** <note>original: "beati mundo corde quoniam ipsi deum videbunt," quoting Matthew 5:8.</note>. In this vision, all joy and the entire reward consists, as well as the entrance into eternal happiness. For the sake of this matter, let us be **sober** and **temperate** in all things, declining any occasions and companionships by which the chastity of both soul and body could be violated.

### ¶ On the three adversaries to be overcome through justice. Chapter XXIII

<image-desc>Ornate square woodcut initial 'H' decorated with floral flourishes and a central classical vase within a lined border.</image-desc>
Desiring to be strong in the offspring of such noble virtues and to expel their opposites, let us join **justice** to purity of heart; and as long as we live, let us entrust those virtues to the guardianship and protection of these two qualities. For we are attacked daily in every place and in many ways by three troublesome adversaries. If we make peace or agreement with any one of them, we are utterly defeated, because they are all in agreement against all justice. 

One of these is the **devil**, another is the **world**, and the third is our **own flesh**, which holds its own weapons at home—namely, the **desires of the flesh**—with which the others fight against us as if they were borrowed tools. The cause and occasion of this battle are **idleness** <note>original: *otium*, often described as the "devil's workshop" in medieval theology.</note>, the injury of virtues, and the neglect of the divine.

### ¶ Concerning Chastity. Chapter XXII

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut initial 'P' featuring scrolling foliage and floral motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>
Furthermore, sobriety of mind and body gives birth to **chastity**: which indeed no one perfectly possesses, except for the person who guards the sobriety of mind and body. ## ¶ ON THE THREEFOLD WAY OF MEETING THE
## BRIDEGROOM.
### Chapter XXV.

<image-desc>Large decorative initial V featuring foliate and floral scrollwork within a double-lined square border.</image-desc>

Therefore, having seen the Bridegroom through grace and a pure conscience, and the soul having now gone out distinguished by virtues, what finally remains? "Go out," he says, "to meet him." <note>This refers to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25:6: "Behold, the bridegroom cometh; go ye out to meet him."</note> Going out is of no use if no reaching follows. A race is in vain when no encounter is offered. It is not enough to go out if it does not happen that you meet him. For indeed, salvation consists in the meeting with the Bridegroom; the virtues receive both their beginning and their end in it, and if it is removed, no virtue is ever perfect. 

Whoever, therefore, desires to meet the most loving Bridegroom, and with Him and in Him to possess eternal life, must meet Him in a threefold manner on this path of life. For a meeting is nothing other than the joining together of two who encounter each other. Just as, therefore, in our homeland <note>original: "patria" — a common medieval term for Heaven, the true home of the soul.</note> we shall be joined to God by three endowments of the soul—namely, by vision, by love, and by fruition <note>In mystical theology, "fruition" (*fruitio*) is the delightful enjoyment and rest found in possessing God.</note>—so in this life...

<summary>This page concludes the allegory of the soul as a kingdom ruled by Free Will and introduces the necessity of "meeting" the Bridegroom through vision, love, and fruition. It emphasizes that practicing virtues is only the preparation ("going out") for the ultimate goal: a direct encounter with God.</summary>

<keywords>Free Will, Justice, Virtues, Spiritual Marriage, Bridegroom, Vision, Love, Fruition, Ruusbroec</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 25 of Jan van Ruusbroec's "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage," transitioning from the soul's "going out" to the specific ways it meets the Divine Bridegroom through the theological virtues in the active life.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
->I<-

In the way of this life, pre-tasting the glory of future blessedness <note>original: "beatitudinis," the state of supreme happiness or "blessedness" in the presence of God</note> through grace, we are joined to God in three ways—as if by the operations of the three theological virtues <note>The theological virtues are Faith, Hope, and Charity</note> corresponding to the three endowments of the soul. However, we are joined more or less excellently according to different degrees of perfection.

And indeed, in the way of the active life in which we are now proceeding in our discourse, we encounter Him in three ways: namely, by a **right intention** illustrated by the light of faith; by a **chief love** inflamed by the little spark of charity; and by a **restful adherence** solidified by the anchor of hope.

By a **right intention**, I say: so that in all things that we either do or suffer, desire or avoid, we may set the simple eye of the heart in a straight line before the divine sight.

By a **chief love** also: for this reason, the union of love is placed in both—that is, in the homeland <note>original: "patria," a common theological term for Heaven, the soul's true home</note> and on the way <note>original: "via," referring to our current earthly life as a journey</note>—because charity, shed abroad in our hearts here through the Holy Spirit, never fails.

By a **restful adherence**: so that, transcending all movements of both moral and spiritual exercise, and every savor of internal sweetness and divinely infused grace, by the swiftness of our intention and the sharpness of our love, we may rest in God as in the final end of our movement.

But so that we may be more clearly instructed concerning these things as far as it now pertains to us, let us repeat those same three things we have already mentioned a little more broadly. Therefore, so that one may be joined to God by meeting Him with a **right intention**: let him always hold Him present under some aspect of divinity; namely, that in all his acts and affections he may desire and intend to honor and love Him who created heaven and earth and all things that are in them; who for man's sake became human <note>original: "humanatus," more literally "en-manned" or incarnated</note> and was crucified; and who not only can, but also wishes to bestow eternal blessedness upon man. For under whatever enigma <note>original: "ænigmate," referring to 1 Corinthians 13:12, "seeing through a glass darkly"</note> or likeness, under a god-like aspect of the deity, a man proposes any object to himself: by reason...

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 25 of "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage," specifically elaborating on the first way the soul meets God in the active life: through a "right intention." It bridges from the previous page's final sentence, completing the thought on how one is instructed to direct their aim toward the Divine.</meta>

->DE ORNATV SPIRITVALIVM NVPTIARVM<-
<note>original: "DE OR.NVP.SP.", an abbreviation for the title "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage"</note>

...intends. If he worships any of the divine persons under the majesty of the godhead: he intends rightly. If he considers the Creator, the Redeemer, the Savior, or the Teacher—if he contemplates eternal blessedness, power, wisdom, truth, or goodness under the unsearchable nature of divinity with the **simple eye of the heart**: he intends rightly. For although the divine names <note>original: "nomina diuina"</note> are many, nonetheless His nature is one and simple. This is because the human intellect cannot grasp the various modes of perfection in a single conception. This occurs partly because it receives knowledge through symbols <note>original: "symbola," meaning signs or representative images</note> drawn from created things, in which there are various modes of perfection according to their different forms; and partly because that which is one and simple is made many in our understanding, even if it were received immediately, just as the procession of divine goodness is multiplied among other creatures.

Therefore, since God is perfect in every way according to one and the same reality, the human intellect cannot fully grasp the divine perfection in a single conception, and consequently, cannot name it with a single word. Thus, it is necessary for the intellect to have various conceptions of Him, which are various "reasons" or aspects, and to assign various names signifying those aspects. However, if the human intellect were to see God through Himself—which will happen **in the homeland** <note>original: "in patria," a common theological term for Heaven, our true home</note>—it would be able to give Him a single name. This name would not signify wisdom alone, or goodness alone, and so on for the rest; rather, it would include the meanings of all of these at once.

Thus it is clear from this that a plurality of divine names occurs because God exceeds our intellect, both because of the fullness of His perfection and because of the narrowness of our intellect, which is insufficient to comprehend Him. This, then, is the first mode of **meeting and joining** <note>original: "obuiationis & coniūctionis"</note> by which the spouse...

This multi-faceted "right intention" is the first way the soul begins to meet its Divine Spouse.</summary>

->CONCERNING CERTAIN [MODES]<-
<note>original: "DE OVIB.", likely an abbreviation or OCR error for "DE QVIBUS," referring to the "certain modes" of meeting God discussed in this section.</note>

<unclear>u</unclear>se is contemplated through an ever-present intention. <note>In medieval theology, "intention" (intentio) is the soul directing itself toward a specific goal, here being God.</note> For a right intention is a kind of spiritual vision. However, it is not enough both to intend and to know, unless love is also present. For this reason, in all its actions, the soul that intends rightly should lean its head upon the breast of the Lord through love <note>This is a traditional mystical reference to St. John the Evangelist leaning on Jesus during the Last Supper, symbolizing deep intimacy.</note>; so that it may be joined by special love to the one to whom it is joined by right intention. Furthermore, in Him who is rightly intended and who is especially loved, one must rest immovably, more than in all His gifts. The soul must nonetheless rest in God above all its own little gifts <note>original: "munuscula," a diminutive suggesting the humble nature of human offerings compared to God's greatness</note> which it sends to the divine majesty through its messengers. For intention and love present all the gifts of the soul’s good works to the divine gaze; but above the whole multitude of these, let the soul desire to rest in its spouse alone, so that it may fulfill what is said: 
> go out to meet him. <note>A reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25:6, a central theme of this book.</note>

It specifically addresses the paradox of the "Divine Names"—how a single, simple God can be described by many different attributes like "Goodness" or "Wisdom" due to the limitations of human understanding.</meta>

->ON THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-
<note>original: "DE OR.NVP.SP.", an abbreviation for "De Ornatu Nuptiarum Spiritualium," the title of the work.</note>
->N iii<-
<note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages in the correct order for binding.</note>

intends. If the soul venerates any of the divine persons under the majesty of the deity, it intends rightly. If it understands God as creator, or redeemer, or savior, or teacher; if it contemplates eternal <term>happiness</term><note>original: "beatitudinem," referring to the state of supreme blessedness in the presence of God</note>, or power, or wisdom, or truth, or goodness under the unsearchable nature of divinity with the simple eye of the heart: it intends rightly. For although there are many divine names, nevertheless the divine nature is one and simple. For the human intellect cannot grasp diverse modes of perfection in a single concept. This is partly because the intellect receives its knowledge through symbols from created things, in which there are various modes of perfection according to various forms. It is also because that which is one and simple becomes "plural" within our intellect even if it were received directly, just as the single flow of divine goodness is multiplied as it enters into different creatures. 

Hence, since God is perfect in all ways according to one and the same reality, the human intellect cannot fully apprehend divine perfection in a single concept, and consequently cannot give it a single name. Therefore, it is necessary for the mind to have diverse concepts—which are different <term>notions</term><note>original: "rationes," referring to the various ways the mind reasons about or categorizes God's attributes</note>—about Him, and to assign diverse names that signify those notions. 

But if the human intellect were to see God through Himself—which will happen in our <term>homeland</term><note>original: "patria," a common theological term for Heaven, representing the soul's true home after its earthly pilgrimage</note>—it would be able to assign Him a single name. This name would not signify wisdom alone, or goodness alone, and so on; rather, it would include the meanings of all of these at once. 

Thus, it is clear from this why a plurality of divine names exists: because God exceeds our intellect, both because of the fullness of His perfection and because of the narrowness of our own intellect, which is inadequate for comprehending Him. This, therefore, is the first mode of meeting and joining, by which the spouse...

# ON THE ADORNMENTS OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE

...surveys the virtues, benefits, and promises: he must again respond to all these with praise of His presence, with reverence, and with the giving of thanks. For through these, faith is made stronger, the spirit more ardent, and more inclined toward every good. Truly, so that he may progress further in works of virtue, he must again go out to meet **CHRIST** through the renunciation of himself in all his actions. This means seeking not at all what is his own, nor setting before himself any foreign cause <note>that is, any motivation outside of God's will</note>, but doing all things with discernment, intending in all things the praise and honor of God, and finally persisting in Him. 

Through these things indeed reason is illuminated, hope is raised up, and charity <note>meaning divine love or *caritas*</note> is increased. The spirit becomes more devoted and more ready for all virtues. For in all good works, the intention is to be directed toward God; for in evil ones, it cannot be. Dual ends <note>competing goals or ultimate purposes</note> are not to be established, such that anything else might be intended along with God which is loved equally or above, and not below, God. 

For every creature, as such, is to be loved below God—not with an excessive love, but in order <note>referring to *ordinata caritas*, or the "right ordering of love" where God is first</note> toward God; so that either the thing itself helps and cooperates toward the good, or through it the soul is led by the hand to God; and thus the rectitude of order is preserved. 

Behold, in these ways: let us hasten to meet the bridegroom (while we run on the path of this life) in all our actions and affections with a right intention: so that at last, the journey’s end being reached and the final cry sounding—*Behold, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him* <note>original: "ecce spōsus venit exite obuiā ei"</note>—we, having prepared our lamps, may be admitted with the bridegroom to the eternal nuptials and meet him with glorious embraces. This is the adornment of the moral and active life, necessary indeed for all the faithful of **CHRIST** chosen for the eternal nuptials: lest, if the oil fails and the lamps are not prepared, they be excluded with the foolish virgins, and with him who does not have the wedding garment...

<summary>This page describes the "active life" of a believer, emphasizing that all virtues and love for created things must be directed toward God to prepare the soul for the "spiritual marriage." It uses the Parable of the Ten Virgins to illustrate the necessity of maintaining a "right intention" as the oil that keeps the soul's lamp burning for Christ's return.</summary>

<keywords>Spiritual Marriage, right intention, renunciation of self, Parable of the Ten Virgins, charity, moral life, active life, Divine Bridegroom</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of virtue for the soul's union with God, transitioning from the foundational virtues of the active life toward a more spiritual or contemplative state.</meta>

...banquet, they may be cast into the outer darkness <note>original: *in tenebras externas*; a biblical reference to the punishment of those excluded from the kingdom of heaven</note>. However, we by no means say that reaching such a height of perfection as we have described is necessary for everyone. But let it be that even if one does not shine with such great marks of virtue, they should at least not pollute their life with the disgrace of any contrary crime. For to lack virtues is surely to be filthy with vices, as **CHRIST** says: "Whoever is not with me is against me." He who is not humble is proud. Indeed, he who is deprived of humility and polluted by the filth of pride should know that he is not of **CHRIST'S** lot <note>meaning he does not belong to Christ's chosen people or inheritance</note>; and so it is with all other virtues and sins. For it is necessary either to possess virtues through grace or to be filthy with sin. Let each person, therefore, prove and examine himself, and act according to the example shown to him in this book.

# ¶ ON THE HIGHEST STAGE OF THE
## ACTIVE LIFE. Chapter XXVII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut drop cap 'C' depicting a stag standing in a wooded landscape.</image-desc>Furthermore, just as in the natural world the kinds of beings are linked in this order—where the highest of the lower things are joined to the lowest of the higher things—so too in moral matters, the highest stage of a lower life reaches the lowest point of a higher one. Therefore, so that we may begin a new approach to the spiritual life, let us add the highest stage of the <term>active life <note>original: *vita activa*; the stage of spiritual development focused on outward works and the cultivation of virtues</note></term> at the end of this present book. 

Thus, the just man, flourishing with the adornments of the virtues previously described, offering all his deeds as a sacrifice of praise and divine reverence before the sight of Divine Majesty, and clinging above all to the divine goodness with a special love, burns with holy desires to gaze upon the face of his **CHRIST**: so that He whom he [seeks] for such great benefits—namely the <term>Incarnation <note>God becoming human in the person of Jesus</note></term> and the <term>Passion <note>the suffering and death of Jesus</note></term>, the destruction of eternal death, the remission of sins, the infusion of grace, and the [institution] of the sacraments...

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page concludes the first book regarding the "active life." It follows the previous page’s allegory of Zacchaeus, explaining how the soul transcends the natural mind to meet Christ in the light of faith, thereby completing its moral preparation for spiritual marriage.</meta>

No human intelligence is capable of comprehending Him under the light of creation alone. But where knowledge or understanding stands outside, love and desire immediately enter. Truly, when the soul—with all its affection and all its intention—rises above everything it understands and into the sea of divinity, it is poured into God in this way; it rests in Him, and God rests in it, through a reciprocal love <note>original: *amore vicario*; referring to a mutual or shared exchange of love between the Creator and the creature</note>. Wherefore, when the soul transcends the entire multitude of created things, every preoccupation of the senses, and its own natural light, it ascends to the summit of its mind. There, meeting **CHRIST** in the light of faith, it is divinely enlightened and taught that God is incomprehensible and unknowable. But where it bows itself with all its desire toward this incomprehensible God, it is there—rightly meeting **CHRIST**—that it is filled with His heavenly gifts. Finally, where it rests in life-giving love above all the gifts of God, above itself, and above every created thing: there it remains in God, and God remains in it. This, then, is the highest way of meeting the bridegroom and the most eminent adornment <note>original: *ornatus*; a key theme referring to the soul "dressing itself" in virtues for its wedding to God</note> that we are able to attain on the path of the active life. Therefore, if we have laid a foundation of justice, charity, and humility, and if we have built upon it the moral edifice—that is, the virtues described previously—and if we meet **CHRIST** through faith, intention, and love: then God already remains in us and we in God, and we flourish in the adornment of the active life.

<meta>This page transitions from the first book, which focused on the "active life" (moral preparation), to the second book, which focuses on the "spiritual and affective life" (the inner life of the heart and soul). It picks up the narrative of the soul meeting Christ, specifically using the imagery of the Wise Virgins from the Gospel of Matthew.</meta>

ON THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE

## ¶ THE SECOND book on the adornment of the spiritual and affective life, containing 71 chapters. The summary of the matters to be discussed in this second book.
### Chapter I.

<image-desc>Large decorative initial P featuring intricate floral and foliate scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>The wise virgin (that is, the chaste soul) who, having despised earthly affections, has decided to live for God alone, has taken into the vessel of her heart the oil of charity <note>original: *charitatis*; in this context, it refers to divine love or "caritas," the highest form of Christian love</note> and of diligent deeds, along with the lamp of a pure conscience. However, while the bridegroom **CHRIST** delays—by withdrawing internal consolation and the influence of His gifts—she suddenly slumbers, sleeps, and grows sluggish. But at midnight (that is, suddenly and unexpectedly), a spiritual cry is made in the soul: "Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him." Since, therefore (as we have already said in the introduction), four things are handed down for us to note in these words—namely, the vision of the bridegroom, His coming, the going out, and the meeting—and since we have already briefly dispatched these matters in the first book regarding the adornment of the active life (as much as God has granted), let us now, invoking His grace, approach the second book. Here, we shall explain these same four points more deeply (as we promised) concerning the adornment of the spiritual and affective <note>The "affective" life refers to the dimension of the soul involving feelings, desires, and the will, as opposed to purely intellectual reasoning.</note> life, which many who are already adorned with moral virtues attain through the diligence of internal fervor. 

First, therefore, he rouses our intellect to see, saying: "Behold," wishing it to be enlightened with spiritual clarity and a more eminent grace. Then he shows what we ought to see, saying "the bridegroom comes," suggesting the spiritual and internal coming of the bridegroom. In the third place, when he says "go out," he commands the spiritual "going out" of internal exercise <note>In mystical theology, "going out" refers to the soul leaving behind its own self-interest or worldly distractions to focus entirely on God.</note> according to the norm of justice. Finally, in the fourth place, he gives the final cause of our entire going out: and this is the meeting with the bridegroom in the unity of divine enjoyment <note>original: *fruitionis*; a technical term in medieval theology for the joyful "possession" or "tasting" of God's presence</note>.

## ¶ ON THE THREE things necessary for the vision of spiritual things.
### Chapter II.

<summary>This page introduces the second book of the treatise, moving from external moral actions to the internal spiritual life through the allegory of the Wise Virgins. It outlines the four stages of the soul's inner journey: vision, arrival, going out, and meeting God.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual life, affective life, Jan van Ruusbroec, Wise Virgins, bridegroom, Christ, charity, divine enjoyment, mystical union</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition into Book II, moving from the external "active life" described in Book I to the internal "spiritual life." It establishes the prerequisites for mystical vision and begins a detailed analysis of the different types of unity—natural and supernatural—between the human soul and God.</meta>

II

<meta>Ink bleed-through from the preceding page is visible in the top margin.</meta>

In order that we may see spiritually, three things are necessary: first, the infusion of the light of grace, which is more excellent [than the active life] and is merited by the diligence of internal fervor. The second is the exclusion of all external forms and of all phantasmagoric tumult <note>original: *phantastici tumultus*; this refers to the distracting "noise" of mental images and sensory memories that clutter the mind during meditation</note>, to such an extent that the mind is free, pure, unhindered, and entirely at rest from all creatures and their forms. The third is the free turning of the will, with a gathering together of all the powers of the soul toward God; and the absolute ascension of that same soul—away from every disordered affection—into the unity of its own mind and into the unity of the divinity. This is so that the rational creature may supernaturally attain and possess the highest unity of the divine nature. For it was for this very reason that God created heaven and earth and all things that are in them. For this reason, He was made man: He taught us by deeds and words and, in a certain way, paved the way for us to this unity itself. He was crucified for us, held not so much by nails of iron as by the nails of love <note>original: *amoris clauis*; a common mystical theme suggesting Christ’s voluntary sacrifice was motivated by his love for humanity</note>, so that He might more justly claim our love for Himself. Then He ascended into heaven and reopened this same unity to us by tearing the veil of the Father’s indignation, so that we might possess eternal happiness within it.

### ¶ ON the threefold natural union in man. Chapter III.

It should be noted, moreover, that there is a threefold natural unity in man, which is nonetheless possessed supernaturally by the elect: namely, the unity of essence, the unity of mind, and the unity of heart. The unity of essence <term>essentia: the fundamental nature or "being" of a person, as distinct from their thoughts or actions</term> is that by which man, along with the rest of creation, is naturally united with God as the primary cause <term>primary cause: the philosophical concept of God as the ultimate source of all existence</term>. For from Him and through Him and in Him are all things. From Him, indeed, they have essentially flowed forth through creation out of nothing <note>original: *creatio ex nichilo*</note>; through Him, they are preserved in being; and in Him, they live as eternal ideas <note>original: *idealiter viuētia*; referring to the Platonic/Augustinian concept that all created things exist first as perfect "ideas" in the mind of God</note> within the mind of the Architect. For indeed...

-> OF THE ADORNING OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS <-

...what was made: in Him was life <note>original: *quod factum est: in ipso vita erat*; a reference to the Gospel of John 1:3-4. Ruusbroec interprets this to mean that everything created exists eternally as a "life" or an idea within God before it is physically manifest.</note>. For indeed, by such a reciprocal necessity (if we may call it that), God and the creature are so mutually inherent to one another that unless God were present in the creature through His **essence**, **presence**, and **power**—as the cause which grants existence—the creature would instantly become nothing. And on the other hand, unless the creature existed, having its life ideally in God, God Himself would surely not be God. Therefore, this unity encompasses every human being **essentially** and **naturally**, whether they be good or evil. Yet it does not make us holy or blessed unless we are reflected back toward the Cause from which we flowed out through grace and **free will**. 

We possess this unity in ourselves in one way, and above ourselves in another, as the principle and preservation of our being. Furthermore, the unity of the **mind** <term>mens: the highest part of the human soul, often distinguished from the lower 'soul' that animates the body</term> is the natural origin of the intellectual powers, from which the <term>spiritus: the spiritual aspect of the person capable of direct communion with God</term> potentially arises as if from a central point. This is our very **essence**, by which we are naturally united with God in that prior unity. But in that higher state, the soul exists as something originated by the First Principle; here below, it exists as the source of its own actions. Yet in both states, it is whole, simple, and one. 

This unity is supreme: it is the intellectual part of the soul placed above the sensory powers and set free from every bodily organ. It is distinguished by three powers—that is, **intellect**, **will**, and **memory** <note>This "triad" of faculties was traditionally seen as the "image of God" within the human soul, mirroring the Trinity.</note>—through which it performs all spiritual actions. Because of this part, the soul is called "spirit." 

Furthermore, the **unity of the heart** is the collection of all sensory powers into the single and, as it were, central organ of the heart: which is indeed the principle and root of all life and sensuality. We do not distinguish this unity from the previous one in order to divide the **essence** of the soul. Rather, because the soul is truly simple and one, and free from all materiality, it is joined to the body as its truest **substantial form** and mover <note>original: *forma substantialis & motor*; a philosophical concept meaning the soul is what makes the body a "human" body and provides its vital energy</note>. Thus, it possesses certain powers that are separate and unmixed...

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that all humans are naturally united to God as their source of existence, but spiritual "blessedness" requires a conscious return to God through grace. He defines the soul's structure, distinguishing between the higher spirit (mind) and the lower animating principle (heart).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the three unities of the human person. II
d

...and free from every physical organ. Through these powers, being similar to its Principle <note>original: *principio*; referring to God as the source or origin from which the soul flows</note>, the soul may acknowledge, love, and remember Him. Because the soul is entirely indivisible and separate through these powers, it may likewise be called **spirit**, being similar to its Principle. Other powers, however, it has attached to bodily organs, suited for governing the body and for other useful functions achieved through physical instruments. Because of these functions, it resides in the principal organ—namely the heart—and is called **soul** <note>original: *anima*; the author notes it is named for "animating" (*animando*) the body</note>. Just as natural life flows from the heart, situated like a fountain near the middle of the body, into the other members, so the soul—the simple and indivisible mover of the body—resides entirely in the center of the heart, directing and infusing its own powers into the movements of each specific organ. These three natural unities, therefore, exist in man as one life and a sort of governance of the soul. In the lower unity, man is sensory and animal-like; in the middle, he is rational and spiritual; and through the third, he is preserved essentially in his being. Generally, all these unities are naturally present in all human beings.

### ¶ ON THE MORAL adornment of these virtues. Chapter IV.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial S with floral motifs and ribbons.</image-desc> Supernaturally, however, these three unities—like a single kingdom divided into three distinct palaces—are inhabited by the elect through grace and are morally decorated by the civic virtues <term>politicis virtutibus: "political" or "civil" virtues, referring to the practical moral conduct of a person living in society according to reason</term> of the active life. But furthermore, they are possessed more worthily by a spiritual indwelling, and are more remarkably adorned by the exercise of internal fervor. The lowest unity, namely that of the heart, is inhabited supernaturally and adorned by the external exercise of moral virtues according to the pattern of CHRIST and the examples of the holy fathers, by the voluntary bearing of the cross of CHRIST, and by the subjection of nature under the Lord's precepts, under the mandates of the Church, and under the institutions and do- <note>The text breaks off here; likely "doctrines" or "teachings" (doctrinis).</note>

...[by teachings] according to natural health and the rule of discretion <note>original: *discretionis canonem*; the principle of using sound judgment and moderation</note> with restraint. 

The second unity, namely that of the mind or spirit, is inhabited supernaturally: and it is marked by the three **theological virtues**—namely **faith**, **hope**, and **charity**—through the influence of grace and God's gifts, and by a noble benevolence of mind in following **CHRIST** and the Christian religion of the Fathers in all virtues. 

Consequently, the third and supreme unity is that of our essence. This unity in a certain way exceeds the grasp of our intellect, yet in a certain way it exists essentially within us. It is possessed by us supernaturally when, in all our diligent actions, we intend the praise and honor of God, and when—transcending ourselves, our own intentions, and all created things—we find rest in Him. This is that unity from which we have naturally emanated in the flow of creation, in which we always essentially remain, and to which we return <note>original: *repedamus*; literally "to step back" or "retreat," suggesting a homecoming</note> with the steps of love. This, then, is the adornment of moral virtues, decorating the three aforementioned unities in the **active life** <note>original: *activa vita*; the life of external good works and moral discipline</note>.

...working <note>original: *operans*; continuing from the previous page's description of a person engaged in the active life</note>, not as an intrusive demander, nor as an anxious sigher. For in this way, one is disposed and prepared for the reception of the **spiritual and affective life** <note>original: *vitae spiritualis & affectivae*; the "affective life" refers to the life of the heart's desires and emotions directed toward God</note>. For the precious liquid is poured into a prepared vessel. No vessel is more worthy than the soul, solid with the gold of charity, and adorned with the precious stones of all the virtues. Nor is there any liquid more useful than the grace of God. Therefore, let him offer himself with all that is his before the divine sight with a simple, upright intention; and in that sublime unity in which God and every spirit that loves God supernaturally are one without intermediary <note>original: *sine medio*; an important mystical concept meaning a direct union with God without anything standing in between</note>: let him rest above himself and all things. For from this unity and grace, the gifts of grace flow out, and the cry of **CHRIST** crying out is heard: “Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him.”

Thus **CHRIST**, the light of eternal truth, provokes us to see, saying: “Behold.” Since through Him—as He is the splendor of the Father's light—the eyes of our mind are illuminated. And just as without Him nothing was made, so without Him nothing is entirely luminous. Moreover, the cry of **CHRIST** by which He cries out spiritually within us is nothing other than the infusion of His grace and His spiritual light. Indeed, the rush of this river gladdens the city of the unity of our mind or spirit: from which the supreme powers flow out potentially toward all virtues, made habitual through grace, and into which they flow back again, carefully preserving the unity of the spirit in the bond of peace and charity. 

In this unity consists virtue, the beginning and the end of every created work, both naturally and supernaturally: insofar as it is enacted as a creature by natural power through grace and celestial gifts. For to this end God pours His grace into the mind—that is, into the unity of the supreme powers—so that with the natural powers distinguished by the habits of virtues and excited by grace, man may always be vigilant in diligent works. For indeed He bestows grace for the sake of the work; but He grants Himself, truly above all gifts of grace, for the sake of peaceful enjoyment <note>original: *fruitionis quietisque*; the state of resting in God’s presence</note>. There is therefore for us a uni- <meta>catchword: dij (likely referring to *dei* or *dignitas* on the following page)</meta>
