...purity <note>original: *-tas*; completing the word from the previous page, likely *puritas* (purity) or *unitas* (unity)</note> of spirit: a mansion of peace, opulent with the riches of divine charity. In this mansion, the entire multitude of virtues gathers itself, commending its life and health to such a great asylum. **Grace**, however, is an internal stimulus of the **Holy Spirit**, exercising and impelling our spirit inwardly toward every virtue. It flows from within and not from without, because God is more intimate to us than we are to ourselves. His internal instinct and operation—whether natural or supernatural—is closer and more intimate to us than our own action. For this reason, the process of divine operation in us moves from the innermost parts to the exterior; however, the operation of creatures <note>meaning anything created, such as people or physical objects</note> works in the opposite way. Because of this (as we have said), grace and divine inspiration do not flow into our imagination from without through sensible species <note>original: *sensibilibus speciebus*; in medieval philosophy, "species" are the mental images or impressions received through the five senses from the physical world</note>, but are infused from within into the unity of our mind.

## ¶ Concerning the three things causing spiritual vision. Chapter VI.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring leafy vines and floral motifs.</image-desc> Therefore **CHRIST** cries out spiritually in the man of desires <note>original: *viro desyderiorũ*; a person whose heart is focused intensely on seeking God</note>: "Await a new grace," and He exhorts him so that he may see, saying: "**Behold.**" Three things (as we said before) cause a spiritual vision for internal exercise in a man. And the first indeed (as we said) is the **illumination of grace**. Moreover, grace in the soul is like a candle in a glass vessel or a transparent lamp. For like that lamp, it warms, illuminates, and shines through the vessel itself—that is, the just man—manifesting itself inwardly to its inhabitant, provided he is an attentive observer of himself. And shining out through external works, he may shine for his neighbors through virtues and examples. This very illumination of grace suddenly moves and excites the man inwardly. And that sudden motion is the first thing that brings about spiritual vision. To this motion, the second part on the man’s side quickly follows: a gathering <note>original: *collectio*; often translated as "recollection," the act of pulling one's scattered thoughts and senses inward to focus on the soul</note>

Let no foreign care solicit him. Let the affection for no creature detain him. Thus, these three things, wherever they meet, cause spiritual vision; they are the beginning and foundation of the whole internal exercise and affective life, fulfilling the first point of the cry: "Behold."

## ¶ Concerning the three spiritual comings of CHRIST. Chapter VII.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap letter 'N' featuring a woodcut illustration of a garden scene with flowers and trees within the frame of the letter.</image-desc> Nevertheless, the clarity of the eyes would profit nothing or very little, nor would the sharpness of sight delight the vision, if a delectable and lovable object were lacking. **CHRIST** therefore, who by His grace prepares the eyes of the mind for seeing, also immediately shows what they ought to see—namely, His internal coming—saying: "The bridegroom comes." It must certainly be noted that three principal comings are found in the spiritual man who exercises himself in inward devotion. Each of these raises him to a higher stage of spiritual life and gives him a more worthy subject for his spiritual exercise. And the first coming indeed moves, excites, and stimulates the man interiorly through his senses; it draws and raises him upward with all his heart and all his strength, demanding that with God he love...

<header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...to make a perfect unity. This is the stimulus, this is the drawing in the heart: namely, it is felt in the unity of the sensitive powers <note>the parts of the soul related to feelings and senses</note>, and especially in the concupiscible power <term>concupiscible power: the faculty of the soul that desires what is perceived as good or beautiful</term>. Since the business of this coming is transacted in the lower part of man, before one tends toward higher things, it is necessary to be cleansed and adorned, and—with the little fire of charity <note>original: *charitatis igniculo*; the spark of divine love</note> placed beneath—to be driven upward. This internal stimulus of divine visitation brings and takes away; it makes one poor and enriches; it humbles and exalts; it instills delights as well as sorrows; it makes one hope and despair; it strikes with heat and cold. These are utterly unspeakable contraries which happen here in alternating turns. This coming, with its business, is divided into four modes (as we shall explain later), exceeding each other by degrees, by which the lower part of man is decorated through the internal exercise of the spiritual life.

The **second coming** of the Bridegroom, CHRIST, is of a more sublime dignity, impressing in a certain way the likeness of Himself: flashing with a fuller grace and more radiant clarity, an opulent influence of divine gifts is perfected in the superior powers of the soul, stabilizing and clarifying the spirit, and enriching it in many ways. This influence of the divine coming demands an outflow <note>the soul acting upon its gifts through works</note>: and with all the infused riches, it seeks a reflux <note>original: *refluxū*; the soul returning its gifts to God in a cycle of love</note> into its own origin. In this coming, God reveals many wonders: but He demands back all the gifts bestowed upon the soul with interest <note>original: *vſura*</note>, requiring even more than any creature could pay, wishing to reap what He did not sow, and to take what He did not put down. The degree and character of this coming and its business is more sublime than the former, and by it the supreme powers of the soul are more remarkably arranged.

The **third coming** of our Bridegroom is truly spiritual and inward, accomplished by a certain divine touch in the unity of the spirit's essence: in which indeed the supreme powers are essentially present, and from which they potentially flow out, and into which again—their business being settled—they are reflected. And always there, whichever way they have proceeded, both because of the bond of charity and because of the unity of the spirit's essence, they remain essential[ly]...

...essentially united. This coming causes the highest and most intimate degree of spiritual life; and by it, the essential unity of the spirit is distinguished in many ways. In each of his spiritual comings, however, CHRIST demands a specific "going out" <note>original: *exitum*; here referring to the soul’s active response and external works in response to internal grace</note> corresponding to his arrival. Therefore, in each of his spiritual comings, he thunders in our hearts, saying: "Go out!"—namely, by living and exercising yourselves spiritually, according to the manner in which my grace and my gifts stimulate and drive you. For if we are to become perfect, it is necessary that according to the way the Holy Spirit moves, draws, pours into, and touches us, we also must hasten to go out and progress with the steps of internal exercise. But if we presume to kick back against <note>original: *recalcitrare*; a metaphor for a horse resisting a stimulus by kicking</note> the stimulation of the Holy Spirit through the unlikeness of our lives, dulling the stimulus itself, we will feel it very little; and being empty of virtues, we will waste away in the sluggishness of our own torpor. These, therefore, are the three spiritual comings of the Bridegroom—involving internal exercise, as we have said—each seeking its own outflow. These we now propose to explain and declare individually and immediately, by the Lord's granting of grace. Therefore, pay close attention, each of you. For it is difficult (as I believe) for anyone to understand well who has never tasted any of these things with an internal palate <note>original: *gustu*; a spiritual "tasting" or experiential knowledge of God</note>.

### ON THE FIRST degree of the first spiritual coming of CHRIST. Chapter VIII.

<image-desc>Large decorative initial 'P' featuring interlaced foliate and vine ornamentation within a square frame.</image-desc>
The **first spiritual coming** of CHRIST, then, which we noted in the preceding chapter would be fourfold, is an internal, sensible instinct and a stimulus of the Holy Spirit, driving us toward all the exercises of the virtues. We wish to compare this coming to the illumination of the sun and its effect upon the earth. For just as the rising sun, with instantaneous motion, illuminates the whole world all the way to the west, and refreshes all things with the life-giving heat of its reflected rays; so indeed the eternal sun, CHRIST, inhabiting the highest part of our spirit...

<meta>This page continues the exposition of Christ’s first spiritual coming, specifically focusing on how divine grace affects different "regions" of the soul, using the metaphor of the sun's rays on mountains versus the lowlands.</meta>

<header>THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS</header>

...the **lower part**, namely the heart, with all the sensitive powers fundamentally joined to it, He illuminates and kindles by the influx of His visitation with a momentary movement. For the grace of the Holy Spirit knows no slow efforts <note>original: *tarda molimina nescit*; a phrase often attributed to St. Ambrose, suggesting that divine grace acts instantaneously rather than through gradual human labor</note>. However, it is necessary that he who desires such things to happen to him should have healthy and open intellectual eyes for seeing.

Indeed, just as the **Sun** in the higher regions illuminates the mountains with the rays of its light, hastens the summer, and ripens the primary fruits—generating a strong wine that gladdens the hearts of the inhabitants with an intoxicating joy—so too does this same sun share the rays of its light with the furthest ends of the earth. And although, because of the slant of the rays, it grows warm with more difficulty, and for this reason seems barren for the growth of vines, it is nevertheless known to abound in many good and useful fruits.

In the same way, those dwelling in the **lower part** of their soul near the "theater of the senses," yet who are not lacking the grace of God, good intentions, upright morals, and praiseworthy exercise, certainly bring forth many various good fruits of virtue; but they are gladdened little, or not at all, by the wine of internal joy and spiritual consolation. Therefore, whoever wishes to abound in wine and desires to fertilize his land with perpendicular <note>meaning direct and intense, as when the sun is directly overhead</note> rays, must necessarily see to it that he dwells in the **mountains** with the gathering of all his powers, suspended in God with his whole heart, free and unencumbered by the passions of the soul and all created things.

For there, **CHRIST**, the **Sun of Justice** <note>original: *sol iustitiæ*; a common title for Christ derived from Malachi 4:2</note>, illuminates hearts that have been so exalted; and these are the "spiritual mountains" which we have compared to visible ones. In the first stage of his first spiritual coming, therefore, **CHRIST**—the Sun of Justice and the splendor of divine clarity—illuminates and kindles the heart that is free and detached from all things, along with all the powers of the soul at once, through the power of his Spirit.

For just as fire, by its natural and inherent quality, immediately introduces a fiery heat into material that is properly prepared for it, so too does **CHRIST**, a consuming fire <note>original: *ignis consumens*; a reference to Deuteronomy 4:24 and Hebrews 12:29</note>...

It marks the beginning of the detailed description of Christ's first spiritual coming as the Sun of Justice.</summary>

### ¶ ON UNITY, compunction, affection, and devotion of the heart. Chapter IX.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'A' featuring interlaced vine-stem or strapwork patterns on a dark, cross-hatched background.</image-desc>Now, once the small fire of charity has been kindled in the heart, soon all the <term>sensitive powers<note>original: *sensitiuae vires*; the faculties of the soul related to the five senses and physical feeling, as opposed to the higher intellectual or "rational" powers.</note></term>, running back together toward their own origin, celebrate the **unity** of their heart. No one can truly possess this unity unless the Holy Spirit, dwelling most inward to the heart, has kindled the fire of His love. For since fire is most active, it has the property of uniting and assimilating to itself all things over which it gains a full victory. 

**Unity of the heart**, therefore, is the gathering of all the sensitive powers into the one dwelling-place of the heart; there the soul, as if reclining on a royal bed, rests sweetly in the peace of the heart. Unity of the heart is also a certain bond, gathering and embracing the body and the soul, the heart and the senses, together with the other powers into one by the bond of **charity**. 

From this unity of the heart, <term>compunction<note>original: *compunctio*; literally a "pricking" or "stinging." While often used for remorse for sin, the author here describes a "compunction of love"—a joyful piercing of the heart by divine grace.</note></term> is generated—not of pain, indeed, but of love. No one perfectly attains this unless they have been entirely recollected within themselves. For when the soul is gathered into the heart's primary dwelling and its most "passive" organ <note>meaning the organ most receptive to external and divine influence</note>, along with all its <note>original: *suburbanis agricolis*; "suburban farmers." A colorful metaphor describing the five senses as laborers who live in the outskirts (the body) and bring their harvest back to the central city (the heart).</note> **suburban farmers** (namely, the sensitive powers), it attends to the operation of the divine visitation within itself. While it remains thus attentive, it is stimulated and pricked within by the fire of the Holy Spirit; it is pierced by love and burns in all its vital parts. 

The **compunction of love**, by which the heart is primarily stimulated, is a certain internal ardor of divine love, kindled by the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, burning the innermost parts of the heart. This compunction of love stimulates, agitates, and impels the person within themselves, even while the person remains entirely ignorant of what has happened to them or the cause of the event. From this compunction, an **affection of love** soon arises, penetrating and piercing the heart and the appetitive power <note>the faculty of the soul that desires or "reaches out" for things</note>. By this affection—

<meta>This page continues the exposition on the "mountains of the spirit" from Chapter IX, specifically detailing how the light of Christ acts differently upon the soul depending on its spiritual elevation and focus.</meta>

->DEOR. NVP. SP.<-

...illuminates and kindles the lower part, namely the heart, with all the <term>sensitive powers<note>original: *sensitivis potentiis*; the faculties of the soul related to physical sensation and emotion.</note></term> radically joined to it, by the influx of His visitation in a momentary motion. For the grace of the Holy Spirit knows no slow efforts <note>original: *tarda molimina nescit*; a traditional theological phrase indicating that divine grace transforms the soul instantly rather than through gradual labor.</note>. Nevertheless, it is necessary that he who desires such things to happen to him has healthy and open intellectual eyes for seeing. For just as the sun, traversing the mountains in the higher regions with the rays of its light, hastens the season and ripens the primary fruits, and generating strong wine, gladdens the hearts of the inhabitants with a drunken joy. The same sun also shares the rays of its light with the furthest ends of the earth; and although it may heat more weakly there due to the slant of the rays, and for this reason seems barren toward certain generations, it is nonetheless known to abound in many good and useful fruits. 

So too, those who dwell in the lower part of their soul, near the **theater of the senses**, yet are not lacking the grace of God—having good intention, upright character, and praiseworthy practice—certainly bring forth many diverse good fruits of the virtues. But they are gladdened only a little or not at all by the wine of internal joy and spiritual consolation. Therefore, whoever wishes to abound in wine and desires to fertilize their land with perpendicular rays, must see to it that they dwell in the mountains with a gathering of all their powers, suspended in God with their whole heart, free and unencumbered from the passions of the soul and all creatures. 

For there, **CHRIST**, the <term>sun of justice<note>original: *sol iustitiae*; a messianic title from Malachi 4:2, frequently applied to Christ in mystical literature.</note></term>, illuminates hearts so exalted; and these are the spiritual mountains we have compared with visible things. Therefore, in the first step of His first spiritual advent, **CHRIST**, the sun of justice and the splendor of divine clarity, illuminates and kindles the heart that is free and released from all things, as well as all the soul's powers at once, through the virtue of His Spirit. For just as fire, by its natural and inherent quality, soon introduces fiery heat into material suited to it in every way, so surely **CHRIST**, the consuming fire...

II

...their free hearts, having been raised into the mountains of the spirit, He approaches with the flame-spewing fire of His internal visitation and says: "Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out immediately," <note>original: *ecce sponsus venit exite subito*; a reference to the Parable of the Ten Virgins in Matthew 25:6.</note> in a manner fitting for the one who is coming.

### ¶ ON UNITY, compunction, affection, and 
### devotion of the heart. Chapter IX.

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut initial 'A' decorated with complex interlacing strapwork or celtic-style knotwork patterns.</image-desc>

**A**nd so, once the heart has been set on fire by the little spark of charity, soon all the <term>sensitive powers<note>original: *sensitive vires*; the faculties of the soul that deal with physical sensation, emotion, and outward perception.</note></term> rush back to their origin and celebrate the unity of their heart. No one can truly possess this unity unless the Holy Spirit, who is most intimate to the heart, approaches with the fire of His love. For since fire is the most active of elements, it has the power to unite and assimilate to itself all things over which it gains a full victory. 

Therefore, the unity of the heart is the gathering of all the sensitive powers into the one dwelling-place of the heart; there the soul, reclining as if on a royal bed, rests sweetly in the peace of the heart. This unity of the heart is also a certain bond, collecting and embracing the body and soul, the heart and the senses, with the other powers into one by the chain of charity. 

From this unity of the heart, <term>compunction<note>original: *compunctio*; while often meaning "remorse" today, in this mystical context it refers to a "piercing" or "stinging" of the soul by divine love.</note></term> is generated—not a compunction of pain, indeed, but of love. No one perfectly attains this unless he has been entirely recollected within himself. For the soul is gathered into the dwelling of the heart—its primary and most receptive organ—along with all its "suburban farmers" (that is, the sensitive powers). As the soul attends to the operation of the divine visitation within itself, and while it is stimulated and pricked by the fire of the Holy Spirit through its very attention, it is moved to love and burns in all its vital parts. 

The compunction of love by which the heart is first stimulated is a certain internal heat of divine love, kindled by the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, burning the innermost parts of the heart. This compunction of love stimulates, agitates, and impels a person within themselves, even while they are entirely ignorant of what has happened or the cause of the event. From this compunction, an affection of love soon arises, penetrating and piercing the heart and the <term>appetitive power<note>original: *appetitivam vim*; the faculty of the soul that desires or "hungers" for a perceived good.</note></term>. With this affection...

# ¶ CONCERNING UNITY, compunction, affection, and devotion of the heart. Chapter IX.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' featuring intricate floral and strapwork patterns within a square frame, beginning the word 'Accenso'.</image-desc>

Moreover, when the heart has been ignited by the small flame of charity, all the <term>sensitive powers<note>original: *sensitivæ vires*; the faculties of the soul associated with physical sensation, emotion, and the basic drive to react to the world.</note></term> immediately run together toward their origin and celebrate the unity of their heart. No one can truly possess this unity unless the Holy Spirit, who is most intimate to the heart, has kindled the fire of His love within it. For since fire is the most active of elements, it has the power to unite and assimilate all things to itself in which it has gained a complete victory. 

Therefore, the unity of the heart is the gathering of all the sensitive powers into the single dwelling-place of the heart, where the soul, reclining as if upon a royal bed, rests sweetly in the peace of the heart. This unity of the heart is also a certain bond that gathers and embraces the body and soul, the heart and the senses, along with all other powers, into one through the bond of <term>charity<note>original: *charitas*; here referring to the highest form of divine love.</note></term>. 

From this unity of the heart, <term>compunction<note>original: *compunctio*; literally a "pricking" or "stinging." While often used for remorse, the author here describes a "compunction of love"—a joyful piercing of the heart by God.</note></term> is generated—not a compunction of sorrow, indeed, but of love. No one perfectly attains this unless he has been entirely recollected within himself. For the soul, having been gathered into the heart—its primary dwelling and its most receptive organ—together with all its "suburban farmers" (that is, the sensitive powers), watches the operation of the divine visitation within itself. While the soul attends to this, it is stimulated and pricked within by the fire of the Holy Spirit; it is pierced by love and catches fire in all its vital parts. 

This compunction of love, by which the heart is first stimulated, is a certain internal ardor of divine love, kindled by the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, burning in the innermost depths of the heart. This compunction of love stimulates, agitates, and impels a person within themselves, while the individual remains entirely ignorant of what has happened to them or the cause of this occurrence. From this compunction, a certain <term>affection<note>original: *affectio*; a deep emotional disposition or state of being moved by God.</note></term> of love soon arises, penetrating and piercing the heart and the <term>appetitive power<note>original: *appetitivam vim*; the faculty of the soul that desires and moves toward what it perceives as good.</note></term>. By this affection—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "unity of the heart." The previous page ended with the description of the heart as a bond that gathers and embraces the inward powers of the soul. This page defines the states of affection, devotion, and gratitude that follow this unity.</meta>

->THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-

...the heart with spiritual savor; truly, he lacks this who has not been gathered within himself and <term>compunct<note>original: *compunctus*; from "compunction," a piercing of the heart by either sorrow for sin or an intense longing for God.</note></term> by the intimate love of the heart. Now, the **affection of love** is a savory appetite of the heart directed toward God as toward the highest good, containing all that is good. This affection of love bids farewell to all creatures; not in such a way that it fails to supply the needs of necessity through their use, but so that it does not foster the lust of sensuality through their abuse. The affection of love feels itself touched inwardly by eternal love, to which it desires to devote itself above all things. The affection of love easily despises all things so that it may attain what it loves.

From this loving affection arises the **devotion of the heart**: a lavish flame of divine praise and honor. No one kindles this flame unless he has been scorched by the intimate affection of love. **Devotion** is the inflammation of the inward appetite toward heavenly things, with the fire of loving affection burning even more intensely. Devotion makes one fervent in the service of God, exciting and kindling the soul both inwardly and outwardly. Devotion makes the body and soul flourish, rendering them venerable in both divine and human sight. God demands and requires the devotion of the heart from us in all our ministries. Finally, devotion directs us in the way of eternal salvation and, by a straight path, carries our flame-vomiting <note>original: *flamivomum*; a vivid medieval term for an appetite or desire that is so hot it "spews" or "breathes" fire.</note> appetite into the highest good.

### ¶ Concerning gratitude and its twofold sorrow. Chapter X.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring a central flower rosette and stylized foliage within the bowl of the letter. The square initial block has a detailed background of fine lines.</image-desc>

Furthermore, the devotion of a heart kindled by the heat of loving compunction and affection is like the flame of the altar of incense, in which the sweet-smelling incense of **gratitude** is burned; for this incense does not smell sweet in a "foreign fire" <note>This refers to the biblical "strange fire" offered by Nadab and Abihu in Leviticus 10, suggesting that true gratitude must come from a heart genuinely ignited by God, not by worldly motives.</note>. Indeed, gratitude gathers the spices of all divine benefits and works into the small mortar of compunction, crushes them with the pestle of affection, and, placing the powders mixed with thanksgiving upon the flame of devotion, offers a sweet-smelling incense of praise and reverence to God. Are not the spices of good odor

<meta>This page continues the sequence of spiritual development following the "unity of the heart," moving from the inward fire of devotion to the outward expression of gratitude and praise. It uses the metaphor of aromatic spices to describe the various blessings of God which, when prepared by the soul, become a fragrant offering.</meta>

...our creation from nothing into a rational life; the creation of all creatures; and even the assignment of angels to serve us. It includes the heavenly instruction of our words and character; the Passion <note>original: *passio*; referring to the suffering and death of Christ.</note> marked by many aromatic stages; the promise of eternal life in the heavens; the full remission of sins; the infusion of grace and charity; His eternal indwelling within us; and His sustenance through the sacraments in every necessity of our current weakness. It further includes the administration of His precious body and blood for our spiritual nourishment, provided according to the desire of each person.

Are these things not, moreover, spices of a good scent? They include the presentation of nature, scripture, and every creature as if in a mirror; the gift of health and strength, and sometimes—for our benefit—the gift of sickness; the creation of peace within us, and our entitlement to the Christian name. 

**Compunction** <note>The "piercing" of the heart by sorrow or longing.</note> gathers these spices; **affection** grinds them; **devotion** sets them on fire; and **gratitude** offers them to the divine "sense of smell" as a fragrant odor of praise. Truly, God is to be praised with all one's strength. To praise God is this: to present honor and reverence to the Divine Majesty in every moment of our lives. 

To praise God is the most proper work of the angels and saints in our **homeland** <term>homeland<note>original: *patria*; a common medieval term for Heaven, the soul's true home.</note></term> and of those who love God here on the **way** <term>way<note>original: *via*; the earthly journey or pilgrimage toward God.</note></term>. God is to be praised with the heart’s affection, with uplifted powers, with words and works, with body and soul, and even with earthly wealth by humbly performing works of piety. Whoever neglects to praise God here will be silent forever. No work is more delightful or joyful to a loving heart than to praise God. A heart full of divine praise desires all creatures to join in praise with it. There is no end to divine praise, for in it consists the state of our **bliss** <note>original: *beatitudo*; the ultimate happiness of union with God.</note>. Let us praise and highly exalt Him forever!

From such devout gratitude, however, a twin sorrow is born in the heart, and a double pain in one's desire. One sorrow arises from the deficiency and incompleteness of our divine praise, honor, thanksgiving, and the service we owe. The other sorrow is for our lack of growth in the virtues; for we do not progress and increase in charity, virtue, and character as we should, so that we might be rendered in some measure worthy to praise, honor, thank, and serve Him (as reason teaches us). These two sorro...

->OF THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE<-

[These] things are the root and fruit, the beginning and the end of all the virtues of the spiritual life. Indeed, the beginning is the sorrow felt for the lack and imperfection of virtues and divine praise: this is the highest work in this first stage of the first advent of the internal visitation <note>original: *visitationis internae*; refers to the experience of God "visiting" the soul through grace.</note>, and through it, the business of this stage is most fully completed.

### ¶ Two examples showing the exercise of this stage, Chapter XI

<image-desc>A large, square decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring an intricate foliate design with scrolled vines and leaf motifs.</image-desc>
To demonstrate the form of exercise for this kind of stage, let us propose two examples from nature. A pot <term>pot<note>original: *cacabus*; a large cooking vessel or cauldron.</note></term> full of any liquid, when the heat of a fire comes upon it, gushes and boils over; and when the bubbling has risen to the top of the pot, the liquid suddenly falls back into the depths, only to erupt again in a new bubbling. In this way, just as the fire always agitates and drives the liquid, and the liquid always gushes and boils: so the internal fire of the Holy Spirit agitates, rouses, and raises the heart and all the powers of the soul to a gushing <note>original: *scaturigo*; a term for a spring or fountain, used here to describe an outpouring of praise.</note> of divine praise in that way (which we mentioned above). Then, falling back again into its own hearth, warmed by the fire of the Holy Spirit, it is lifted up again in the same way by a new boiling. Thus the fire of love always burns in the hearth of the heart, and the heart always overflows in divine praises and thanksgivings through words and deeds. Supported always by humility, the soul considers whatever it has done as little, compared to those things which it judges it ought to do by duty, or what it desires to perform beyond what is required <term>beyond what is required<note>original: *supererogare*; to do more than is strictly necessary or commanded by law, coming from a place of excessive love.</note></term> with daily sighs. 

Again, in the springtime, as the sun ascends toward us and summer is already playing at the gates: by the force of the solar heat penetrating the depths of the earth, the moisture is raised from the roots through the trunk into the branches, and by heavenly power, they produce leaves and flowers everywhere on the trees. In a similar way indeed, as the eternal sun, CHRIST, ascends in our hearts, and the flowering of virtues...

<summary>This page uses two vivid natural metaphors—a boiling cauldron and the rising of sap in spring—to describe how the Holy Spirit drives the soul to constant acts of praise and humble devotion. It emphasizes that true spiritual progress involves a cycle of "boiling over" with love for God and then returning to a state of humble self-reflection.</summary>
<keywords>Holy Spirit, divine praise, humility, metaphors of nature, boiling pot, springtime, Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual exercise</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the first stage of the "first advent" of Christ—the sensitive or sensory union—and transitions into the second stage, which Ruusbroec characterizes as the infusion of spiritual sweetness. ->II<-

announcing that the blooming summer of the spiritual life is at hand: he pours the rays and heat of his visitation <note>original: *visitationis*; the theological concept of God "visiting" or making His presence felt in the soul.</note> into our affections; and gathering and uniting our hearts from the lowest multitude of earthly things within themselves, he pierces them with the heat of love, and makes them leafy with affection and fruitful with gratitude. Fruitful, I say, with early-ripening fruits: which are stored away, gathered together in the cellar of humility—which sorrow for the defect and negligence of life has dug for itself—for the table of the banquet of eternal happiness. In that place, they shall happily eat the labors of their hands. Let these things be said concerning the first stage of divine visitation, by which the sensitive part of the soul is united.

### OF the second stage of the first spiritual advent of CHRIST: in which the sweetness of the heart is infused. Chapter XII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring intricate floral and foliage patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

Since indeed we compared the first spiritual advent of CHRIST, which ascends by four stages, to the illumination of the sun in the discussion of the first stage: now, for easier understanding, let us follow the remaining stages (with similar comparisons) according to the half-yearly course of the sun. In the first stage, therefore, refreshed by the spring-like pleasantness, we smelled the abundance of the coming summer: now let us approach with a hungry heart to taste the sweetness of the fruits. Therefore, when the sun, through its daily revolutions, has passed through March and April, and ascends toward us through the time of May, giving a beginning to summer: the power and efficacy of the sun is doubled in the things growing from the earth, both because of the disposition of the sun as it is received <note>original: *patientis solis*; a scholastic term referring to the object (the earth/soul) receiving the influence of the sun.</note> and because of the approach of the acting sun <note>original: *agentis solis*; the sun as the active source of heat and light.</note>. At that time, while the kindness of the heavens reigns and favors the opportuneness of the season: the humors of the earth are raised into the air by the heat, dew and rain are produced, and the fruits of the earth are multiplied by continuous growth. In this manner, when the light-flowing <note>original: *lucifluo*; flowing with light.</note> sun, JESUS CHRIST, is exalted above all things in our hearts, and [our] natural [powers...]

<summary>Ruusbroec concludes the first stage of spiritual union, describing how humility and sorrow for one's failings prepare the soul for eternal happiness. He then transitions to the second stage of Christ's "advent," using the metaphor of the sun's strengthening heat in May to describe the increasing intensity of divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual summer, cellar of humility, divine visitation, solar metaphor, Jesus Christ, spiritual growth, May, sweetness of heart</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "second stage" of the first spiritual advent of Christ—the infusion of sweetness. Ruusbroec uses the metaphor of the height of summer to describe the soul's growth and its transition toward spiritual drunkenness.</meta>

...with the desires contrary to the spirit having been restrained by the moderation of discernment <note>original: *discretionis*; the spiritual gift of "discretion" or "discernment" (Latin: *discretio*) which allows a soul to distinguish between different spiritual impulses.</note>, and the virtues being possessed according to the form of the previous stage; and when the richness <note>original: *adipibus*, literally "the fat." In the context of biblical sacrifice, the fat was considered the choicest part offered to God; here it refers to the spiritual delight or "flavor" of the virtues.</note> of the virtues—that is, their flavors and delights—has been offered as a sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving through the fire of love: then, as the spiritual summer begins, a new rain of eternal consolation and divine sweetness, a heavenly dew, distills, soaking the earth of the heart and (unless there is some obstacle on the heart's part) causing it to flourish with a double increase of virtues. This is a new coming of the spiritual visitation of CHRIST, and a fresh operation of the Holy Spirit, lifting the soul to a higher level of internal exercise. CHRIST also cries out in this coming of his, saying: "Go forth <note>original: *exite*; a central theme in Ruusbroec's work is the soul's response to God's call to 'go out' of itself to meet the Bridegroom.</note> with the steps of spiritual exercise, according to the requirements of this, my coming." Indeed, once the sweetness of this consolation is tasted: suddenly the heart and all the sensory powers are drenched in such a torrent of internal pleasure that a person considers themselves completely wrapped in the divine embraces of love. These delights are greater than all the pleasures of the world, even if one creature could experience them all at once. In this infusion of pleasure, God likewise glides into the heart through his gifts—into a heart so affected—bringing with him a flavor of such sweetness and a consolation of such joy that he makes the heart itself overflow externally with sweet-flowing pleasure. The abundance of this pleasure suggests to the thoughts of the heart how many calamities of death occupy those who do not love. This pleasure causes a certain "overflow" of the heart, so that a person cannot contain themselves for the richness of their joy.

# II

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'H' containing a scene of a figure kneeling in a landscape with a building in the distance.</image-desc>

**H**owever, this pleasure, when drunk by a thirsty heart, gives birth to a **spiritual drunkenness**. For spiritual drunkenness is truly a richer reception of flavor and internal pleasure in the heart’s affection—richer, I say, than the heart itself can digest or than desire can long for. 

Spiritual drunkenness flows forth and overflows in various spiritual "drunkards" <note>original: *ebrijs*; those intoxicated by the Spirit.</note> through many kinds of bodily gestures. Some, indeed, reveal the fullness of their joy through songs and divine praises. Some express the abundance of sweetness through overflowing tears. In others, such an uncontrollable tingling <note>original: *titillatio*; a physical sensation of spiritual excitement or "itching" common in mystical literature.</note> takes hold of their limbs that it forces them to either run, or leap, or dance with their feet, or clap with their hands. This one shows the fullness of his heart with shouts; that one melts in the pleasure of sweetness in silence and within himself. This man thinks everyone feels what he feels; that one considers no one else to abound in such great delights. 

Sometimes, a person in this state believes he cannot lose—or is about to lose—the abundance of pleasure he feels. Sometimes he wonders why everyone is not converted to the Lord <note>original: *dominum*</note>, so that they might be intoxicated by such great delights of internal sweetness. Occasionally, he thinks God attends to him alone and passes over others, leaving them empty of such sweetness. At other times, he is stunned at such a great miracle <note>original: *miraculum*</note> within himself, wondering what it is that he feels, where it comes from, or where it is going, or what has happened to him. 

This is the most delightful life, according to the heart’s affection, that can be had by a human being situated in this earthly dwelling. But sometimes our little drunkard <note>original: *ebriosulus*; a diminutive used affectionately to describe the soul overwhelmed by God.</note> is filled with such an abundance of delights from the influx of divine sweetness that his heart—like a vessel full of new wine <note>original: *musto*; unfermented wine, often associated with the "new wine" of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost.</note> without a vent—threatens a sudden rupture. Lest the small cask <note>original: *doliolum*</note> of his heart should burst, let him open for himself the flame-spewing...

<summary>This page describes "spiritual drunkenness," a mystical state where the heart is so overwhelmed by divine sweetness that it overflows into physical expressions like dancing, weeping, or singing. Ruusbroec explains how this intense experience can make the soul feel uniquely chosen by God or fear the loss of such a profound connection.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual drunkenness, divine sweetness, mystical ecstasy, Jan van Ruusbroec, interior life, religious experience, heart's affection, new wine</keywords>

OF THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL NUPTIALS <note>original: *DEOR.NVP.SP.*, an abbreviation for *De Ornatu Spiritualium Nuptiarum*.</note>

the sharpness of devotion, the breath of praise and thanksgiving <note>original: *gratiarum actionis*</note>; and, sighing with all the heart's humility, let him say: "I confess, Lord, that you are indeed able to do all things and to breathe where you will <note>original: *spirare vbi vis*; a reference to John 3:8, describing the Spirit moving freely like the wind.</note>. To each individual, you divide the sweetness of your goodness just as you wish. Yet, I thank the honey-flowing goodness of your unsearchable will with all the intention of my heart (as much as I am able), because you anticipate me—so degenerate and crippled a little servant—with the blessings of your sweetness. For if a service of a thousand years were to follow, it would be outweighed by the weight <note>original: *bamento*, likely a contraction of *libramento* (weight/balance) or *momento* (moment).</note> of such sweetness alone. I am clearly unworthy and I am nothing; you have seen what you have done. Give, however, Lord, what you give; infuse what you infuse. But let you not have given your gifts in vain: follow them with the favor of your goodness, so that you may preserve what is given, make what is preserved sprout, and bring forth the fruit of eternal goodness." By this kind of humility, the vineyard of the Lord of Sabaoth <note>original: *vinea dñi sabaoth*; "Sabaoth" is a Hebrew title for God meaning "of Hosts" or "of Armies."</note>—namely, the intoxicated heart—is fed more richly, so that it may bear the abundant clusters of higher virtues.

### ¶ How some are tempted in this drunkenness. Chapter 12.

<image-desc>A large ornamental historiated initial "V" containing an intricate design of scrolling vines or floral patterns.</image-desc>But because this abundance of delights is granted to some at the beginning of their conversion—those who have turned with an undivided will and who despise the pleasures of the world with their whole heart so that they might be free for God alone—yet they are still tender in their strength. Such people, who have need of the milk of consolation <note>original: *lacte consolationis*; a common biblical metaphor for beginners in faith who cannot yet "digest" difficult trials.</note> rather than the solid food of temptation or divine abandonment, are struck down by moderate temptations. Hence, at the end of spring or the beginning of summer, when the trees are still blossoming and at the start of the day when the sun has not yet risen higher, the flowers of future fruits are frequently attacked by two dangers: namely, frost <note>original: *pruina*</note> and mist <note>original: *nebula*</note>. What is signified by the frost, if not the hidden pride <note>original: *elatio*</note> of the heart? Therefore, in this flowering stage of the spiritual life, those who think they are something when they are nothing are constricted by the cold of that frost, deceiving themselves, or those who consider themselves worthy of their merit...

<summary>This page describes the humble prayer of a soul experiencing spiritual intoxication and introduces the dangers that threaten beginners, using the metaphor of frost and mist attacking spring blossoms to represent spiritual pride.</summary>

II

the reward of the deed <note>original: *mercedem facti*</note>, becoming like a wineskin wasting away in the frost. This is the frost that destroys the little flowers and nullifies the fruit. Truly, what is signified by the "mist" if not the clouding of reason? And what clouds reason more at this stage—and strikes down the flowers, which are the powers of the soul fertile with the fruit of good works—than to take delight in the gifts of divine sweetness and consolation for their own sake, rather than rising up to the praise of the Giver with all thanksgiving? Because of this, the recognition of truth is often lost, and a certain flavor of sweetness from a diabolical fiction <note>The author warns that false spiritual experiences can mimic divine ones to lead the soul into pride.</note> takes hold, ultimately deceiving the wretched person through falsehood.

### ¶ Precaution against temptations. Chapter XV.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut drop cap 'V' featuring a central stem with symmetrical scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>
**S**o that we may escape the aforementioned little temptations with noble caution, let us be taught briefly by the marvelous example of the flight of the bee. For the bee, dwelling in the unity of its congregation, flies out—not in a whirlwind of storms or winds, but in the stillness of a more serene breeze while the sun shines most brightly. In its circling wanderings, it surveys all flowers endowed with sweetness; yet, captured by the beauty of no single flower and the sweetness of none, it creates no delays. Instead, flying around them all widely and briefly, it extracts the material of honey and wax with the "hand" of its sting <note>original: *aculei sui manu*; a poetic way of describing the bee collecting pollen/nectar.</note>.

And bringing this together into the dwelling of its congregation, it produces fruit most usefully: fashioning a paramount thing of sweetness <note>honey, representing spiritual joy</note> and a precious nourishment for light <note>wax, representing the "light" of good works or candles used in worship</note>. Therefore, emulate the prudence of the little bee. Behold, **CHRIST**, the sun of eternal brightness, radiating the hearts of His chosen ones—open with devotion—with the light of His clarity, makes all their interior parts beautiful with the sweetness of flowers and a honey-flowing pleasantness of joy.

Therefore, if you are wise, follow the example of the bee: fly around the individual sweet gifts of God—both past and present—on the wings of reason and consideration. With the sting of charity and discretion <term>discretion: the spiritual ability to distinguish between good and evil impulses</term>, taste the sweetnesses of unique divine consolation in such a way that

Like a bee, a person should appreciate God's gifts briefly to create the "honey" of devotion and "wax" of good works without becoming stuck in selfish pleasure.</summary>

In this unity, you desire to remain with God eternally. This is the second degree of the first spiritual coming of **CHRIST**, which adorns the lower part of the human person.

## THE INVITATION TO DIVINE UNITY WHICH WOUNDS THE HEART. CHAPTER XVI.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial E featuring floral and foliate motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
And so, when May has ended and the sun turns its course in a circle toward the summer solstice, now reaching the middle of June, the earth is scorched by a greater heat. As the sun more abundantly draws up the humors <note>In medieval science, "humors" were the vital fluids of the earth or the body; here, they represent the soul's natural inclinations and energies being "evaporated" or refined by God's grace.</note> of the earth, the ground is dried out, and the things growing from the earth ripen more quickly. In this same way, when **CHRIST**, the Sun of Justice <note>original: *sole iustitie*; a traditional title for Christ derived from Malachi 4:2, symbolizing his role as the source of spiritual light and growth.</note>, is exalted in our hearts at the highest level—that is, when all the gifts of His consolation and sweetness, however exceptional, are set aside, and we strive for His most loving presence and His sweetest embraces, returning with humble praise and inward thanksgiving to the sea of God's riches from which all gifts flow—He strives to draw all things, that is, all the powers of our soul, toward Himself. 

Therefore, when the heart of the lover is neither conquered nor entirely held back by any tastes of divine consolation, but transcends all things (so that it may find the one whom it loves), the third degree of divine visitation is soon reached, lifting up and adorning the lower part of the soul. The operation of this visitation is a certain divine invitation or attraction, drawing the affection of the heart and all its powers upward, enticing and inviting them to His union, and crying out: "Leave the land and the country of your heart <note>original: *exite de terra ac patria cordis veſtri*; echoing God's call to Abraham in Genesis 12:1.</note>, and come into that region of my unity which I shall show you, according to the demand of my drawing <note>original: *tractus*; a technical mystical term describing the irresistible spiritual pull or "tug" God exerts on the soul to lead it into deeper contemplation.</note>." The significance of this drawing is not plain...

<summary>This page describes the transition to the third stage of spiritual progress, where Christ acts like a summer sun, drawing the soul's powers away from individual gifts and toward a total union with God. The author uses the biblical language of Abraham's call to illustrate the soul's departure from its "own land" into God's unity.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Unity, Sun of Justice, Spiritual Solstice, Jan van Ruusbroec, Contemplation, Thanksgiving, Divine Invitation, Mystical Drawing</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the third degree of the soul's spiritual journey, moving from the "invitation" of God to the resulting "spiritual languor" or lovesickness that arises when the soul's desire for union is delayed.</meta>

->II<-

...we cannot express it more clearly to the inexperienced, unless I repeat what I said before: namely, that this is a certain attraction or invitation of God, inwardly calling the heart into the embrace of His unity. This invitation affects and delights the heart of the lover above all previous delights, lifting it to a new stage of higher practice <note>original: *gradu exercitij altioris*; referring to a more advanced level of spiritual discipline or devotion.</note>. For this invitation to such an abundance of delights soon bursts forth <note>original: *explodit*; in this context, it suggests a powerful, outward release or manifestation of internal pressure.</note> with all affection through the gaping channels of the heart, and all the soul's powers prepare and adorn themselves with all desire for the bond of such peace and charity. This invitation is a certain illumination of **CHRIST**, the eternal sun, making the heart so burst with the joy of such love that it cannot be shut away by any human power. 

Because of this, the heart is wounded within and suffers the injury of love. Yet love, although it wounds, does not make one sad. For its wounding is both a most sweet passion <note>In the medieval sense, a "passion" is something the soul undergoes or suffers, often used to describe intense spiritual experiences.</note> and a most heavy pain. The wound of love is truly the most certain seal of future health, causing joy and sorrow at the same time. Finally, the shining sun **CHRIST** pours the rays of His brightness into the wounded heart, inviting it again to the embrace of His unity, and through this, renewing and reopening the heart's wound.

### ON THE LANGUOR AND IMPATIENCE OF LOVE. CHAPTER XVII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter C featuring an intricate pattern of stylized leaves and vines within a square frame.</image-desc>
But when **CHRIST** invites the heart by the grace of such a visitation, and the heart, having prepared itself in a bridal manner, raises itself with all its strength toward the kiss of union—and yet is not able to achieve the bond of unity—it soon falls into a spiritual languor <note>original: *lāgore*; a state of spiritual lovesickness or weakness caused by intense longing for God.</note>. For once the vitals of the heart are wounded by the sting of love, when it cannot obtain what it desires above all things, it is compelled to remain there where, by the very necessity of its stay, it languishes even more; and in the midst of this arising languor, it labors and groans...

# ON THE LANGUOR AND IMPATIENCE OF LOVE

...mixes. For at this time, when the eternal sun, **CHRIST**, is raised to the higher parts of the soul, and divinely pours out the burning rays of His light through the heart's affection and the intimate sting of burning fervor: a new heat, added to the previous one, alters the humors <term>humors: in medieval medicine, these were the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) thought to determine health and temperament</term> of the body; it burns, consumes, and dries up the strength of nature, breaking down the body with weakness. Therefore, the heart's affectionate panting for the kiss of union offered to it, and the heat-bearing irradiation of the eternal sun, cause a state of continual languor <note>original: *languoris*; a state of faintness or "lovesickness" common in mystical literature, where the soul is overwhelmed by desire for God.</note>. But do not be afraid, healthy reader. For this languor is not "unto death," but so that the works of our sun may be manifested <note>A reference to John 11:4, where Jesus speaks of Lazarus’s illness.</note>.

Furthermore, when the soul is unable to attain the kiss of the divine embrace, yet is entirely unwilling to be without it, a state of impatience of love <note>original: *amoris impatientia*</note> is generated in between these two states, pressing upon all things, both exterior and interior, with an intolerable burning. While this lasts, no use of any creature delights her; nor does she find anywhere among all things where she might be willing or content to lay her head. But while this impatience reigns, many subtle utterances of the spiritual senses are often revealed, and the doctrine of a singular wisdom is infused. 

Impatient love is ready to endure all things so that it may obtain what it loves. Moreover, this impatience of love is a certain internal instability of the soul, burning and refusing to obey any warnings until it attains what it loves. 

The impatience of love eats away at the lover’s heart and drinks up their blood. In this stage, the internal fervor of the heart is stronger than in all other stages of this spiritual summer <note>The author uses the seasons as a metaphor for the soul's progression; "summer" represents the height of passionate, active love.</note>, crushing and consuming the bodily nature from within without any exterior labor, and refining <note>original: *decoquens*; literally "cooking down" or ripening through heat.</note> and ripening the fruits of spiritual virtues more than in the stages mentioned before. 

Furthermore, after the first part of the burning summer in June, July occupies the middle, with the sun intensifying its heat much more. Similarly, while the impatience of love reigns, **CHRIST**, the shining sun, increases His heat. For [the rays] of His brightness...

<meta>This page continues Chapter XVII from the previous page, further describing the intense physical and emotional symptoms of the "impatience of love" and transitioning into Chapter XVIII, which discusses the visionary graces that follow this state.</meta>

II
e iij

The rays burn the heart of the impatient lover with such fervor that they cause his innermost blood to boil. This same impatience of love claims a powerful sovereignty over all the stages of spiritual exercise; for while all other stages observe some certain measure, this one alone chooses this specific "measure": to be beyond all measure. <note>original: *vt careat modo*; literally "to lack a mode or limit." In mystical theology, this refers to the soul's transition from human "modes" of acting to the "modeless" or infinite way in which God acts.</note>

Sometimes the impatient lover, with panting desire and burning impatience, longs to be released from the prison of the body so that he may be united to the One whom he loves. In this state, he turns the eyes of his heart entirely toward heaven, contemplating the celestial court filled with glory. He sees his Beloved crowned with glory and honor, making the saints drunk on the richness of His glory and quenching their thirst from the torrent of His pleasure <note>A reference to Psalm 36:8: "Thou shalt make them drink of the river of thy pleasures."</note>. Yet he sees himself as if he were an exile from his true homeland, overwhelmed by many troubles.

From this, groans, tears, and panting desires are born in many people. For when he considers the misery he suffers—from which he cannot free his neck no matter how much his will struggles—abundant tears soon flow from his eyes, revealing the depth of his internal sorrow. These tears refresh his heart a little with a warmth like falling dew; they are truly beneficial to his physical nature for preserving his bodily health <note>The author suggests that weeping acts as a physical release valve for the "heat" of spiritual passion, preventing the body from failing.</note>. In this way, the impatience of love is made bearable, so that the safety and life of the body are not lost. For this reason, every consideration, every spiritual exercise, and every moderate rule is useful to him, so that he may preserve his health and life for the further growth of his virtues.

### ON THE VARIOUS SPIRITUAL GRACES WHICH ARE FREQUENTLY BESTOWED UPON THE IMPATIENT LOVER. Chapter XVIII.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop cap 'I' featuring floral and foliate patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
Now, spiritual graces are sometimes bestowed upon the impatient lover. For those who love impatiently are occasionally snatched up into ecstasy <term>ecstasy: from the Greek *ekstasis*, meaning "to stand outside oneself"; a state of being overwhelmed by divine vision or "rapture"</term> or into the spirit. There, they are taught concerning the truths necessary for themselves or others, whether through spoken words, physical images, or spiritual likenesses...

### Page 78

...[revealed] or certainly some things regarding future events are revealed to them. And these are called **revelations** or **visions**. If these are exhibited in **imaginary figures** <note>original: *imaginarijs figuris*. In medieval thought, the "imagination" was the faculty that created or stored mental images; thus, an imaginary vision involves seeing symbols or figures like those in a dream.</note> through the power of an angel of God, they are shown in the mirror of the imagination. 

But if it is an **intellectual truth** / or some spiritual likenesses / by which God reveals himself in some way: they are imprinted on the **intellectual eyes** <note>original: *itellectualibus oculis*. This refers to the soul's ability to understand spiritual truths directly, without the need for mental pictures or sensory symbols.</note> / and can be expressed in words as far as one's ability allows. 

Sometimes, however, the impatient lover is caught up into an **excess of mind** <note>original: *mentis excessum*; a state of being "outside" one's normal mental state, often used interchangeably with ecstasy.</note> / yet not entirely outside himself into some incomprehensible good: who, according to that mode which he saw and heard in this simple intuition / would be unable to explain it in any words. For God alone works this without the aid of any creature / and without any intermediary. And it is called <term>rapture</term> <note>original: *raptus*. A state where the soul is "snatched" up by God, bypassing the normal senses and reasoning.</note>. 

Sometimes, indeed, God flashes in the spirit with sudden lightning. And behold, a certain flash of extraordinary clarity, shining from a certain simple serenity in a brief stroke / snatches the spirit above itself as if in a moment: but when the light soon vanishes / the spirit returns to itself. For this is a certain special and excellent work of God alone: which renders those to whom it is granted by a singular gift / radiant with **intellectual light**. 

Sometimes also, one who is suffering the impatience of love is irradiated with a certain spiritual light by God, who acts through whatever intermediary he pleases. At the charming face of this light, the desiring affection of the heart is delighted to its very marrow: while it raises itself with a fiery impetus to meet it / soon, just as **new wine** <note>original: *mustū*. A reference to the biblical metaphor (Matthew 9:17) of new wine bursting old wineskins, here representing the soul's inability to contain divine joy.</note> without a vent-hole bursts new jars / so indeed the longing of the heart, by the meeting of this light, is so drenched by such a torrent of unusual pleasure / that because of the abundance of new joy, which it is least able to contain within itself due to the narrowness of its own capacity / it is compelled to break out in a physical voice. 

This shouting, however, is called <term>jubilation</term> <note>original: *iubilus*. In mystical tradition, this is a wordless cry or song of joy that erupts when the heart's happiness exceeds its ability to speak.</note>: which is a certain joy of the heart / which, because it cannot be explained in words...

**II**

It cannot [be contained], yet it cannot be entirely kept silent: it bursts forth through the breath of the voice. This **jubilation** <term>jubilus</term> <note>original: *iubilus*. In medieval mystical tradition, a *jubilus* is a wordless song or shout of joy that erupts when the heart is so full of divine love that language fails.</note> occurs when the heart meets the aforementioned light in such a way: whether the suffering person wills it or not, it inevitably resounds outwardly as long as this exercise lasts, following its own course. Furthermore, some who are suspended in deep devotion are taught through dreams by their own angel or by other angels revealing things to them. 

There are truly some who, while keeping watch over their senses, abound in many intrusions of thoughts which they judge to be revelations, or they see marvelous things in dreams. Because these people are focused on the business of the senses, they do not know the **impatience of love**. They do not know the wounds of **charity** and **affection**; not being held by this [divine] languor, they may appear healthy but perhaps languish more sickly. For one's own ingenuity can fashion such intrusions for itself, or a good or bad angel can inspire them. Therefore, if any who are stirred by such things presume to babble about unknown matters, they should be listened to only insofar as what they say agrees with **Holy Scripture** / or is consistent with reasonable truth. But if greater faith is placed in them, they may lead perhaps into the pit of **deception** <note>original: *seductionis*; here meaning being led astray or deceived by false spiritual experiences.</note>.

This refers to the "Dog Days" of summer, a period traditionally associated with extreme heat, the spreading of disease, and the "madness" of dogs.</note> and begins to depart from us, days of sickly heat arise, such that during these days even dogs are sometimes driven to madness. --- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the end of the previous chapter regarding the "impatience of love." It uses the metaphor of intense summer heat to describe the physical and spiritual toll that divine longing takes on the mystic, comparing the soul's state to parched earth or fish in water.</meta>

<header>THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...[con]fining, men languish and wither away, and together with men, sometimes the fish likewise die. We do not say the cause of this is the excessive heat of the sun at that time, but rather the disposition and intemperance of the soil, and of those living in it who are not able to withstand the power of the sun. In this way, the impatient lover, while suffering too much heat of divine love, endures as it were the impression of the heat of the days. For while he is wounded and languid from the heat of divine love, he is parched by continuous burning through impatience; as the flame of affection and desire increases further, he incurs a certain intolerable anguish of the heart, just as if one in labor were deprived of the power of giving birth.

Thus it happens that while, with an alternating gaze, he does not cease to contemplate now the wound of the heart, now the face of the beloved: as the dryness—which is the refinement <note>original: *lima*; literally "file." In mystical literature, this refers to the way divine heat "files away" or polishes the soul by removing earthly attachments.</note> of heat—increases, at the same time the heat of impatience and the pain of anguish also grow. At last, as the languor increases, it brings it about that—just as a tree in a dry place—so he too, entirely fails and withers from excessive heat. Having been made a **martyr of love**, dying bodily, knowing nothing of **purgatory** <term>purgatory</term> <note>The place of purification after death. The author suggests the intensity of divine love on earth is so purifying that the soul bypasses this state and goes straight to heaven.</note>, and purged by the fire of love, he flies away to the heavenly homeland as the **Bridegroom** <term>Sponsum</term> meets him.

Truly, although he who is not permitted to live by the languor of love might die safely, nevertheless, as long as more abundant fruit is hoped for from the tree, it must not be destroyed in any way. Sometimes, however, God pours such a sweetness of suavity into the impatient heart that the heart swims in delights like a fish in water. From this, the innermost parts of the heart boil more vehemently with the heat of **charity**, and the health of nature wastes away further. Finally, it is necessary for all those enduring the impatience of love to languish under this exercise; nevertheless, not all die, being governed by the rule of **discretion** <term>discretio</term> <note>original: *discretionis*. A vital monastic virtue meaning spiritual discernment or moderation, which allows the mystic to balance intense spiritual experiences with physical survival.</note>.

## ¶ CONCERNING A CERTAIN OTHER
## DANGER. Chapter XX.

<vocab>love, languor, impatience, heart, sweetness, charity, purgatory, bridegroom, discretion</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "impatience of love," moving from the physical sensation of spiritual heat to a specific metaphor involving a dangerous "honey-dew." This natural phenomenon serves as an allegory for spiritual delusions that can occur when the soul is overwhelmed by intense religious fervor.</meta>

## II

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut capital letter 'A' with intricate white-line vine and floral patterns on a dark square background.</image-desc>

**A**nother danger, again, the suffering lover should know he must avoid above all else. For at this time of year <note>Likely referring to the "dog days" of midsummer, previously established as the setting for this spiritual crisis.</note>, a certain kind of dew is generated; it is better known by shepherds than it once was by the wise men of the world. It is a very thick vapor, like the smoke of oil or of tallow or fat, generated from a mixture of three elements: namely, earth, water, and air. And the heat of fire is within it, processing it; thus, an oily moisture is produced from it, very sweet, much like honey. And when it falls upon plants, appearing in its descent like a dripping rain, the plants at first become as if soaked in honey. But later, when the subtle, airy moisture evaporates, the coarse earthly residue remains. And because it was well mixed and processed, it appears like flying flour from a mill sprinkled over the leaves of trees and herbs. From the dryness, it sometimes curves and contracts them; at other times, as the heat evaporates, it draws the moisture of the leaf out to the surface with it. And because an outer skin <note>original: *pellicula*</note> is formed by the coldness of the air, the trapped air is turned into a sensory animal spirit, and from this, worms and small flies are generated.

And shepherds predict from these things (as often happens) the death of livestock, especially sheep. The cause of this is the too greedy consumption of the sweet pasture. Whereby the liver <term>liver</term> <note>original: *epar*. In medieval medicine, the liver was considered the central organ for digestion and the production of blood and "spirits."</note> becomes blocked; and the great amount of nourishment drawn in by it cannot be further digested or sent to the limbs because the veins are overfilled. Therefore, it rots in the liver, and leeches <note>original: *sanguisugę*. While literally "bloodsuckers," in this context the author refers to liver flukes, a common parasite in sheep that shepherds associated with "honey-dew" or mildew on grass.</note> are generated from it and fill the entire liver. 

Following the example of this phenomenon: in the days of the greatest heat <note>original: *caumatū*; referring to the intense heat of both the season and the "impatience of love."</note> of impatient love, through the work of Satan transforming himself into an angel of light, a certain deceptive <note>original: *phantasticū*; referring to the "phantasia" or the faculty of imagination which, if misguided, leads to spiritual delusion.</note> light, shining inwardly, is poured in, by which they feel themselves as if entirely wrapped in a sack. However, in this light, many wonders [are seen] through imaginary images...

<summary>The author uses a detailed medieval scientific explanation of "honey-dew" (mildew) causing liver flukes in sheep as an allegory for spiritual delusion. He warns that just as sheep die from overindulging in sweet, tainted grass, a mystic can be led astray by deceptive "internal lights" sent by the devil.</summary>

<keywords>impatience of love, honey-dew, liver flukes, shepherds, spiritual delusion, Satan as angel of light, medieval medicine, allegory</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical warning against spiritual pride, following the description of "honey-dew" that attracts livestock but breeds parasites. The author now applies this natural phenomenon to the psychology of the "suffering lover" or the mystic who is prone to mistaking their own thoughts for divine revelation.</meta>

<header>OF THE SPIRITUAL NUPTIALS</header>

...are shown. Some people even admit into their thoughts certain ideas as if they were divine inspirations; sometimes these are indeed true, but more often they are false. These are far too eagerly accepted by the inexperienced, and they are foolishly venerated as if they were a divine revelation. Because the person delights in these things with an inward joy of the heart, they are, in a sense, drenched with a viscous and honeyed dew. Consequently, the foliage of their character <note>original: *morum*</note>, the flowers of their virtues, and the fruits of their good works are stained by this sweet but deceptive sprinkling.

As a result, everything begins to blister with wormy outer skins <note>original: *pelliculis*; referring back to the "worms and small flies" mentioned on the previous page</note>, because they are being eaten away by vainglory just as a moth consumes fabric. And when the unskilled soul <note>original: *imperita anima*</note> begins to nourish itself delightfully on these "sweet pastures" time and again, the liver—by which we mean the deep affection of divine love <note>In medieval medicine, the liver was often considered the seat of the "concupiscible" power or the source of the blood and emotions.</note>—rots from this poisonous fodder. Then, a hidden leech <note>original: *sanguisuga*</note> is born: the pride of one’s own self-esteem. The soul unhappily withers away, eventually destined for a miserable death through diabolical deception.

For the cunning serpent, noticing that the wretched fool delights in vain visions and ill-omened oracles, repeats his tricks. The blood of divine love and devoted affection, which was once pure and fervent, is consumed by the rising pride of the heart. This happens while the person does not hesitate to claim as true—and stubbornly defend—things that are contrary to divine truth, shown to them through frivolous visions or diabolical suggestions. At the same time, despite the "fruits" of his supposed merits, he falls headlong into the abyss of the serpent’s seduction. The vital organs of his charity are gnawed away by the leech of pride. However, for those who have walked the paths previously shown, even if it were possible for them to be tempted by this kind of "diabolical light," it would be very difficult for them to be overcome by it in any way.

<summary>This page concludes an allegory comparing spiritual delusion to a toxic "honey-dew" that breeds leeches and worms. It warns that pride and false visions can corrupt the "liver" of divine love and lead a soul into diabolical deception.</summary>

<meta>This page follows a warning about the "honey-dew" of false spiritual pride and diabolical visions. Here, the author introduces the ant as a model of spiritual prudence and patience, contrasting the ant’s industrious gathering with the "leaf-wandering" of the aimless or deceived soul.</meta>

# ¶ AN EXAMPLE of the ant for the caution of the impatient lover.
## Chapter XXI.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'V' set in a square frame, embellished with intricate scrolling floral and foliage patterns.</image-desc>

In order that the impatient lover may guard against the snares of the enemy, and—by prudently and naturally running through this level of intolerable heat—may climb to the heights of the virtues, let us propose an example from the industry of the ant <note>original: *formica*</note> for the sake of caution. The ant is a tiny little creature <note>original: *vermiculus*; literally "little worm," but used historically for various small insects</note>, yet very strong relative to the smallness of its little body. It is also wise and not easily killed. It dwells in a dry little spot of burning earth within the small gathering-place of its paths. Looking forward to the future, it collects grains of wheat in summer to eat in winter, cutting the grains so that they do not sprout and flower, but instead may be eaten during the unseasonable time of labor <note>Referring to winter, when the earth is dormant and labor in the fields is impossible.</note>. It does not seek leaf-wandering <note>original: *foliuaga*; wandering aimlessly among leaves</note> detours, but they all go forward together in a group on the public way. And when the time appointed for this by nature arrives, taking up wings and dividing the air with thin little membranes, it seeks the heights.

By the example of this little creature, let the impatient lover be strong in awaiting the new coming of CHRIST, and be prudent and cautious against the diabolical injections of visions. Let him not choose to die with foolish ease; rather, let him always provide divine praises for God and new virtues for himself by living patiently. Let him dwell in the gathering of his heart and all his powers, and, consenting to the divine invitation, let him always sigh with the whole desire of his heart for the union of deifying unity <note>original: *unionem deificę vnitatis*; the mystical concept of becoming "god-like" through union with the Divine</note>.

Let him have his dwelling in a dry place of burning earth—that is, in the <term>impatience</term> <note>In this context, *impatientia* refers to a burning, restless longing or "holy suffering" that cannot be satisfied in this life.</note> of burning love. Laboring in the brief summer of this life, let him treasure up the fruits of eternal bliss <note>original: *beatítūdis*</note>. Let him cut the collected fruit into two parts: through one part desiring the enjoyment of divinity with all the heart's affection, and through the other part restraining himself through the moderation of reason—as much as he is able—while awaiting the time prefixed by God. Thus, the fruit will be saved forever. Let him not walk as a leaf-wanderer through the windings...

<summary>This chapter uses the ant as an allegory for the spiritual seeker, emphasizing the need for industrious preparation, the rejection of false visions, and the patient endurance of divine longing.</summary>
<keywords>ant allegory, spiritual industry, impatience of love, divine union, prudence, resistance to visions, spiritual labor</keywords>
