It concludes the description of the third stage—focused on the "royal road" of love—and begins the fourth stage, which explores the experience of spiritual desolation through the metaphor of the changing seasons and the sun's path through the zodiac.</meta>

...[let him not seek] winding detours <note>original: *amfractus devios*</note>, that is, he should not adopt a singular <note>meaning an eccentric or idiosyncratic</note> way of living. Instead, he should walk with a free brow along the royal road of love wherever it leads—through bitter things and sweet, through joyful things and sad, and through whatever storms of love may arise. For at last, when the appointed time is found and the virtues of contemplation are worthily prepared, he will receive wings (by God’s gift) and, with swift flight, he will penetrate the hidden secrets of heaven to see God <note>original: *deivīsurus*; literally "intending to see God"</note>. Let these things be said for the third stage.

# ¶ Concerning the fourth stage of the first spiritual coming of CHRIST, which is the desolation of the impatient lover. Chapter XXII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial Q with intricate foliage and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

Now, wishing to establish the fourth stage of the first spiritual coming of CHRIST—which points to a deeper exercise of the "lower man" <note>This refers to the sensory and emotional part of the human person, as opposed to the "higher" intellectual or spark of the soul.</note>—let us follow the course of the sun, as we began. Thus, July being passed, the sun completes its course in August, where they place the sign of the Virgin <note>referring to the zodiac sign Virgo</note>. For at this time the glorious mother of CHRIST, Mary, ascended into the heavens, full of celestial joy and wealthy with the riches of all virtues. In these days, the heat of the solar rays is diminished, and the fruits of the earth that last for the whole year—such as grains, wines, and the like (which have reached the limit of maturity fixed for them by nature)—are gathered. Also, from the grains born that year, new seeds for the future harvest are cast, so that, covered by the clods of earth and dying, they may be multiplied for human use. And in this hemispheric circuit of the sun, the whole efficiency of solar power in our regions is brought to its completion.

In this way, our glorious sun, JESUS—once the higher parts of the soul have been attained according to the form of the third stage described above, and once the innermost parts of the heart have been scorched by the burning rays of June and July—descending in a straight motion toward August: he withdraws and hides the heat-bearing rays of his light, leaving his lover behind. Once the heat of love has grown lukewarm, the heart of the lover soon becomes barren with a dry numbness <note>original: *torpore*</note>. This, therefore, is the divine...

<summary>The text concludes the "third stage" of spiritual growth and introduces the "fourth stage," using the transition from July to August as a metaphor for the soul moving from the heat of spiritual passion into a period of "desolation" and "numbness."</summary>
<keywords>spiritual stages, Jesus as the Sun, August, Virgo, spiritual desolation, Mary's Assumption, agricultural metaphor, contemplation</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "fourth stage" of Christ's spiritual coming. The previous page ended discussing the sun's transition into August (Virgo) and the withdrawal of its summer heat, symbolizing a change in the soul's experience.</meta>

[...the radiance] of fervor and splendor is withdrawn, and it is the desolation of the lover. The fourth stage of the first spiritual coming of CHRIST carries the lower part of the soul <note>The *inferiorem animae partem* refers to the sensory and emotional faculties, which must be trained through hardship.</note> toward higher exercises of virtue, so that He decorates it with a new ornament of spiritual life. Thus CHRIST, coming in this way, resounds with a new cry, saying: "Go out <note>original: *exite*</note> according to the form that I show you." But he who "goes out"—who was once wealthy, burning with the heat of love and filled with every sweetness—now finds himself poor and lukewarm, forsaken by God and desolated of all sweetness. He has been transferred from the burning summer of impatient love into the lukewarmness of autumn, and the abundant riches of divine love have been exchanged for a great poverty. Sitting therefore mournfully on the pavement of desolation, he complains and says:

> Were you not once that powerful, rich, and famous man, full of riches and lacking nothing? You who, looking down upon the earth, dwelt entirely in heavenly things; you who, content with no gifts of God, gasped for the embrace of divinity alone? And where now is your power, which not only restrained the passions of your own body at a nod, but even—flying in divine things—trampled the necks of all creatures? Where are the riches of virtues? Where are the delights of love? Where is the internal consolation of sweetness, from whose abundance you most devoutly overflowed with sweet tears, flaming sighs, honeyed praises, and thanksgivings? Alas, my harp is turned to mourning <note>A reference to Job 30:31: "My harp also is turned to mourning, and my organ into the voice of them that weep."</note>, and I have become weak, inglorious, and a beggar. The things which my soul previously refused to touch are now my food because of my want <note>A reference to Job 6:7.</note>.

And it happens to a person of this kind just like one who desired to build a great building with much labor and copious expense, but soon, with everything pulled down to the ground in one rush, he has lost both his work and his costs. Thus it happens that nature, grieving that it has suffered such a great loss, murmurs intolerably and is saddened. Nor is he only stripped of spiritual consolations, but even fre- <meta>catchword: fre- (frequently/often)</meta>

**N**ow, wishing to establish the fourth stage of the first spiritual coming of CHRIST—which suggests a higher training for the **lower man** <note>The "lower man" refers to the sensory and emotional faculties of the soul, which must be disciplined before higher union occurs.</note>—let us follow the course of the sun as we began. Thus, with July having passed, the sun completes August, where the sign of the **Virgin** <note>The zodiac sign Virgo.</note> is located. For at this time, the glorious mother of CHRIST, **Mary**, ascends into the heavens <note>A reference to the Feast of the Assumption on August 15th, when Mary is traditionally said to have been taken up into heaven.</note> full of celestial joy and rich with the wealth of all virtues. During these days, the heat of the sun's rays diminishes, and the fruits of the earth that last for the whole year—such as grains, wines, and similar things (which have endured until their natural limit of ripeness)—are gathered. Also, from the crops born that year, new seeds for the future harvest are cast, so that by dying under the covering of the soil <note>original: *glebis operientibus emortua*</note>, they may be multiplied for human use. And in this turning of the sun's hemisphere, the entire efficiency of the solar power in our regions is fulfilled.

In this same way, our glorious sun, **JESUS**, having attained the higher parts of the soul according to the form of the third stage described before, and the intimate parts of the heart having been scorched by the burning rays of June and July, He now descends with a direct movement toward August. He withdraws and hides the heat-bearing rays of His light, thereby abandoning His lover. ->OF THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE<-

...frequently he is despoiled of temporal possessions <note>Worldly goods or wealth.</note>, deprived of friends and kin, and abandoned by all creatures. Consigned to oblivion, he is thought to possess no holiness; his character and his works are interpreted sinisterly <note>original: "sinistre"; meaning unfairly or with ill intent.</note> by everyone. He is held in contempt by those he lives with and occasionally falls into sickness. He very often endures carnal temptations of the body and, at times, spiritual temptations that are more dangerous for everyone. Broken by such great calamities, he begins to fear a fall and to strike against the threshold of despair, placed as he is at the final limit of his standing. An exile, therefore, and a wanderer, destitute of divine consolations, he seeks and he laments. He seeks the good works of others because he suffers such poverty. In a way, he begs for the alms of prayers from door to door; he commits himself to the prayers of the Church and calls upon the angels—he calls out to see if there is anyone who might answer him, and he turns to some of the saints for help.

### ¶ Counsel on how the desolate person should conduct and rule themselves. Chapter XXIV.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring scrolling vine or acanthus motifs inside a square border.</image-desc>

Having thus enumerated the calamities on every side, both divine and human, that afflict the desolate lover, let us now describe a consoling counsel by which he ought to rule and comfort himself. You, therefore, who feel—or rather complain—that you have been abandoned by God (though this is done out of love, not out of hatred): reflect with a humble heart that you had nothing from yourself that you did not first receive. And for this reason, just as you ought not to have gloried in those gifts as if you had not received them from another, so now you should not be grieved that you have lost what was received from elsewhere. For you do not possess from your own nature vital riches, but rather lethal vices; not spiritual sweetness, but bodily vanities; not fervor, but sluggishness; not diligence, but negligence; not progress, but many-sided defects. Therefore, strive to emulate Job—a man of supreme patience and illustrious desolation...

<meta>This page continues the spiritual counsel for the "desolate lover" who feels abandoned by God. The previous page ended with the author beginning to offer comfort to the suffering soul, comparing their state to the trials of Job.</meta>

## II.

Sit in the dunghill <note>original: "sterquilinio"; a reference to the biblical Job (Job 2:8), symbolizing utter humiliation and the loss of all earthly comforts.</note> of your weakness, and by the power of the Crucified One (whose strength withered like a potsherd <note>original: "vt testa aruit"; a reference to Psalm 22:15, "my strength is dried up like a potsherd."</note>), if any pus of impatience should break out, scrape it away; and with the patience of a submissive will, speak thus:

> The Lord gave, and the Lord has taken away; as it pleased the Lord, so it has been done; blessed be the name of the Lord. <note>original: "Dominus dedit, Dominus abstulit..." Job 1:21.</note>

Denying yourself also in all things, you should add from your own heart:

> I will, O Lord, that just as I once joyfully used your gifts, so now I am also willing to go without them, deprived of your grace, provided only that it be the pleasure of your blessed will and honorable to your divine majesty. Not my will, O Lord, according to the desires of nature, but yours; and not according to the longing of my spirit, but let your will, not mine, be done. And because, Lord, I am entirely yours: take what is yours. For you will find me ready to go with you, whether into hell or into paradise (if you wish it, and it befits your praise). Therefore, do with me whatever pleases you, provided only that you do not omit the marks of your omnipotence.

Saying these things, prepare for yourself a bath of eternal joy from all your calamities and desolations, and offer yourself into the hands of God. Rejoice in that which he has granted you to suffer for his honor. If you truly conduct yourself in this way (unless I am mistaken), you have never been refreshed by such intimate joy. For I think nothing is sweeter to a lover than to feel himself brought entirely under the will of his beloved—provided, however, that one ascends to this state through all the prescribed steps, even if one does not use all the modes of the prescribed exercises. For we do not say that all are necessary, but we affirm that the foundations of the virtues are necessary: which are **humble obedience** in acting and the **patience of a mortified will** in enduring. For in these two, the entire structure of this degree is founded with perpetual solidity.

Furthermore, the month of August having passed and coming to September, the sun enters the equinox, making the days equal to the nights and balancing the light with the darkness. In a similar way, our sun, CHRIST, maintains an equilibrium of equity <note>original: "aequatitas"; a sense of fairness or divine balance.</note> in his desolate ones, making the days of joy equal to the nights of sorrow...

->### THE ORNAMENT OF SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<--

...ness of desolation, balancing in their hearts the darkness of previous consolations against clarity. He equates bitter things with sweet, sorrows with joys, cold with heat, poverty with riches, and calamities with delights; and with the sole exception of sin, he weighs all outward circumstances <note>original: "accidentia"; in medieval philosophy, these are the non-essential properties of a thing, such as its current state of health or wealth.</note> with an even scale. But while we describe the chills of autumn, the setting of the sun, and the barrenness of this season, it is not right to overlook the vintage of more precious fruits.

You, therefore, who are in such a state—namely, balancing all things—who seem to yourself stripped of all virtues and abandoned by God and all his creatures: if you are a wise harvester, the fruits of the earth are ripe. Gather the wine, the oil, and the grain. Whatever your physical nature suffers in any way, offer it freely to the divine sight without a murmur of protest against the higher will. Reflect upon the internal and external exercises of virtues which you once performed with a joyful spirit, back when the field of knowledge was radiant and the seasons of love were burning; and performing these tasks according to the measure of your strength with your greatest effort and a good and generous heart, offer them to God. Know without a doubt that they have never been so pleasing to the divine eyes, nor ever so glorious in themselves.

Furthermore, be willing to forgo every consolation and every taste of sweetness ever poured into you from heaven, so long as it serves the divine honor. In this way, you will be enriched by the fruit of your grain, wine, and oil; thus the weeds <note>original: "lolium"; often translated as tares or darnel, representing spiritual sluggishness.</note> of drowsiness will be turned into the wheat of diligence, the vinegar of desolation into the wine of exultation, and the dregs <note>original: "amurca"; the bitter, watery sediment left after pressing olives.</note> of poverty and the brine <note>original: "muria"</note> of vanity into the oil of charity. By your patience and the wise governance of your condition, your friends and neighbors will also be taught, and they will advance toward the fruit of a better life. Through this, the seeds of your virtues, multiplied by the growth of many others, will mature into a bountiful harvest. This, then, is the fourth step, which beautifully adorns the sensitive part of the soul and leads into an exceptional and singular spiritual exercise <note>The "sensitive part" refers to the part of the human soul responsible for feeling and emotion, which Ruusbroec argues must be disciplined and decorated with virtue.</note>.

<summary>Ruusbroec explains how the soul can find spiritual 'harvest' even in times of desolation by balancing hardships against former joys and offering their suffering to God. This process constitutes the fourth step in the refinement of the soul's emotional and sensory nature.</summary>

<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual desolation, virtue, harvest metaphor, patience, charity, fourth step, spiritual exercises</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "autumnal" stage of the spiritual life, where the soul feels abandoned and cold. The previous page advised the soul to offer its sufferings to God as a "harvest" of patience. This page transitions into a warning about the spiritual sicknesses that can take root during this difficult period of desolation.</meta>

Nevertheless, we by no means prescribe a growth of virtues or an increase in perfection at this stage. But because those who ascend to this degree are proven, tempted, and attacked in manifold ways by God and by creatures: for this reason, the virtue of patience and of a mortified will <note>A "mortified" will refers to the practice of subduing one's own desires to align with the divine.</note>, along with a singular humility, is considered their special ornament of perfection; although, without a doubt, patience and the surrender of one's own will into the divine will are necessary for all who seek salvation.

### ¶ CONCERNING THE SPIRITUAL illnesses which
### arise during the time of this stage. Chapter 24.

<margin>Evil humors of the soul, and indeed they arise in them.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial H containing a floral motif with a central vase or urn from which symmetrical foliage and flowers emerge.</image-desc>
Indeed, during the time of this stage—when the warmth is dampened by the decline of the sun at the equinox—evil humors <note>In medieval medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids believed to control health and temperament. The author uses this as a metaphor for spiritual health.</note> are generated in many of the unwary, obstructing the stomach, creating adverse illnesses, and stifling the appetite and the taste for delightful foods; many are even killed by their own imbalance <note>original: "discrasia"; a medical term for an imbalance of bodily fluids or "humors."</note> or by a putrid multitude of these fluids. By the multitude or putridity of these humors, some turn into sufferers of dropsy <note>original: "hydropes"; a condition involving painful swelling from excess fluid, here used to represent spiritual stagnation.</note>, while others fall into various fevers; for which reason they either suffer sickness for a long time, or succumb more quickly, or after a long illness they nevertheless die at last. In this same way, whoever—after having possessed a good will, or after a taste of divine sweetness—stumbles and wanders from the path of truth: being held back by a spiritual obstruction, dropsy, or fever, they do not progress; or, having died to the virtues, they also finally succumb to the death of eternal damnation. But especially in this time of desolation, there is need for great fortitude. And so that one may exercise themselves according to the formula of the preceding chapter, it is very necessary indeed that they do not fall ill. For the unwise, and those governed by a poor regimen <note>original: "regimine"; here referring to a disciplined way of life or spiritual routine.</note>, incur a spiritual obstruction of the stomach and a stifling of their appetite. The season has indeed grown cold in them, and therefore their nature in virtues and good works...

This page begins by describing the symptoms of this spiritual decline: a longing for physical comfort and a lack of discipline.</meta>

...weakened, they desire the softness of the flesh and the rest of the body even beyond the rule of discretion <note>The "rule of discretion" refers to the virtue of discernment, or the ability to judge what is truly necessary for the body versus what is merely a selfish indulgence.</note>. Some also desire to be refreshed by divine consolations, provided they can obtain these without their own expense or effort. Some "hunt" for comfort in created things, although this sometimes leads to harmful dangers. Finally, some appear to themselves to be faint-hearted, fragile, and exhausted; because of this, they suspect that whatever kindness or comfort they are able to show must be reserved entirely for themselves. In this way, to give in to the desires of the flesh and to indulge the body’s pleasures rather than its necessities is like having evil humors <note>In medieval medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids believed to control health and temperament. The author uses this as a metaphor for spiritual health.</note> that block the "stomach of the heart," utterly suffocating the appetite for delightful foods—that is, for the spiritual virtues.

## CONCERNING SPIRITUAL dropsy, which
### is greed. Chapter 25.

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial G decorated with floral and foliate patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

Once these evil and corrupt humors have been generated, in the course of time, those full of such fluids slide into "dropsy" <note>original: "hydropisim." Dropsy (now called edema) is a condition characterized by an accumulation of fluid that causes swelling. Here, it is a metaphor for greed: the more a person "drinks" (acquires), the more they "swell" with possessions, yet their thirst only increases.</note>—that is, into a thirst and desire for temporal things. Whatever they drink through the acquisition of any opportunity, the more they thirst, because they suffer from a spiritual dropsy. The belly—that is, the possession and collection of things owned—always swells, and yet the thirst and desire for having does not decrease. The "face" of the conscience is drawn tight and grows thin, because they place a barrier against the grace by which they should be nourished and grow fat <note>In a spiritual sense, to "grow fat" means to be rich in grace and spiritual health.</note>. Furthermore, those in whom the water of temporal possessions occupies the "chest"—that is, those who cling to earthly things with a fervent affection—are not able to walk in the works of charity, because they are weak and destitute of strength. The "trachea" of the breath—that is, the influx of charity and grace—is also blocked in them; and therefore, the waters of temporal...

<summary>This page uses medical metaphors—evil humors and dropsy—to describe the spiritual decline into selfishness and greed (avarice). It explains how a soul focused on physical comfort and material wealth becomes spiritually "suffocated" and unable to perform acts of charity.</summary>
<keywords>dropsy, greed, avarice, spiritual humors, discretion, charity, temporal things, spiritual illness</keywords>

However, those who are only possessed by the "waters" of temporal possessions around their "belly"—that is, within an excessive affection of the heart—so that they exercise mastery and give to others when necessity demands: though they may have been sick for a long time with the weakness of disordered affection, they can nevertheless be easily cured.

### CONCERNING the four types of spiritual fevers. Chapter 26.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'N' featuring white-line floral and foliate patterns against a dark background, set within a square frame.</image-desc>

Now, some people, arising from these evil humors of disordered affection—who focus on bodily delights and gape for the comforts of created things—do not fall into the spiritual **dropsy** <note>original: "hydropem." This refers to the greed discussed in the previous chapter.</note> of which we have already spoken, but instead fall into four types of spiritual fevers: namely, the **daily**, the **tertian** <note>A fever that returns every third day.</note>, and the **double quartan** <note>A fever that returns every fourth day.</note>, which is both simple and composite.

The spiritual and daily fever is **excessive curiosity**—namely, the wandering of the heart and the senses. For there are some who curiously travel through everything, wanting to know all things, to speak about everything, and to involve themselves in all matters; yet they neglect themselves quite carelessly. Indeed, while they are worried about other people's affairs, they are forgetful regarding their own. They are troubled by many things they have seen or heard; they are easily irritated on any occasion; their thoughts are scattered, twisting their hearts; and their mind is tossed about in every direction by the wind of levity <note>A medieval term for fickleness or a lack of serious spiritual purpose.</note>, just like a reed. 

This is the daily *synocha* <note>original: "synocha." In medieval medicine, a "synocha" was a continuous fever that did not remit.</note>, or the fever of curiosity. Because they are occupied with such things from morning until evening, and at night they either reflect on them while awake or dream of them while slumbering. Therefore, although this fever may not destroy the life of grace, nor immediately introduce a mortal vice <note>A serious sin that separates the soul from God.</note>, it nevertheless diminishes the ardor of devotion, hinders the internal exercise of the heart, and dulls the taste for divine sweetness and virtues. Because of these things, the merit of the eternal reward is sometimes taken away. Now, the tertian [fever]...

Truly, those suffering from the tertian fever of instability caused by cold are these: those who are raised toward God with a right intention, indeed, but not a simple one; because, clearly, some external thing is placed alongside Him, which is sought equally and out of order under that same intention. And because that charity grows lukewarm from a lack of fervor—since it is not the love of God alone, but a disordered affection for the works of virtue that equally excites and instigates them—we rightly identify cold as the cause of this fever. Those suffering from this are truly inconstant from the heart.

For in everything they do, nature secretly hunts for what is its own. Very often they are even ignorant of this pursuit, as they have no knowledge of themselves. These people similarly change their way of life in various ways according to the variation of days; and seeking counsel regarding their life, today they choose one confessor, tomorrow another; they are troublesome

Indeed, with what dread should they not tremble, who have never had knowledge of God or the spiritual life? Who have never tasted that sweetness even with a slight taste or touched it with the tips of their lips, with which the hearts of those practicing spiritually are often deeply drenched.

## HOW CHRIST ascended these four risings.
### Chapter XXVII.

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter 'V' featuring floral patterns and interlacing lines, set within a square frame.</image-desc>

<margin>the first step</margin>
Truly, while we have the light, let us walk in the light, lest we be overtaken by darkness and go astray. Let us consider our head and our prince, CHRIST: who taught the four prescribed steps <note>The "four steps" or "four risings" refer to stages of spiritual advancement often compared to the rising of the sun.</note> in his words and demonstrated them through his deeds. For he himself, the sun of eternal brightness, rising for us from an origin before the dawn in the horizon of the most holy Trinity, proceeding from the womb of the stainless Virgin and Mother—who is the dawn and the beginning of the day of grace—illuminated our horizon, shining with the grace of his presence both sensibly <note>In this context, "sensibly" means through the physical senses, referring to Christ's physical incarnation on Earth.</note> and spiritually. Therefore, he held the first step, and still holds it, because he was unique and united. For in him, all virtues ever made or to be made were united and gathered together with their creators. And so, while he was equal and unique in the form of God, humbly taking the form of a servant, he was mercifully united to human nature. His heart was also pierced by the intimate heat of divine love: because he himself came to send fire upon the earth, which ardently kindles the hearts of his chosen ones. He was also moved with sensible affection and charitable faithfulness toward the Father, and toward those who are to enjoy Him eternally. Therefore, his heart burned with piously constant desires for the needs of all, in the devotion of flame-spewing love <note>original: "flammivomi amoris." This vivid term suggests a love so intense it erupts like fire.</note> in the sight of the Father's majesty; and all his words and works, kindled by the little spark of love, were fragrant with the praise and honor of the Father, with all thanksgiving. This much for the first step. Having risen in this step, Christ, the true sun, ascending to the second step, shone forth more brightly and more fervently. For...

<summary>This page begins a new section of the treatise, moving from spiritual illness to the imitation of Christ. It describes Christ as the "True Sun" who ascends through four spiritual degrees, starting with His incarnation and the union of His divine and human natures.</summary>
<keywords>Christology, the Four Risings, Divine Love, Incarnation, Trinity, Virgin Mary, spiritual light</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Christ as the archetype of spiritual ascent, moving from the first step (his incarnation and unity) into the second and third steps of his earthly life and divine mission.</meta>

->OF THE SPIRITUAL NUPTIALS<-

From the fullness of the grace existing in him and the multitude of gifts, his heart overflowed with kindness, his manner with gentleness, his conversation with humility, and every ministry with generosity. He was so gracious and lovable that the character of his conversation drew all men of good will to his love. He is the unstained lily of the valleys <note>original: *lilium convallium*. A biblical image from the Song of Solomon often used by medieval writers to describe Christ's purity.</note>, the flower of the field exposed to every traveler: from the fullness of whose sweetness, just like bees, the souls of the faithful receive the honeycombs of eternal sweetness and consolation. For all the gifts bestowed upon his humanity, he offered to his Father—the Father of lights, I say, from whom every best gift and every perfect gift descends <note>A direct reference to the Epistle of James 1:17: "Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, and cometh down from the Father of lights."</note>—a perpetual sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving, according to that nature in which he is less than the Father <margin>2nd step</margin> <note>This reflects the Athanasian Creed: Christ is equal to the Father in his divinity, but "lesser" (minor) in his human nature.</note>. And according to the highest powers of his soul, and in the supreme unity of the Godhead from which all gifts flow: he rested continually. This is for the second step.

But ascending the third step, CHRIST the most glorious sun shone more brightly and more hotly. For from the beginning of his conception until the day of his glorification, his sensory powers and his entire <term>sensuality<gloss>In medieval thought, "sensuality" (sensualitas) refers to the part of the soul responsible for physical senses and emotions, not necessarily carnal desire.</gloss></term> were invited and drawn by the Father toward those same glorious delights with which he is now satisfied—as if drinking fully from the torrent of divine pleasure—according to that same part of himself which has been glorified. And although he was moved toward these delights by a continuous appetite, both natural and supernatural, nevertheless, while stationed in the painful exile of this life according to this part, he wished to wait steadfastly for the time pre-envisioned and preordained by the great counsel of the Father. This is for the <margin>3rd step</margin> third step. 

But when the fullness of the preordained time arrived, in which CHRIST had decreed to gather all the fruits of all virtues performed and to be performed into the heavenly barn: he, the Sun of Justice, at the...

...tending toward his decline, by his very descent he climbed the fourth step. Because he was made obedient to the Father even unto death on the cross, he surrendered himself voluntarily into the hands of his enemies, abandoned by his friends and neighbors in his final hour of need. His sensory nature was also deprived of all interior consolation, with the heavy burden of insults, punishments, and the <term>just satisfaction<gloss>In medieval theology, this refers to the "payment" or atonement Christ offered to God's justice to pay for human sin.</gloss></term> for all sins <margin>4th step</margin> placed upon him. Carrying this with humble patience, weighed down by the mass of such a heavy bundle, he breathed out his blessed spirit upon the cross (just as he willed). And so, placed in this step of desolation, this faithful <note>original: *athleta fidus amoris*. A common early Christian metaphor comparing Christ or a martyr to an athlete training for and winning a grueling contest.</note> athlete of love performed strong and giant-like works of charity, by which he earned eternal salvation for us. Behold, by these four steps, according to the lower part of his humanity, our Bridegroom has been adorned. For according to that nature, he endured the shames of the passion for our sins. Therefore, a name was given to him which is above every name; he is called Savior of the World, and having been glorified and exalted above all things, he sits at the right hand of the Father to reign omnipotently for ages of ages, so that at his name every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth <note>This entire passage is a close paraphrase of Philippians 2:8–10, a cornerstone of medieval "imitation of Christ" theology.</note>.

->ON THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-

...from the fullness of grace existing in him and the multitude of gifts: his heart overflowed with kindness, his manner with gentleness, his way of life with humility, and every service with generosity. He is the unpolluted lily of the valleys, the flower of the field open to every traveler; from the fullness of whose sweetness all the industrious <note>original: *argumentosae*. For all the gifts bestowed upon his humanity, he himself offered a perpetual sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving to his Father—the Father of lights, I say, from whom

every best gift and every perfect gift <note>A reference to James 1:17: "Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights."</note> descends—according to that nature by which he is lesser than the Father. And according to the highest powers of his soul, and in the highest unity of the divinity from which all gifts flow down, he rested continually. This concludes the second step.

For from the beginning of his conception until the day of his glorification, his sensory powers and his entire <term>sensuality<gloss>In medieval theology, *sensualitas* refers to the part of the soul that perceives through the senses and experiences emotion. # THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS

From the fullness of grace existing in him and the multitude of gifts, his heart overflowed with kindness, his manner with gentleness, his conversation with humility, and every service with generosity. He was so gracious and lovable that the character of his way of life <note>original: *conversatio*. In this context, it refers to his social conduct and behavior among people.</note> drew all people of good will toward the love of him. He is the unpolluted lily of the valleys, the very flower of the field <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon 2:1, often applied to Christ in medieval mysticism to signify his accessibility and purity.</note> exposed to every traveler; from the fullness of his sweetness, all the industrious bees of the faithful receive honeycombs of sweetness and eternal consolation. For all the gifts conferred upon his humanity, he himself offered a perpetual sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving to his Father—the Father of lights <note>original: *patri luminum*. A reference to James 1:17: "Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights."</note>, I say, from whom every best gift and every perfect gift descends—
<margin>The 2nd step</margin>
according to the nature by which he is less than the Father <note>In traditional theology, Christ is considered equal to the Father in his divinity, but "less" than the Father in his human nature.</note>. And according to the highest powers of his soul, and in the supreme unity of the divinity from which all gifts flow down, he rested perpetually. This completes the second step.

But ascending the third step, Christ, the most glorious sun, shone more brightly and fervently. For from the beginning of his conception until the day of his glorification, his sensory powers and his entire sensory nature <note>original: *sensualitas*. In medieval theology, this refers to the capacity for physical feeling, emotion, and perception, rather than the modern connotation of "sensuality."</note> were invited and drawn by the Father toward those same glorious delights with which, according to that same part of himself, he—now fully glorified—is given to drink as if from the torrent of divine pleasure <note>original: *torrente divinae voluptatis*. An allusion to Psalm 36:8: "They drink their fill of the abundance of your house; and you give them to drink of the river of your pleasures."</note>. And although he was moved toward these delights by a continuous appetite, both natural and supernatural, nevertheless, while he was positioned in the painful exile of this life according to this part of his being, he chose to wait constantly for the time foreseen and preordained by the great counsel of the Father. 
<margin>The 3rd step</margin>
This completes the third step. When, however, the fullness of the preordained time arrived, in which Christ had decreed to gather all the fruits of all the virtues performed and to be performed into the heavenly storehouse, he himself, the sun of justice <note>original: *sol iustitiae*. A common title for Christ taken from the prophet Malachi, used here to signal the transition to his sacrifice.</note>, toward the set... <note>The Latin text ends mid-word with *oc*, likely part of *occasum* (setting) or *occidentem* (the west/sunset), symbolizing the beginning of his Passion.</note>

<margin>
The 4th step
</margin>
Turning toward his end, by his own descent he ascended the fourth step. For, having been made obedient to the Father even unto death on the cross, he voluntarily gave himself into the hands of his enemies, and was abandoned even by his friends and neighbors in his final moment of greatest need. His sensitive nature <note>original: *natura sensitiua*. In medieval philosophy, this refers to the part of the soul responsible for physical senses, emotions, and the capacity to feel pain.</note> was also deprived of all interior consolation, as a heavy burden of insults, punishments, and the just satisfaction <note>original: *iustitialis satisfactionis*. The theological concept that Christ’s suffering "satisfied" the debt of justice owed for human sin.</note> for all sins was imposed upon him. Carrying this with humble patience, burdened by the mass of such a heavy bundle, he breathed out his blessed spirit on the cross (as he willed). And so, placed in this step of desolation, the faithful athlete of love <note>The "athlete of love" is a common medieval metaphor for Christ, depicting his Passion as a heroic struggle or wrestling match against death and the devil.</note> performed strong and giant-like works of charity, by which he earned eternal salvation for us. Behold, in these four steps, according to the lower part of his humanity, our bridegroom was adorned; for according to that nature, he endured the shames of the passion for our sins. Therefore, a name was given to him which is above every name: called the **Savior of the world**, glorified and exalted above all things, he sits at the right hand of the Father to reign with all power forever and ever, so that at his name every knee shall bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth <note>A paraphrase of Philippians 2:9-10, emphasizing Christ's exaltation following his extreme humility.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the conclusion of the first spiritual advent (focused on the "lower" or sensory powers of the soul) to the second spiritual advent, which concerns the "higher" or intellectual powers of the soul. ...[here] he has already received the first spiritual advent of CHRIST; and through the internal exercise of the heart, with sensory unity <note>original: *sensuali unitate*. This refers to the integration of the lower faculties—the senses and emotions—so they are no longer scattered by worldly desires.</note>, and the vital divine gifts of the heart adorned with the best virtues, he has already gone out with spiritual steps to meet the bridegroom. Indeed, once the man is purified and tranquil, and deeply recollected into the unity of his own heart, he is now ready (whenever it pleases God) to be illuminated inwardly; which is granted to many in the beginning—especially to those who offer themselves whole and entire to the divine will, renouncing the freedom of their own will (which is the whole root of perfection <note>Renouncing the "proprię voluntatis" (own will) is a core tenet of medieval mysticism, seen as the essential step to allowing God to act within the soul.</note>). However, they must afterwards ascend the aforementioned steps and exercise themselves according to the prescribed patterns of moral and spiritual exercises. Yet they can fulfill this more easily than others who begin from the bottom, as they are led by the hand by a greater light.

### ¶ ON THE SECOND spiritual coming of CHRIST,
### likened to a fountain flowing into three streams. Chapter XXIX

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial "O" containing ornate, interlacing foliage and floral patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

Having shown, therefore, the first spiritual advent of our bridegroom, which adorned the lower powers of the soul through four steps, let us now, with grace as our guide, pass on to his second advent, which adorns, illuminates, and enriches the supreme powers <note>original: *supremas vires*. In medieval psychology, these are the "higher" faculties of the soul: the memory (as a vessel for God), the intellect, and the will.</note>. We wish to liken this advent to a fountain flowing with three streams. Truly, this fountain is the fullness of grace in the unity of the spirit, gushing forth by divine infusion. For there, grace holds itself as a source by remaining essentially within; and from there it is derived through three streams into the three intellectual powers of the soul, as if diffusing itself actively into three channels. And so, these three streams are certain singular, surpassing influences or divine operations in the su[preme powers...]

### ON the first stream, by which the memory is adorned. Chapter XXX.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring elaborate floral and foliage motifs.</image-desc>
Indeed, the first stream (when the Bridegroom comes in this manner) flowing into the spirit is a certain simple and uniform mental serenity, varied by no distinctions of forms <term>forms (Latin: *formae*): mental images, concepts, or sensory impressions that occupy the mind</term> whatsoever. This stream, originating from the aforementioned little fountain of grace, flowing down like water in the unity of the spirit from the highest to the lowest parts: it passes through all the channels of both the highest and lowest powers. Like the ebb and flow of the sea, returning to its origin, it lifts these powers above all multiplicity and the preoccupations of their duties, creating mental simplicity in the person, and shows and grants the unity of the spirit in the bond of peace. Thus, the person, according to the intellectual memory, is as if raised into ethereal serenity <note>original: *serenitatem ethereā*. A state of clarity compared to the pure, unclouded upper atmosphere.</note>, and is purified from the incursions of all forms and all temporal changeabilities. Therefore, our Bridegroom, **CHRIST JESUS**, coming into the spirit by the sailing of this little stream: demands an "outgoing" <note>original: *exitum*. In mystical theology, this refers to the soul "going out" of its own self-interest and sensory distractions to meet God in the center of the soul.</note> according to the manner and requirement of the inflowing light of grace.

Therefore, the person of this kind, "going out," finds himself by consideration of himself, through this simple and serene light infused into him, to be recollected, stabilized, penetrated, and anchored in the unity of his own spirit or mind, and established in a new degree of spiritual life more eminent than the previous ones. Having turned into himself, therefore, he fills the channel of his memory with the uniform purity of the mental ether <note>original: *mentalis etheris*. A metaphor for a state of consciousness that is clear and free from the "cloudiness" of worldly thoughts.</note>, with the incursions of all species <note>original: *specierum*. Here referring to mental images or sensory perceptions that "strike" the mind.</note> and the multitudes of all things having been entirely put behind and excluded. Here to

<summary>This page describes the first of three spiritual "streams" of grace that cleanse the memory, lifting the mind into a state of simple, image-free serenity. This process prepares the soul's higher powers for a deeper union with Christ by removing the distractions of worldly thoughts and mental images.</summary>

THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS

[Therefore, the person] inclines toward the unity of the spirit naturally and supernaturally. Thus, that same unity, through God's gifts and a simple intention, will be inclined toward that most excellent unity in which the Father and the Son, through the bond of the Holy Spirit, are united with all their saints. For through this, the soul, adorned according to the requirement of the first stream, has "gone out" <note>original: *exiuit*. In mystical theology, the "outgoing" is the soul's movement beyond its own limited self to meet God in the "unity of the spirit."</note> sufficiently.

### ¶ CONCERNING THE SECOND stream, which adorns the light of the intellect. Chapter XXXI.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter S with floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>Indeed, when the spirit inclines itself in this way toward the divine unity through love and a most serene intention, and the faithfulness of divine goodness breathes into it spiritually: there arises, from the fullness of grace flowing from the fountain-source in the unity of the spirit, a second stream—namely, a certain spiritual clarity <term>spiritual clarity (Latin: *spiritualis claritas*): a supernatural light given to the intellect to perceive divine truths beyond human reason</term>. This clarity, with its multiform influence, illuminates discernment, revealing the intellect and infusing a most brilliant truth into all the virtues. Nevertheless, the efficacy of this clarity is by no means subject to our own power. For although it may be continually present in our souls, God—according to the unsearchable pleasure of His will—reveals and hides it, gives and takes it away, and makes it keep silent or speak at His command, according to the time and place. For it is His own; therefore, He works in it when He wills, where He wills, what He wills, and for whom He wills. Those who are enlightened by the light of this clarity do not necessarily require revelations or supersensory attraction. For their life, conduct, dwelling, and entire inner disposition in the spirit exist above the realm of the senses; and there God reveals to them whatever is necessary for themselves or for others, as He wills. He could, however, if He wished, strip them of their external senses and, through various symbols, reveal <note>original: *apocalypsare*. A Latinized Greek term meaning to unveil or disclose in the manner of a prophetic revelation.</note> to them internally many things about the future in various ways. But in this stream of light, **CHRIST** coming in that very light...

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on the purification of memory and introduces the second stream of grace, which provides "spiritual clarity" to the intellect. This light allows for divine discernment and is governed entirely by God's will rather than human effort.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XXXI, following the description of the "second stream" of grace which clarifies the intellect. The text moves from the initial reception of this light to the soul's active contemplation of the nature of God and the Trinity.</meta>

->II<-

[He] commands him to go out and walk with this light as his guide. Therefore, let him go out, **full of light** through this radiance, observing his own state and his life, both inward and outward—specifically, whether he bears a perfect likeness of **CHRIST**, not only according to His humanity but also according to His divinity; for we were created in the image and likeness of God. Let him also raise his clarified eyes, with the assistance of enlightened reason, toward intellectual truth <term>intellectual truth (Latin: *intellectualis veritas*): spiritual reality perceived directly by the mind's eye through grace, rather than through physical senses or human logic</term>. Let him consider and behold the sublime nature of God with the personal properties and attributes of the Trinity, as far as the "created light" <note>original: *creatum lumen*. In mystical theology, this refers to the limited light of human reason and grace, which, while divine in origin, is still a "creature" compared to the infinite, "uncreated" light of God’s own essence.</note> is sufficient to show.

For it is not fitting for a nature of such immense goodness to grow sterile with a dead lack of fruitfulness; rather, it should grow rich with the virtues and works of unsearchable excellence. For the nature of the Godhead is considered and gazed upon as a certain immense simplicity, as an inaccessible height, as an inscrutable depth, as an incomprehensible breadth and eternal length, as a silent and tranquil darkness <note>original: *caligo*. Ruusbroec uses this to describe the "Divine Dark," a state where God is so bright that he appears as darkness to the limited human intellect.</note>, as a vast solitude and the eternal rest of the saints, and as the common enjoyment of Himself and of all good things. Many more wonders can be considered in this infinite **sea of the Godhead** <note>original: *pelago deitatis*</note>.

But who can bring forth sensible likenesses to express this contemplation, given the materiality and coarseness of the senses with which and through which we speak? Yet, in truth, the immense and most simple Good is beheld with intellectual eyes. Nevertheless, the intellectual Word itself—which is, in a certain way, "incarnated" in our voices—is clothed with as many members of likenesses as he who clothes the word with his voice is able to explain, according to the diligence of his enlightened reason. Let our enlightened intellect nonetheless contemplate the properties and attributes of the Persons <note>Referring to the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit as distinct persons within the one Godhead.</note>; for these things, in the light of this stream, are more clearly shown...

<summary>This page describes the soul's movement from self-examination to the contemplation of the Divine Essence, portrayed as an "infinite sea" and a "tranquil darkness." It emphasizes that while human language is inadequate to describe God, the enlightened intellect can perceive the attributes of the Trinity through grace.</summary>
<keywords>contemplation, Divine Dark, Trinity, intellectual truth, sea of the Godhead, image of God, Ruusbroec, mysticism</keywords>

And first indeed, let him consider the Father to be all-powerful: power and majesty, the creator, the preserver, the mover, the beginning and the end, the primary cause of all things created. Let him observe, secondly, that the Word of God the Father <note>original: "verbum dei patris"</note> is the only-begotten: the unsearchable wisdom and truth of all creatures, the exemplar and life of the Fatherly art <note>In medieval theology, the "Fatherly art" refers to the divine wisdom through which God "designs" and creates the universe</note>, the infallible rule, the eye of the Godhead <term>Godhead (Latin: *deitas*): the essential, divine nature of God, shared by the Trinity</term> seeing through all hidden things, the brightness of the glory of the Father, illuminating His saints and His elect according to the merits of each. Let him attend thirdly, with the gaze of illuminated reason, to the Holy Spirit: the incomprehensible breadth of charity, the inestimable mercy of piety, the benevolence of immutable faithfulness, the overflowing wealth of generosity, the unsearchable sea of goodness, the never-failing stream of honey-sweet delight that inebriates the citizens of heaven, the fiery flame reducing all things to the ash of deifying unity <note>original: "deificæ vnitatis". This refers to the mystical process where the soul's ego is "burned away" to be united with God.</note>, the living fountain eternally flowing with every delight of sweetness, the preparer and guide to the joy of eternal happiness, the bond and embrace of the Father and the Son and of all the saints in the enjoyment of the delights and riches of the Godhead. All these things, expressed through many names <note>original: "plurificata vocabulis"</note> within the most simple nature of the divinity, are seen uniformly and indivisibly; and yet, according to the small measure of our understanding, they are distinguished in multiple ways according to the distinction of the Persons. For goodness, bounty, majesty, and truth—among these, according to the concepts of our mind, there is a very great distinction; although in the divine nature they are simply one. Nevertheless, the personal relations <term>personal relations: the specific ways the Father, Son, and Spirit relate to one another (e.g., begetting and being begotten)</term> differ among themselves by an eternal distinction. For the unbegotten Father <note>original: "pater ingenerabilis"</note> always begets the Son. The Son indeed, being always begotten, does not beget another son, so that He may be eternally the only Son of the only Father. Therefore, fatherhood and sonship relat[ions]...

<summary>This page details a mystical contemplation of the Trinity, assigning specific attributes to the Father (power), the Son (wisdom), and the Holy Spirit (goodness/love). It explains the theological paradox of how God can be "simple" and "one" while also being experienced as three distinct Persons with different relations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of the Persons of the Trinity. This page picks up with "relationships" (Latin: *relationes*), completing the sentence regarding how the Persons are defined by their relation to one another.</meta>

relationships: by which the Father and the Son are referred to one another. Furthermore, the Father and the Son breathe forth a single Spirit, the bond of the will and charity of both; who indeed neither begets nor is begotten, but is eternally breathed forth from both. The relationship by which He is referred to them is called **procession** <term>Procession (Latin: *processio*): The theological term describing the "coming forth" of the Holy Spirit from the Father and the Son, distinct from the "begetting" of the Son.</term>. These three Persons are one God and one Spirit: to whom individual attributes along with their works are shared communally without division, as they operate in the power of one most simple nature.

### ¶ ON THE WONDER OF DIVINE
### communication. Chapter XXXII.

Indeed, the incomprehensible depth of the riches and the bountiful communication of the divine nature leaves anyone stunned above all else with wonder at its principal communication and outflowing—provided that person has been lifted up in admiration of divine works by an intellect illuminated by a divinely given light. For such a person contemplates the incomprehensible essence of the Godhead <term>Godhead (Latin: *deitas*): the essential, divine nature of God shared by the Trinity</term>, the shared source of enjoyment for God and all the blessed. He gazes upon the divine Persons communicating <note>original: "communicare". In this context, it refers to God "imparting" or "sharing" His own essence and gifts with His creation.</note> themselves through their outflows and works to all things, in every state and time, in heaven above and on earth below—to intellectual, rational, and irrational, material and immaterial creatures—according to the dignity, capacity, and need of each. God is **common** <note>original: "communis". Meaning "shared" or "available to all," implying that God does not withhold Himself from any part of creation.</note> to all with all His gifts. The angels of God are common. The rational soul also communicates itself with all its powers to the entire body and all its members; existing entirely in the whole and in every single part; equally whole according to its essence, which is altogether simple and indivisible; according to its reason...

Nevertheless, the personal relations differ among themselves by an eternal distinction. <meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 32 regarding the divine nature's communication to creatures. <header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...[It is] not, however, having parts in any way. For just as by the sharp sword of the word of God <note>A reference to Hebrews 4:12: "For the word of God is alive and active. Sharper than any double-edged sword, it penetrates even to dividing soul and spirit..."</note>, the spirit and the soul are divided within the one essence of the soul: so indeed the powers of the spirit are distinguished from the faculties of the soul and from one another individually. By the symbol of this truth, God bestows specific and particular perfections upon individual creatures; and yet, He cares for all things collectively and equally. For from Him, through Him, and in Him, all things are created, preserved, and depend upon Him universally, essentially, and equally. Therefore, as the mind contemplates the marvelous riches of the divine nature, the excellence of His majesty, and the manifold gifts which He grants and displays to His creatures, there is first born within it an unspeakable wonder. And from this wonder arises a sublime confidence in the divine goodness, and a certain singular, internal joy of the spirit, which penetrates and embraces all the powers of the soul and the very unity of the spirit.

By this stream, as if by a fiery torrent of love, the Bridegroom CHRIST, coming into the spirit, says: "Go out <note>original: "exite"</note> in spiritual exercise according to the manner of my coming." and the simple memory is elevated above the assaults of the senses and the imagination <note>original: "phantasticos," referring to the mental images produced by the lower mind</note>, and is stabilized by the unity of the spirit. Through the second stream, which is a certain infused clarity, the intellect and reason are divinely illuminated to recognize any exercise of the virtues and to penetrate the secrets of Holy Scripture with the sharpness of discernment. Through the third stream, however, which is an infusion of spiritual fire, the will is kindled with silent and tranquil love and is endowed with magnificent riches. He, therefore, into whom these streams are infused, becomes spiritual and luminous with divine light. For grace, springing up in the unity of the spirit like a living fountain and flowing out through the streams of the soul’s powers, manifests the efficacy of its fruitfulness through the wealth of the virtues; but it demands the reflux <note>The "ebbing" or flowing back of the soul's energy toward God, its source</note> of all these streams to the place of their origin.

## ¶ ON THE FOURFOLD exit: by which one goes out at this stage.
### Chapter 34.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'M' inside a square frame, embellished with intricate foliate and floral scrolls. At the bottom center of the letter 'M', there is a small, stylized human face.</image-desc>

Therefore, let the spiritual man remain in the unity of his spirit, stabilized by the bond of love; and going out with the step of illuminated reason and the overflowing abundance of charity, let him traverse heaven and earth. Let him consider all things with luminous discernment, and with true largeness of heart—relying on divine riches—let him show himself generous to all. For he is inclined to go out in four ways—not so much because it is divinely demanded, as by the habits of his own virtues. And indeed, going out in the first way into heaven, he ascends in mind; he visits and surveys the state and glory of God and of the blessed. In the second way, going out with the compassion of mercy, he visits sinners and those who love the delights of the world too perversely. Going out in the third way, he descends mourning into purgatory, visiting the souls begging for the intercessions <note>original: "suffragia," referring to the prayers and good works of the living offered on behalf of the dead</note> of the living. In the fourth way, going out, he finally remembers himself and all the elect <note>term: "elect," those chosen by God for salvation</note> dwelling on earth. These things in...

<header>THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...these things have been said in a general sense. Now, however, let us investigate the business of each individual departure in detail.

### ON THE BUSINESS of the first departure. Chapter 35.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring intricate floral and vine patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>**P**rovided with this first departure, therefore, let a person ascend into heaven by the contemplation of luminous reason <note>original: "luminose rationis"; this refers to reason enlightened by divine grace rather than mere human logic</note>, beholding the glory of God and the blessedness of the saints. Let him take notice of the brilliant and opulent **outflowing** <note>original: "effluentia"</note> of God, by which He pours over and inebriates the blessed spirits, not only with the abundance of heavenly delights but also with the sweetest goodness of His own essence. Let him nonetheless take notice of the **flowing back** <note>original: "refluentia"</note> of all good things into that very fountain of unity from which the torrent of divine pleasure flows; they pour back in happy charity not only the gifts received and the goods they have gained, but also their very selves. For the divine outflowing always seeks the return-flow of its own stream. For God is like a certain **sea** <note>Ruusbroec frequently uses the metaphor of the sea and its tides (flux and reflux) to describe the dynamic relationship between God and the soul</note>, continually making a flux and reflux: pouring the waters of glory or grace into the minds of His elect according to the dignity or need of each, and reabsorbing them along with their channels to the very last drop, even with interest. From some, He even demands more than their strength is sufficient to provide. For, showing Himself to the eyes of reason as the one of supreme richness, generosity, and infinite goodness, He demands by that very showing of Himself to be honored, praised, and loved according to His own dignity. For God wants to be loved by us according to the magnificence of His own dignity. Because no creature at all is able to achieve this, every spirit fails in this divine salvation; yet, desiring to pay what is asked, love—striving with its utmost strength—exceeds its own measure. And wandering in that very excess of the mind, it loves without measure, wishing with all its heart but unable to complete the work...

<sig>g</sig>

<summary>This page describes the first 'departure' of the soul, using the metaphor of a divine tide (flux and reflux) to explain how God's grace flows into the soul and how the soul attempts to return that love. It emphasizes that the soul’s love for God necessarily 'exceeds its own measure' because God’s dignity is infinite and cannot be fully matched by any created being.</summary>

<keywords>contemplation, flux and reflux, divine grace, departure (exitus), mysticism, Jan van Ruusbroec, reason, celestial glory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s first "departure" into the heavens. On the previous page, the author described the "flowing out" of God's grace and the "flowing back" of the soul's love. This page picks up mid-sentence, contrasting the infinite power of God with the finite nature of created beings, and how love attempts to bridge that gap.</meta>

<header>II</header>

...of infinite power, since the power of all creatures is finite. Yet love, impatient and knowing no measure, receiving neither solace from impossibility nor remedy from difficulty, weakened by its own deficiency yet resuming its strength, is renewed in its whole spirit toward the work it has undertaken: namely, so that God may be loved according to what He requires and according to the desire of love. 

For this reason, all the blessed spirits are continually gathered into one flame of love, so that they might be able to complete, at least in some way, this required and desired work—namely, to love God according to the fitness of His dignity (which no one is sufficient to fulfill alone)—at least by all being joined together in one flaming wedge <note>original: "cuneũ" (cuneus). This is a military term for a V-shaped or "pig-head" formation used to break through enemy lines, here used to describe the collective force of the saints' love piercing toward God.</note> of love. 

However, reason manifests clearer than light that this will always be impossible for any creature. But love, by no means despairing because of this impossibility, chooses one of two paths: either to complete the work it has undertaken, or, melted by its own super-fervent heat, to be utterly annihilated in its own failure. Yet it achieves neither: for love itself is not reduced to nothing, and yet God is always loved by all creatures in a way that falls short <note>original: "deficiter"; the soul constantly "fails" to love God as much as He is worth, creating a perpetual state of longing.</note> of His true dignity. 

In this, however, luminous reason rejoices with wonderful pleasure: because God, its beloved, is so rich in Himself and of such most excellent majesty, that He exceeds all created power infinitely. Thus, He is loved by no one else according to the greatness of His dignity except by His own proper love. 

Furthermore, the spiritual person, rich in the light of reason, should dedicate ceremonies of love <note>original: "encęnia amoris"; encaenia refers to the dedication of a temple or an anniversary festival.</note> to all the choirs of blessed spirits—individually to each according to the merit of their dignity, and collectively to them all. He draws what he may liberally bestow both from the treasury of the failing <note>original: "deficientium"; this likely refers to the "poverty" of the creature's own resources when compared to God.</note> riches of God, and from the chest of his own wide-flowing mind, which overflows with the wonderful gifts of God. Let him fly everywhere around all the ranks, states, and merits of the blessed, contemplating the divine mansions in each one according to the dignity of their merits. And he himself, rich and overflowing with grace...

<header>ON THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS</header>

By this first departure toward the rising of the eternal sun, let the soul ascend under the guidance of the "love of the beauty of the house of God and the place of the dwelling of His glory." <note>original: "dilectione decoris domus dei & loci habitationis gloriæ eius," a reference to Psalm 26:8, expressing the soul's longing for heaven.</note>

## ¶ CONCERNING THE TASK of the second departure. Chapter 36

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'S' entwined with vine and floral motifs.</image-desc>In the second departure, moving with the steps of the mind toward the region of the north <note>In medieval spiritual geography, the "North" often symbolized the coldness of sin or the dwelling of the devil.</note>: let the soul visit sinners with mournful compassion and honey-sweet mercy. Carrying them on the vehicle of devoted prayer, let the soul present them to divine mercy; let it testify that His immense goodness and powerful, all-encompassing majesty have given much and promised even more—bringing these things back to the memory of God, as if He had forgotten them. For He Himself wishes to be asked, and **Charity** <term>Charitas: the highest form of Christian love, focused on the well-being of others and God.</term> patiently endures delays in obtaining all she desires. 

Nevertheless, the soul is confounded by its own persistence and boldness; yet it commits everything to the rich and most generous goodness of God, who loves the fullness of immense love and hates nothing of what He has made. In this, they are content in a wondrous peace. Therefore, because the spiritual man carries the inward heart of universal charity, he devoutly prays to God in words and desires that He might pour out the grace of His mercy upon the Jews, the Gentiles, and all the unfaithful of the world. Thus, He who is the giver of graces may be magnificently praised and honored by the angels rejoicing in heaven over the conversion of many sinners, and our glory, peace, and joy may be increased to the ends of the earth. 

This is the task of the second departure, turning toward the north: where he who used to rise in the morning placed his throne and became **Lucifer**, the king over all the children of pride, under whose tyranny blind sinners are miserably oppressed.

<header>II</header>
<sig>g iiij</sig>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring a cross design with foliate flourishes within a square frame.</image-desc>In the third departure: let the person endowed with spiritual light descend to **Purgatory** <term>Purgatorium: in medieval theology, a place of purification for souls who died in a state of grace but still require cleansing from the effects of sin.</term> and visit the souls of the faithful departed. Let him consider in his mind their bitter punishments and their longing wait for divine mercy. Therefore, let him cry out for mercy and divine bounty with the voices of both heart and mouth, representing to the rich goodness of God their painful exile and their breathless longing for their **homeland** <note>original: "patriam," referring to Heaven as the true home of the soul.</note>. Let him plead that they departed from this world with **Faith**, **Hope**, and **Charity** <term>Charitas: the highest form of love, directed toward God and neighbor.</term> as their companions, and that the entire confidence of their liberation is established in the remedy of His passion and mercy. Among these things, however, it should be known that a spiritual person can sometimes be moved by the **Holy Spirit** to pray specifically for a sinner, for a soul, or for some special business. This is done to such an extent that he clearly knows and perceives it to be an instinct of the Holy Spirit, and not a natural affection or a mere readiness of the will. In such a case, he is inflamed with such devotion in asking and such persistence in prayer that he receives a spiritual response that his prayers will be heard by the Lord. By that very fact, the impulse of the Holy Spirit ceases, and the prayer, having reached the certainty of its attainment, is concluded. These things are said briefly regarding the task of the third departure, which tends toward the common end of mortal life. He to whom **Charity** has given a wider capacity will surely follow this out more extensively.

¶ Concerning the task of the fourth departure. <column-break/> Chapter 38.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'Q' (graphically represented by a 'V') with floral and leaf patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>In the fourth departure: turning his path toward the midday <note>In the spiritual geography of this text, "Midday" or the "South" represents the warmth and light of the Holy Spirit and those living in a state of active grace.</note> of a lighter breeze, let the person clear with spiritual light visit himself and all people of good will dwelling on earth. Because these people shine with the splendor of **Faith**, grow mild through the consolation of **Hope**, and burn with the heat of **Charity**, we rightly say they dwell in the southern region of heaven. Visiting these, therefore, let him likewise "taste and see" <note>A reference to Psalm 34:8, "O taste and see that the Lord is good."</note> their communion and...

<header>OF THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS</header>

...desiring with a single heart of **charity** <term>Charitas: the highest form of love, which seeks the good of others and is directed toward God.</term> and with all your **innermost being** <note>original: "praecordiis," literally the parts around the heart, signifying the depth of one's soul.</note>, you call upon God with the voice of prayer. You ask that He shower them with the usual gifts of His overflowing mercy: that He might confirm them in the necessity of His love and the stable covenant of charity, for the glory and honor of His most excellent majesty. Let [the spiritual person] therefore teach, correct, and serve everyone faithfully according to the line of **discretion** <note>In mystical theology, "discretion" is the ability to discern the correct course of action or the true nature of a spiritual impulse.</note>. For one who is full of the maternal inner-feelings of common charity is worthily held by God as a mediator for all and a faithful advocate in all things.

Within himself he is also whole and integrated; gathered with the most sweet society of all the chosen, let him possess the **unity of his spirit in the bond of peace**. And not only that, but let him inherit the sublime unity of the **Godhead** <note>original: "deitatis," referring to the essence of God.</note>, in which every spirit praising the Lord truly rests. This is truly the spiritual and **affective life** <term>Affective life: a stage of the spiritual journey focused on the transformation of the will and the emotions through love for God.</term>. For through these things, the spirits and souls of the righteous, and all the powers, habits, and acts of the virtues, are supernaturally adorned according to all that is fitting.

### ¶CONCERNING CERTAIN PEOPLE WHO SPEAK LOFTILY: BUT LACK COMMON CHARITY. Chapter XXXIX.

<image-desc>A square woodcut decorative initial letter "E" featuring detailed floral scrollwork and foliage designs.</image-desc>

THERE ARE, however, to be found certain people who are rich in subtle words, bringing forth high knowledge with a clear flow of eloquence; yet they have by no means reached this level of spiritual clarity, nor have they perceived the flavor of this common charity, seasoned by the bounty of the Spirit. Therefore, so that such people may become known to themselves and to others, we will now note them by three points; first, they shall become manifest to themselves, and in the remaining points, they will become clear to others who understand. First, indeed, to a spiritual man enlightened by divine clarity, **simplicity**...

<header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...with a single heart of love <note>original: "charitatis," often translated as charity, but in this context meaning the highest form of selfless, divine love.</note>, desiring with all his heart and calling upon God with the voice of prayer, so that, bedewing them with the customary gifts of His flowing piety, He might strengthen them in the bond of His love and the stable covenant of charity, for the glory and honor of His most excellent majesty. Entirely whole within himself, gathered with the most sweet company of all the elect, let him possess the unity of his spirit in the bond of peace <note>A reference to Ephesians 4:3, "endeavoring to keep the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace."</note>. And not that alone: but let him also inherit the sublime unity of the Godhead, in which surely every spirit praising the Lord finds rest. This is truly the spiritual and affective life. For through these things, the spirits and souls of the righteous, and all the powers, habits, and acts of the virtues, are adorned supernaturally according to the fittingness of all things.

<header>II</header>
<sig>g iiij</sig>

...ty and the stability of his spirit dwelling within him <note>original: "incolente," referring to the internal presence of the Holy Spirit providing a firm foundation.</note>, receiving the influx of truth without the wandering discourse of reason under the divine light: they, on the contrary, are inwardly difficult <term>difficult (discholi): from the Greek *dyskolos*, describing someone who is hard to please, fretful, or intellectually restless</term> in their complexity, full of curious study, and occupied with the inquiries of reason, never gathered into the rest of internal unity. Secondly, while for the illuminated man, divine light possesses an infused wisdom <term>infused wisdom (sapientiam infusam): knowledge given directly by God's grace rather than earned through human study</term> within him which shows the truth clearly, point by point, without any labor: that eloquent speaker fashions for himself subtle phantasms <note>original: "phantasmata," referring here to mental images or intellectual constructs that can distract from pure spiritual truth.</note>, which he turns over and over, composing and dividing, imagining and dictating ingeniously what he might bring forth eloquently, but emptily <note>original: "ventose," literally "windily," implying words full of air but lacking substance.</note>. For he is neither rich in the wealth of wisdom, nor a generous dispenser of doctrine.
