It never leads toward spiritual unity; but by forcing in subtle thoughts, it leads rather toward a fragmented state <note>original: "multiformitaten," the opposite of the simple "unity" sought in the contemplative life.</note>. Those who are like this stubbornly bring forth and defend their own doctrine and their own opinion, even if another's is sounder or equal. In every exercise of the virtues, they grow numb with negligence and lack of care; and in all their behavior, they swell with spiritual pride <note>original: "elatione," an uplifting of the self in arrogance.</note>.

Thirdly, while for our illuminated man common charity <term>common charity (communi charitate): the "Common Life" is a key theme for the author, where a person lives both in the heights of God and in the service of their neighbor simultaneously</term> flows out into heaven and earth (as we said before): this "high-talker" is singular and self-absorbed in all things; he considers himself wiser and better than others. And therefore he wants himself and his doctrine to be highly valued above others. Whatever he has not taught, he thinks untaught; and what he has not advised, he thinks unadvised. Those who do not emulate his ways, he judges as lacking in character. And those who do not follow him as a leader, he deems to be wandering in error. In taking up the necessities of the body, he is not frugal; he passes, for the most part, toward plea[sure]...

<header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...poverty; and he utterly neglects small defects and considers them as nothing. Such a person is certainly neither just, nor humble, nor generous, nor a minister to the poor, nor burning with deep devotion. He is not endowed with internal diligence, nor affected by a sensible love of God, and he possesses no true knowledge—shaped by the virtues—of either God or himself. Let everyone carefully weigh these three points; let him avoid, correct, and condemn them both in himself and in others. Yet, let no one judge rashly that any person is like this, unless manifest works have revealed him to be so. For a vain judgment, fashioning lies about a matter and staining a healthy heart, prevents one from seeing divine things.

### ¶ On the commonality of **CHRIST** as a model of common charity. Chapter 40.

<image-desc>A large woodcut decorative initial 'V' featuring ornate floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

In order that we may ascend to this chief grade of common charity <note>original: "communis charitas." In this context, "common" does not mean "ordinary," but rather "universal" or "shared"—a love that is available to everyone and excludes no one.</note>—and that we may uniquely possess it as something more desirable and higher than all the grades we have previously set down—let us set before ourselves **JESUS CHRIST** as our primary exemplar. He was, is, and shall be forever "common," sent into the world for the common benefit of all who wish to be partakers of his communion. 

Now, when he said that he was not sent except to the lost sheep of the house of Israel <note>A reference to Matthew 15:24.</note>, this is not to be understood as referring to the Jews alone, but to all those who are to see the glory of God in the heavenly homeland. For we believe that these alone belong to the "house of Israel" <term>house of Israel: The author uses a traditional medieval allegorical interpretation where "Israel" is translated as "the man who sees God," applying the title to all the elect rather than just an ethnic group</term>. Indeed, because the Jews despised the Gospel, the apostles turned toward the Gentiles. Because the Gentiles received the preaching and the Gospel of **CHRIST**, they entered the Church. In this way, "all Israel" was saved—that is, all those who were elected before the foundation of the world. 

Now, therefore, let us inquire how **CHRIST** shows himself to be "common" to all in faithful charity. In the first place, there was his devoted prayer, which (as he taught) he always practiced, and which penetrated the heavens; this prayer...

<summary>This page concludes a warning against intellectual pride and introduces Jesus Christ as the perfect example of "common charity," interpreting the "house of Israel" as the universal community of the elect.</summary>
<keywords>common charity, Jesus Christ, House of Israel, allegory, elect, pride vs virtue, universal salvation</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 40 regarding Christ as the ultimate model of "common charity"—a love that is shared universally. It concludes the description of Christ's selfless life and begins Chapter 41, which applies these principles to the structure and resources of the Church community.</meta>

<header>II</header>

...always ascending to the Father’s presence, he effectively carried out the common business of all who wish to be saved. He was, moreover, "common" <note>original: "communis." Here, the author emphasizes that Christ's virtues were not private or exclusive, but were lived openly for the benefit of all.</note> in his fervent love, in his truthful teaching, in his gentle correction, in his sweet consolation, in his generous giving, and in his kind and merciful forgiveness. His body and soul, his life and death, and his entire ministry were shared by all. Even now, his sacraments and gifts are for everyone. He never took a morsel of bread, a sip of drink, or any other bodily necessity without the intention of benefiting all who seek salvation. He possessed absolutely nothing of his own; rather, all things were shared by all: his soul with his body, his mother with his disciples, and his cloak with his tunic. He ate for our sake and drank for our sake; he lived for us, and for our sake he endured death. Nor should we overlook that remarkable mark of love: that through his overflowing charity, he shared the fruit and merit of his most blessed passion <note>The "passion" refers to the suffering and crucifixion of Jesus.</note> with those who believe in him, and he will eternally share the glory and honor of his magnificent triumph. As a brave champion, he reserved for himself alone the agonies of that cruel death, which he alone endured.

### ¶ On the community of the church. ->Chapter 41.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'C' with intricate floral and vine-like scrollwork.</image-desc>
**CHRIST**, when passing from this world to the Father, faithfully entrusted his treasure and his annual inheritance—namely the sacraments and the spiritual goods of the church which he won through the labors of his death—to his stewards <note>In a medieval context, these "stewards" are the clergy and officials of the Church who manage its resources and rituals.</note>, to be shared with his family of believers. Indeed, the goods of **CHRIST** and his ministers, sustained by the hardships of his shared passion, are dedicated to the common good. To speak of specific cases: all people devoted to divine worship, whether they belong to a religious order or live outside of one, who live on the offerings and alms of the faithful—whether in hermitages <note>original: "reclusoriis." Small, secluded dwellings for religious hermits.</note> or in monasteries, or any other religious houses—

<vocab>Christ, ecclesiastical community, sacraments, passion, utility, monastery</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the meditation on Christ as the perfect example of universal, shared love and begins a new chapter on how the Church's resources and sacraments are communal property intended for the benefit of all faithful people.</summary>
<keywords>Christ, common charity, sacraments, ecclesiastical goods, community, passion of Christ, monastic life, alms</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 41 regarding the communal nature of the Church. The previous page established Christ as the ultimate model of shared love and began to apply this to the ecclesiastical structure, emphasizing that the Church's resources and spiritual gifts were intended for the benefit of all.</meta>

<header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...dwelling together are undoubtedly bound to serve the common good, at least through the support of their prayers. In the beginning of the religious life of the Church, the supreme pontiffs, bishops, priests, and others assigned to various offices in their proper order were "common" <note>original: "communes." Here, it means they were accessible to everyone and lived for the public good rather than private interest.</note> to all. By teaching the people, they built up the Catholic Church; and through the faith of **CHRIST**, they strengthened it with an eternal defense using words and writings that were reddened <note>original: "rubricatis," referring to the practice of using red ink for titles or important headings, but here metaphorically suggesting they were "signed" with the blood of martyrs.</note> by their own blood. The Church shines with their doctrine just as the moon reflects the sun. These men, simple in spirit and pure of heart, possessed a stable peace in the unity of their spirit. Illuminated by divine wisdom, rich in faith, and fervent in charity, they faithfully ministered to God and to humanity.

But now, matters have turned to the contrary. Indeed, the gold of splendid charity has been darkened. The finest color of the virtues has changed. The "stones of the sanctuary" <note>A reference to Lamentations 4:1, used here to describe the spiritual leaders who should be the foundation of the Church.</note> in the spiritual building are scattered at the head of every street. For those who have been appointed over the family of God and who possess the patrimony <term>patrimony: the inherited property or wealth of the Church, originally intended to support the poor</term> of the Crucified One and the goods of the Church—which were bestowed upon the early fathers by the merit of their holiness and the impulse of charity—are called "stewards" <note>original: "dispensatores."</note>.

On the contrary, there are many today who are duplicitous, unstable, and utterly dissipated in spirit. Entirely devoted to worldly matters, they are ignorant of the peace of the spirit; they do not know purity of heart, nor do they understand or attend to the mysteries of their own offices, the sacraments of the Church, or the secrets of Holy Scripture. Because of this, praying only with their lips, they lick the "bark" of the sacred words, but they do not taste the "kernel" <note>A common medieval metaphor: the "bark" or "husk" is the literal surface of the text, while the "kernel" is the deep, spiritual meaning.</note> of spiritual understanding. And thus, with their senses dulled and blunted, deprived of the light of divine truth, they grow dark spiritually.

And because they are entirely devoid of spiritual delights, many of them hunt for the allurements of carnal pleasures—namely, in food, clothing, and drink, and other comforts of the body. One only wishes that their chastity (which we blush to speak ill of) were preserved. Truly, as long as they remain in this state—or rather, as long as they lie in this fallen condition—they will by no means deserve to be spiritually enlightened by divine light. However, just as the early fathers, who were fervent in charity...

<summary>This page contrasts the selfless, communal devotion of the early Church leaders with the perceived corruption and worldliness of the contemporary clergy, who focus on physical luxury over spiritual depth.</summary>

and flowing with generosity, they reserved nothing of worldly goods for themselves. In the same way now, some are, on the contrary, greedy and covetous; not like simple doves, but like hawks, violent and fraudulently predatory. These behaviors are contrary to the ways of the early Church fathers. This life is entirely opposed to the communal life (of which we spoke above). I do not condemn ecclesiastical ranks, but I rebuke the fault wherever it may be found. Let each person examine themselves; and if they find themselves at fault, let them correct, teach, and amend themselves. If, however, one is conscious of no wrongdoing, let them have joy, rest, and peace in the testimony of an innocent conscience. Praising God and serving Him faithfully, let such a person be useful to themselves and to all people for God’s sake.

### ¶ That CHRIST communicates himself in three ways in the sacrament of the Eucharist. Chapter 42.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'Q' featuring foliage and a stylized floral motif within a square frame.</image-desc>

Since we prefer this degree of communal life above all others, and so that the desire for it may be more deeply fixed in the hearts of those reading these words, let us present an exemplary grace: the precious dedication <term>dedication [original: *encenia*]. This term, derived from Greek, refers to a feast of dedication or a new gift marking a holy beginning.</term> entrusted by **CHRIST** to the priests of the Church, and bestowed upon His faithful for the common good. 

Before the feast day of Passover, **JESUS**—knowing that He was about to pass from this world to the Father—shared a supper with His disciples. Once the ritual of the paschal lamb and the Old Law was completed, He wished to offer a nourishing dish to His dear companions at the end of the banquet—for which reason He had long "desired to eat this Passover" <note>original: "desyderauerat hoc pascha... māducare." A reference to Luke 22:15.</note> with His disciples. So that He might inaugurate the New Law as the Old was ending, He took bread and the cup of wine into His holy and venerable hands. He consecrated His body from the substance of bread and His blood from the substance of wine. Giving it to His disciples for them to share together, He intended it, through them and their successors, for all who believe in Him until the end of the age. This most precious and soul-nourishing dish, which in heaven and on earth [is] blessed—

Those dwelling together are, without a doubt, bound to serve the common good, at least through the support of their prayers. In the beginning of the Christian religion, the supreme pontiffs, bishops, priests, and others appointed to various offices in their proper order were communal in spirit. By their teaching, they built up the Catholic Church; and through the faith of **CHRIST**, they strengthened it with an eternal defense using words and writings rubricated <note>original: *rubricatis*. Literally "reddened," referring to the practice of writing headings or important text in red ink; here, it metaphorically suggests their teachings were signed in their own blood through martyrdom.</note> with their own blood. The Church shines with their doctrine as the moon reflects the sun. These men, simple in soul and pure of heart, possessed a stable peace in the unity of their spirit. Illuminated by divine wisdom, rich in faith, and fervent in charity, they ministered faithfully to God and to their fellow men.

But now, the situation has turned to the opposite. Indeed, the gold of splendid charity has been darkened. The finest color of the virtues has been changed. The stones of the sanctuary in the spiritual building are scattered at the head of every street <note>This is a reference to the Book of Lamentations 4:1, a common medieval metaphor used to describe the corruption of the clergy and the ruin of the Church's spiritual structure.</note>. For these men, who are set over the family of God, possess the inheritance of the Crucified and the goods of the Church—which were bestowed upon the early fathers by the impulse of charity due to the merit of their holiness. They are called stewards <term>stewards [original: *dispensatores*]. In a medieval context, these were officials entrusted with managing the property and spiritual welfare of the Church on behalf of the poor.</term>.

On the contrary, they are many-hearted <note>original: *multicordes*. A term meaning double-minded or fragmented in purpose, directly contrasting with the "simple" (one-fold) hearts of the early fathers.</note>, unstable, and inwardly utterly dissipated. Entirely devoted to worldly affairs, they do not know the peace of the spirit; they are ignorant of purity of heart. They neither understand nor attend to the mysteries of their own offices, the sacraments of the Church, or the Holy Scriptures. For this reason, praying only with their lips, they lick the bark <note>original: *corticem*. The "husk" or "rind." A metaphor for focusing on the literal or outward ritual without understanding the inner meaning.</note> of the sacred words, but they do not taste the kernel of spiritual understanding. And therefore, with senses dulled and blunt, deprived of the light of divine truth, they are spiritually darkened. 

Because they are utterly devoid of spiritual delights, many of them hunt for the allurements of carnal pleasures—namely in food, clothing, and drinks, and other bodily comforts—would that their chastity (which we blush to defame) were preserved. Indeed, as long as they remain in this state, or rather as long as they lie in this fallen condition, they will in no way deserve to be spiritually enlightened by divine light. However, just as the early fathers, fervent in charity...

### ¶ With what meditation we shall be exercised when consecrating or receiving the sacrament of the Eucharist. Chapter 43.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'N' decorated with stylized acanthus leaves and floral patterns.</image-desc>
**WE** truly, whenever we have either consecrated or received the sacrament of the Lord's body, are commanded by Him whom we receive to do so in His memory. It is therefore worthwhile that we be taught (as grace inspires us) with what kind of meditation we should be engaged while consecrating or receiving this life-giving sacrament. First, it is necessary for us to consider how **CHRIST** inclines Himself toward us with loving affection, vital appetite, and intimate desire; and how He pours Himself into our physical nature with the entire flow of His heart, truly giving us that which He took from our humanity—namely, flesh and blood, and a completely bodily nature. 

Let us also reflect, meditating more vigilantly within our hearts, that this same precious body which we receive was pierced with cruel wounds for our sake, and slaughtered in a most atrocious <term>martyry</term> <note>A witness to faith through suffering and death.</note> unheard of in all previous ages, while the charity of His most faithful heart for our salvation inflamed Him to voluntary endurance. For by this meditation, according to the lower part of our humanity, we are fed and adorned by the glorious humanity of **CHRIST**. Let us therefore observe the blessed body itself...

For in this way, **CHRIST** received him.

He immerses himself with intimate affection in the sacred wounds, hiding himself in the open heart of his Savior. 

In this exercise, many hidden things are usually revealed, and special graces are granted. Indeed, the affection of compassion and the focus of the imagination on the wounds could be so vehement and so great that it would seem to the person that they feel the pains of the wounds of **CHRIST** in their own heart and in all their members. Thus, if the stigmata <note>original: *stigmata*; physical marks corresponding to the wounds of the crucified Christ</note> of the wounds were truly to be granted to any person in any way, he would certainly deserve to receive them through the efficacy of such compassion and the vehemence of his imagination. 

Therefore, with this preparation, we receive **CHRIST** according to His humanity as worthily as we can according to our small measure, and we honor Him with solemn reverence to the best of our ability. Truly, it nevertheless behooves us to inhabit the unity of our spirit, and—with clear discernment leading the way—to pour out the broadest charity <note>original: *charitate*; the highest form of Christian love</note> upon heaven and earth. Through this, we bear the likeness of **CHRIST** according to the spirit in whatever way possible, and we respond worthily enough to His coming according to our strength.

### How the unity of the divine nature is the originating cause of all spiritual activity. ->Chapter XLIV.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring intricate foliate and floral patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

The supreme super-essential <note>original: *supeſſentialis*; referring to God's existence beyond the category of "being" as understood by humans</note> unity of the divine nature—in which the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are joined in unity—possesses its own nature within the naked essence of our spirit, beyond the grasp and understanding of all our powers. That very unity (I say) is an infinite sea of the divine essence and a darkness of most excellent brightness <note>A classic mystical paradox where God's light is so blindingly bright it appears as darkness to the human intellect</note>, through which God, overshadowing all created light, exceeds every creature in the inestimable eminence of His dignity. 

This sublime unity of the divine nature is itself living and fruitful. For from it, as if from the womb of paternal fruitfulness, the eternal Word is generated without any shadow of change. Through this generation, the Father knows the Son, and the Son knows the Father and all things that are. The Father in the Son, as if in the immaculate mirror of His majesty, and the Son in the Father as if in his origin—seeing as they are of one simple nature—contemplate one another in mutual vision. And from this mutual sight of the Father and the Son, an eternal delight in one another is derived, and love...

co-essential and co-eternal, who is called the Holy Spirit and proceeds by a mutual breathing <note>original: *mutuo spiramento*; in medieval theology, "spiration" or "breathing" is the technical term for the way the Holy Spirit originates from the Father and the Son.</note>. Through this Holy Spirit, and through eternal wisdom, God the Father propagates Himself to every creature, though in different ways. He rewards each of the spiritually elect with gifts of grace and kindles them with the fire of charity <note>original: *charitatis*; divine, selfless love</note>, each according to the merit of their own dignity, as well as according to the rank of their state of life. In this state, through the mediation of the virtues, they are placed and chosen by divine predestination. For this reason, every elect spirit in heaven and on earth is directed and moved in virtue and justice.

### AN ANALOGY: how God, inhabiting the essence of the soul, causes spiritual life by His influence. ->Chapter XLV.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial "H" with floral flourishes within a square frame.</image-desc>We find a certain analogy for this matter in the natural world. For God created the Empyrean heaven <note>The highest, most "fiery" heaven in medieval science, believed to be the dwelling place of God and the angels.</note> to be pure, uniform, and bright. With its simple clarity, it surrounds all the other heavens and the material things contained within them. It exists as a kind of local habitation and bodily region for God and all the blessed, full of glory and eternal joy. And because this heaven is (as we have said) a certain eternal and uniform clarity, it is subject neither to time nor to motion, nor to any mutability at all; rather, it is established in perpetual immobility above all material, moving things. 

Immediately beneath this heaven is placed the "first movable" sphere <note>original: *primum mobile*; in the Ptolemaic system, this was the outermost moving sphere that pushed all the other planetary spheres.</note>. By its own motion—acting as the cause for moving all others—this sphere receives its power from the immobile uniformity and primary dignity of the Empyrean, with the power of God cooperating. For as its motion acts, the firmament and the other spheres of the planets are rolled around in a circle. By the influence of these motions, all things that possess a life capable of being born or decaying are nourished and grow, each according to its own kind. 

According to this likeness, the essence of the soul is a kind of spiritual region, full of a god-like clarity that exceeds the grasp of all the soul's own powers—unless perhaps (a point I will pass over for now) it is reached by the vision of simple intelligence. The essence therefore is...

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the Holy Spirit's role in guiding the elect and introduces a cosmological analogy, comparing the soul's essence to the unmoving Empyrean heaven which drives all other spiritual "motions."</summary>
<keywords>Holy Spirit, Spiration, Charity, Empyrean Heaven, Primum Mobile, Essence of the Soul, Ruusbroec, Medieval Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological analogy from Chapter 45, comparing the structure of the human soul to the medieval understanding of the heavens. It then transitions into Chapter 46, which introduces the third "coming" or advent of Christ to the soul, focusing on the highest state of spiritual union.</meta>

...of the soul, just like the Empyrean heaven <note>The highest, most "fiery" heaven in medieval science, believed to be the dwelling place of God and the angels.</note>: to which the unity of the spirit or mind is immediately joined, as if it were the First Mover <note>original: *primum mobile*; in the medieval cosmos, this was the outermost moving sphere that imparted motion to all the inner spheres (planets and stars).</note>. For in this unity, the spirit is first moved, both naturally and supernaturally, by the influence of the divine power dwelling in the essence of the soul. Since we possess nothing, whether natural or supernatural, from ourselves. However, this first movement of divine influence, when it moves supernaturally, is then the first and principal cause of all movement. And in this movement, to certain people enlightened by the grace of God, the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit are conferred, like seven planets illuminating their whole life with a special brightness and making it fruitful with honey-sweet charity <note>original: *charitate melliflua*</note>. This, therefore, is the way in which God—dwelling in the essential unity of the soul as if it were His own region—pours out the gifts of grace into the potential unity of the spirit and all the powers of the soul.

## Concerning the third spiritual advent of CHRIST, compared to the vein of a single fountain. Chapter XLVI.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring detailed scrolling vine and leaf patterns in a woodcut style.</image-desc>
Therefore, so that we may attain the supreme adornment of the spiritual life, which is accomplished by the third advent of the Bridegroom in the unity of our essence, and so that we may undergo the highest exercise of our spirit under the created light, lifting us to a higher degree—lest perhaps we labor in vain with the previous steps either broken or uncultivated—we must take the greatest care to observe whether we are fittingly adorned in the "outer man" by the moral virtues of the active life. We must also check whether we possess the unity of our spirit through the four steps of the first advent of the Bridegroom, having nobly climbed them by the steps of internal exercise. In this state, one is enlightened intellectually by supernatural wisdom in the divine bond of peace, drenching heaven and earth with a flood of generous charity, and—by clinging to it—flowing back <note>original: *refluentes*; the catchword at the bottom of the page suggests the sentence continues with the soul returning to its source.</note> into the abyss of divine unity from which all things flow, laden with the gifts of honor and reverence.

<summary>This page explains how God's grace moves the soul's "unity" like a celestial motor and introduces the third spiritual coming of Christ, emphasizing the need for a foundation in moral virtue before reaching higher mystical union.</summary>

<header>THE ORNAMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...beings, for every rational and intellectual creature, the more it is heaped with greater gifts from God, the more it is reflected back into its origin by a flow of love, a desire for tenacity and adherence, and a deeper gaze of penetration. This origin requires a flowing back <note>original: *refluxum*; a key concept in Ruusbroec’s mysticism where the soul returns to its source in God after receiving His grace.</note> from the favorable grace of its gifts; and we, provoked by the readiness of charity, the fullness of goodness, and the likeness of deiformity <term>deiformity: the state of being shaped into the likeness of God</term>, hasten to flow back into Him. **These** things being so: soon, by the breath of divine favor and the effect of His hidden operation, by the meeting of our burning love, and by the flowing of all our powers into the unity of the essence which He inhabits, the third spiritual advent of **CHRIST** arises, adorning us with a deeper and therefore more worthy exercise.

Indeed, this new coming is an extremely intimate touch of **CHRIST**, touching the inner recesses of our spirit with the brightness of His dignity. Having likened the preceding coming of **CHRIST** to a fountain flowing out in three streams, we wish to compare this one to the vein <note>original: *venae*; here referring to the underground source or "spring-vein" that feeds a fountain.</note> of the fountain. This is especially true since none of the streams arises except from the fountain’s overflowing, nor does any passage of the fountain’s waters overflow unless the bubbling of the living vein fills it.

For the grace of God (as we said before), holding the likeness of a fountain in the unity of the mind, flows into the supreme powers in streams of grace, habituating and forming them for the acts of the virtues. But grace itself springs forth like a living vein into that same unity from which it emanates, out of the living and most inexhaustible sea of the riches of God, which is most full of the faithfulness and piety of God. And this most hidden spring of grace is the third advent of **CHRIST**, which we designate by the name of "the touch." However, the spirit does not cooperate with this touch, but rather "suffers" it <note>original: *patitur*; in medieval theology, this means the soul is a passive recipient of God's action rather than an active participant.</note>, because the supreme powers are joined together by this touch in the unity of the spirit. There, God alone (who is the primary cause of all virtues and of our salvation) is operating. There, also, all human operation and reason fail. For even reason, by the divine light...

<summary>Jan van Ruusbroec describes the "third coming" of Christ as a mystical "touch" at the very core of the soul, where God acts alone and human reason is surpassed. This internal movement is compared to a hidden spring or "vein" that feeds the more outward "streams" of virtue and grace.</summary>

the power of love <note>original: *virtus amativa*; the soul's capacity to love and desire God</note> is clearer and greater: it feels this touch, even though reason is not able to comprehend the manner of it. For it is (as we have said) the operation of God alone; it is the bubbling spring and origin of all grace and all gifts, acting as the ultimate medium <note>original: *medij*; a bridge or middle point</note> between God and the creature. Truly, above this touch, in the silent and tranquil essence of the spirit itself, a certain incomprehensible brightness flashes forth. And this is the most excellent Trinity of the Godhead, from the overflow of whose riches this touch is caused. There, indeed, God reigns in the spirit and the spirit reigns in God.

### ¶ ON THE GOING OUT AND THE EXERCISE OF THIS STEP. Chapter XLVII.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter V, framed in a square border, entwined with elaborate vine and leaf patterns.</image-desc>**CHRIST**, coming truly in a spiritual way through this touch in the mind, thunders again with a new cry, as if at midnight, saying: "Go out to this exercise of the spirit," according to the manner and requirement of this touch. For this deep touch draws and incites our spirit to the deepest and most sublime spiritual exercise that can be performed under created light <note>The "created light" refers to natural human reason and the light of grace given to creatures, as opposed to the uncreated light of God’s own essence.</note>. Therefore, the spirit raises itself by the efficacy of the power of love, surpassing the business of every outward operation, into the unity of the mind—the place where the living vein of this touch bubbles up. Furthermore, this touch incites the intellect to know God in His brightness, and it draws and invites the affection to enjoy Him immediately <note>original: *fruendum*; a technical term for "fruition," the blissful, restful enjoyment of God in mystical union</note> through the power of love. This is what the spirit, loving God above all things, desires both naturally and supernaturally. Through the illuminated reason, the spirit exalts itself with an intimate gaze, contemplating the inner recesses of itself where this touch bubbles up with a most attentive view. rations and every created light <note>original: *creatum lumen*; the natural light of human reason or grace given to creatures</note>: in the scrutiny of this prospect, it fails and grows dim. Because the super-shining divine brightness <note>original: *claritas*; brilliance or divine clarity</note> which causes this touch, by its very meeting, darkens every created sight; for it exists as a splendor that beats back the sharp edge of every created light by its infinity. To this light, all intellects—even those illustrated by created light—stand in a spiritual relationship just as the eye of a bat <note>original: *vespertilionis*; a traditional medieval metaphor for the intellect's inability to look directly at the "sun" of divine truth</note> stands physically toward the brightness of the sun's light. Nevertheless, to scrutinize the origin of this touch, this much must be known: what God is, and what this touch is. The spirit is always incited by new stings <note>original: *stimulis*; goads or impulses</note>, both from God and from itself. And luminous reason, in inquiring what it is, and in scrutinizing the principle of this honey-flowing vein <note>original: *mellifluae venae*; a poetic image for the source of divine grace</note>, is always skillful and vigorous. But truly, the knowledge of the beginning of this scrutiny does not exceed the rational knowledge of the total investigation. For this reason, reason and all consideration are forced to cry out: "What this may be, I am utterly ignorant." For the super-shining divine brightness, by its meeting, beats back and darkens every created intellect. Thus, God shines over in a cloud of His brightness <note>original: *claritatis suae nube*; the concept that God is hidden from the mind by the very intensity of His light</note> in heaven, on earth, and in all spirits, as if before a lightning flash. But those who have penetrated above themselves, even into their own origin (which is the door of eternal life), through the sharpness of virtues and spiritual exercises, are able to feel this touch. For there, so immense a flash of divine brightness shines that all reason and every intellect fails and grows dull in its investigation; it is compelled to yield, suffering only the overshadowing of the incomprehensible divine brightness. But although reason and intellect are compelled to halt their step before the doors, being beaten back by the immensity of the divine brightness, the power of love <note>original: *amativa virtus*; the soul's capacity for loving and desiring God</note>—which, just as the intellect is invited to knowledge, is itself divinely invited to taste—desiring to follow the impetus of love, does not weary of proceeding. ## ¶ CONCERNING SPIRITUAL HUNGER. Chapter 48

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'V' inside a square frame, featuring intricate floral and vine patterns.</image-desc> Therefore, when the **will** (which we call the **power of love** <note>original: *amativā virtutem*</note>) persists with its greatest effort to complete the work it has begun, it encounters a certain insatiable spiritual hunger. This is an inward and impatient desire to grasp the **uncreated Good** <note>God Himself, who exists without being created</note>, even though the soul itself is a created thing. For since the spirit desires the "face" of divine enjoyment <note>original: *fruitio*; a technical term for the joyful, direct experience of God</note> above all things, and is drawn and invited to this by God, it naturally longs with all its strength to be filled. 

Here, then, arises this **spiritual ravenous hunger** <note>original: *bolismus*; literally a "bovine hunger," used here to describe an intense, medically-coded craving</note>—that is, a "canine" and insatiable hunger of the will, which strives to measure the immeasurable Good and to grasp the incomprehensible. It is as if a tiny little sea-fish strove to swallow the entire ocean at once; the efforts of both will be frustrated forever. These souls are the needy ones and the poorest of all. For, suffering a "canine" void and the thirstiness of parched sand, whatever they eat or drink, they are never satisfied. 

Because of this, this hunger is eternal: for a created vessel <note>The human soul</note> can never fully contain the uncreated Good. And so, since the infinite greatness of the Divinity exceeds every capacity of the spirit by its infinite magnitude, a hungry desire is established forever. Indeed, a table of rich foods and seasoned drinks—bountiful and overflowing—is prepared in the sight of the spirit; yet no one knows it except the one who receives it. But the full satisfaction of this enjoyment is itself the very dish that is desired, which calls every appetite to itself by its own sweetness. Yet, because of its sheer magnitude, it is not grasped by any appetite at all. And because love has this confidence—that it does not grow weary of its effort even after being frustrated a second or third time, but always prepares its strength anew after a failure—it also renews and increases that very hunger. From this...

<summary>This page explores the paradox of "spiritual hunger," where the soul's desire to "swallow" the infinite goodness of God leads to a state of eternal, ravenous longing that can never be fully satisfied by a created being.</summary>

...yet <note>This completes the word "however" or "yet" from the previous page.</note> the vivid vein is touched: and equally, honey-flowing streams of delights flow forth. For according to the multifaceted desire of the spirit, this touch brings with it the delights of taste. But because these things are performed within the creature's capacity under the immensity of divine greatness, therefore in this degree an **impatient hunger**—for which every created thing is not enough, nor is God sufficient <note>original: *nec deus ſuppetit*; meaning God cannot be fully "contained" or exhausted by the soul's capacity</note>—perpetually perseveres. Thus it happens that even if God were to grant most abundantly to this hungry spirit all the riches of the saints and whatever can be given to a creature, while keeping Himself alone back, that greedy, unrestrained, and insatiable hunger would nevertheless remain. Indeed, the internal **touch of God** <note>original: *tactus*; a technical term in mysticism for the direct, non-conceptual contact between God and the soul</note> causes hunger and greedy desire in us, with the divine Spirit inciting our spirit toward this. And therefore, the more vehement the touch, the greedier the hunger and the more impetuous the appetite. Truly, this is the life of love above reason and intellect, according to the business of its highest operation; because in this degree reason can neither bestow upon nor take away from love anything of value, since our love is touched by divine love. And to add something unique and from my own sense <note>Ruusbroec here shifts to a personal testimonial of his mystical experience</note>: I feel no divorce from divine charity will ever occur hereafter. Furthermore, the touch of God from that part by which He touches us and we feel Him, as well as our greedy desire of hunger—both are created things. And therefore, as long as we live, both can receive continual increase.

### CONCERNING THE CONFLICT OF DIVINE LOVE AND OUR OWN.
<margin>Chapter 49.</margin>

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'I' featuring intertwined foliage and vine motifs within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>
In this impetus of love, two spirits—namely God's and our own—conflict with each other. For God leans Himself into our spirit through the **Holy Spirit**; and from here, with the **finger of God** <note>A traditional biblical metaphor for the Holy Spirit's action</note> touching us, we are scorched by a little spark of love. Conversely, our spirit, moved by the divine touch and subjected by the power of love, impresses and inclines itself into God; and from here, God touches us back with a reciprocal touch.

<summary>This page describes the insatiable "spiritual hunger" that arises when the soul is touched by God, explaining that no created gift can satisfy the soul's desire for the Creator Himself. It then begins a new chapter on the "conflict" of love, where the Holy Spirit and the human spirit interact through a reciprocal touch.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual hunger, divine touch, Holy Spirit, mystical union, Jan van Ruusbroec, love beyond reason, spiritual conflict</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's mystical journey toward God. The previous page concluded with Jan van Ruusbroec describing an "impatient hunger" for the Divine that persists even when God grants great spiritual riches, as the soul can only be satisfied by God Himself. This page explores the "conflict of love" that arises from this intense spiritual longing.</meta>

60
->II<-
<margin>h iiij</margin>

Therefore, from these mutual touches, a **conflict of love** arises: namely, when in an inward meeting, God and our spirit encounter one another from both sides. And through this, by the penetrating points <note>original: *acuminibus*; referring to sharp, piercing spiritual insights or "stings" of desire</note> of love, each is equally wounded. Each, moreover—namely God’s Spirit and our own—shine through one another with mutual brilliance, the one showing His face to the other. This causes them to long for one another with a perpetual hunger. The one hungers for and desires the whole of the other, and the one offers and exhibits His whole self to the other. This makes the lovers, having been melted, float in the fire of love.

Furthermore, the touch of God and His giving, and our hunger and giving back <note>original: *relargitio*; the soul’s response of offering itself back to God in return for His grace</note>, confirm love with a stable solidity. This flow and this ebb <note>original: *fluxus/refluxus*; a central theme in Ruusbroec’s theology describing the rhythmic movement of the soul into God and back out into works of virtue</note> make the little fountain of love overflow; and the touch of God and the very greedy hunger of our love become one simple love. At this degree, the loving spirit is so possessed by love that, having forgotten itself, it knows absolutely nothing of all things except love. 

Thus the spirit is totally consumed by the fire of love; it penetrates the touch of God so deeply that—its own greed having been thoroughly pushed back and blunted—all its own operation is annulled and it is compelled to fail entirely. This very failure of work, being above all the "business" of active effort, emphatically becomes love. The spirit now possesses the inward, uniform **peak of its creation** <note>original: *apicem*; the "spark" or highest point of the soul where it is most like God</note> above every multitude of virtues, where all created works receive their beginning and end. This is love, reciprocal in itself, and equally the root and foundation of all virtues.

## ¶ CONCERNING THE HIGHEST DEGREE OF THE SPIRITUAL EXIT
## CHAPTER 50.

Truly, since our spirit is living and effective, and fertile with the offspring of virtues, the powers of the soul do not allow themselves to be idle in the unity of the spirit. For both the incomprehensible clarity of God and the divine...

This marks the transition to the "highest degree" of spiritual departure, where the soul finds the root of all virtue in its own essence.</summary>

<keywords>mystical union, conflict of love, divine touch, spiritual hunger, Ruusbroec, flux and reflux, apex of the soul, spiritual exit</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues Jan van Ruusbroec's description of the "conflict of love." The previous page described the soul's intense hunger for God and the mutual "wounding" of the spirit and the Divine as they encounter one another. This section concludes the discussion on the "third spiritual coming" and transitions to the fourth main article of the soul’s meeting with the Bridegroom.</meta>

THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS.

An infinite sea of love hangs over the spirit from above: it touches the power of love. And suddenly the spirit is restored to its former work: it is driven with a greedier hunger and a more gaping effort than before. And the more ardent, elevated, and enlightened it becomes, the more quickly the "business" of its activity is ended in the leisure of love, falling back into that same state by the renewed touch of God. This is truly a life that is beyond the world and heavenly. For the spirit, always desiring and hungering, presumes it can swallow <note>original: *glutire*; Ruusbroec often uses "eating" or "swallowing" imagery to describe the soul's desire to be completely absorbed by God</note> God. But the matter being reversed: the spirit is itself absorbed by the greatness of the divine touch, and deprived of its own work, it is itself made into love above every work. 

For in the unity of the spirit, the union of the highest powers is celebrated. There, grace essentially stands above all activity: existing as the fountain and origin of charity and all virtues. And just as the outflow of charity and virtues originates there, so too the inward flow <note>original: *refluxus*; the return of the soul's powers back to their divine source</note> of the powers occurs there through an internal hunger for tasting God, and an eternal dwelling within through simple love. These things, however, are carried out below God and within the grasp and manner of a creature. This, moreover, is the highest degree of spiritual exercise which can be possessed under a created light. Above this nothing remains except the intellectual and contemplative life under divine light and in a manner entirely godlike <note>original: *deiformi*; shaped by or taking the form of God</note>. And in this very exercise, neither error nor deception occurs. And pertaining to the "better part," begun in this pilgrimage <note>original: *peregrinatio*; referring to the soul's life on earth</note>, it will not be taken away in the fatherland <note>original: *patria*; a traditional term for Heaven, the true home of the soul</note>, but flowing eternally, it will be brought to perfection in happy glory. Let these things be said concerning the third spiritual coming.

## ¶ CONCERNING THE FOURTH / principal article: namely, concerning
## the meeting of the bridegroom. Chapter 51.

<meta>This page transitions from the third part of Ruusbroec's treatise to the fourth and final section. It summarizes the previous steps of the spiritual life—initial vision, the comings of the Bridegroom, and spiritual exercise—before defining the "meeting" (obviatio) between the soul and Christ.</meta>

II

<image-desc>Decorative historiated woodcut initial 'T' featuring a stylized cross and floral vine patterns within a rectangular frame.</image-desc>Therefore, having explained the three articles mentioned at the beginning of these books—namely, first, how anyone embracing the infancy of the spiritual life receives spiritual sight through God's grace for internal exercise (which we are exhorted to do when it is said, "Behold"); second, how the Bridegroom comes into us in three spiritual comings (one having four forms and the others being primarily uniform); and third, how, within almost a single context, one must go out into spiritual exercise according to the requirements and promptings of each coming—it now remains for us to treat the fourth article in the fourth place: namely, concerning the spiritual meeting with the Bridegroom. For all our spiritual vision, whether under the light of grace or of glory, and every zealous outward movement of any exercise, is established for no other reason, and is undertaken for no other cause, than that we may meet the Bridegroom **CHRIST** our Lord and be joined to him by the bond of love <note>original: *amoris glutinio*; literally "the glue of love," a favorite medieval metaphor for the intensity of mystical union</note>. For he himself is our eternal peace; he is the end and the reward of all our labor. Your Charity <note>a traditional way of addressing a religious audience, similar to "your Graciousness" or "dear friends"</note> knows that a "meeting" is the encounter and union of at least two persons coming from opposite places and who are entirely distinct in themselves. **CHRIST**, however, comes from above as the almighty Lord and generous giver; but we come from below as poor servants, having absolutely no power of ourselves but standing in need of all things. **CHRIST** proceeds from the innermost depths toward the exterior; we, conversely, spiritually enter from the exterior toward the innermost depths. And therefore, it is necessary that a spiritual encounter occurs here.

### OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'F' at the beginning of the text, featuring a human figure seated at a writing desk or pulpit within an architectural frame decorated with scrollwork and foliage.</image-desc>

NOW THERE OCCURS between God and our spirit a twofold meeting: namely, a mediated and an immediate one. Therefore, let each of you attend carefully here. The unity of our spirit exists in two ways: namely, essentially and potentially. According to its essential state, the spirit receives the coming of **CHRIST** naturally and immediately, without any interval of change. For that being and life which we have in the mind of the supreme Craftsman <note>Referring to God as the creator who holds the 'blueprints' or archetypes of all things</note> through their own ideas <note>original: *ideas proprias*; the divine archetypes or pre-existing forms of created things in the mind of God</note>, we do not so much "have" as we are that very thing in Him (as the Scripture says: *What was made was life in Him* <note>John 1:3-4</note>). And that very being which we possess essentially in ourselves is immediately and inseparably united in us. 

For this reason, our spirit—according to the highest and innermost apex of its essence—always receives the impression of its Exemplar <note>the divine pattern or "blueprint" after which the soul was created</note> in whose image it was created, and the marking of the light of the divine countenance. Thus, it is naturally made a perpetual dwelling of God, possessed by the eternal immanence of the divine essence, and is always frequented by the new arrival of divine visitation, and illuminated by the new light of the eternal generation of the Word <note>The "Word" or "Logos" refers to Christ; Ruusbroec describes the soul as being constantly renewed by the birth of Christ within the spirit</note>. 

Indeed, where He always is, there God comes continuously; and where He always comes, there He is continuously. But where He never was, there He will never come, because with Him there is no change, nor shadow of turning <note>original: *vicissitudinis obumbratio*; a reference to James 1:17</note>. Moreover, everything in which He exists, He Himself is that thing in Himself; because He cannot go outside the infinity of His own essence. For this reason, God and our spirit naturally possess each other in a reciprocal possession. For just as we live in God through "ideal reasons" <note>original: *rationes ideales*; the eternal concepts of all creatures existing in the divine mind</note>, so also God is in us through His essence, presence, and power. Our spirit is also, according to its highest part, immediately capable of divine clarity and of all divine things; but also through the clarity of its eternal Exemplar, shining essentially and personally within itself, the spirit according to its high[est part...]

<summary>Ruusbroec explains the "essential unity" of the human spirit with God, arguing that the soul's highest point is an immediate dwelling place for the Divine Word. He describes a reciprocal relationship where we exist in God as divine ideas, while God dwells in us through His essence.</summary>

<keywords>essential unity, immediate meeting, apex of the spirit, divine dwelling, ideas, Word of God, image of God, reciprocal possession</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "essential unity" of the human spirit with God. It concludes a sentence from the previous page regarding the eternal generation of the Son (the Word) and how the spirit exists both within the divine essence and as a created being.</meta>

## II

...[Son, which] naturally and habitually inclines itself into the divine essence; and there, possessing a certain natural and habitual bliss immanently through the eternal generation of the Son, it flows out again from there with all creatures. Through the will of the most holy Trinity, it is placed in its own created being. In this degree, it holds the image of the Trinity and Unity according to which it was created. According to the face of its creation, it receives the impression of its Eternal Pattern <note>original: *impressionem*; the "stamp" or "seal" of God's likeness on the soul</note> without the shadowing of change <note>original: *absque vicissitudinis obumbratione*; a biblical reference to James 1:17, suggesting that God’s light in the soul is constant and does not flicker or fade</note>, like an unspotted mirror that retains the image of a present object, and through a new contemplation, it renews its fresh knowledge without intermission by a new clarity.

Now this **essential unity** of our spirit with God exists not at all by its own power, but remaining in God, it flows out from God; and depending on God, it is reflected back into God as into its eternal cause. Nor has it ever been separated from God to that extent, nor shall it be separated for eternity. For it is naturally within us to such an extent that, if it were in any way separated from God, the creature would fall back into nothingness. 

Finally, this unity exceeds time and place, always operating without intermission according to the mode of divine operation—except only for the impression of its own image, which it receives not by acting but by "undergoing" <note>original: *patiendo*; in scholastic philosophy, this refers to being the recipient of an action rather than the performer of it. The soul is passive as God "stamps" His image upon it</note>, as something like God and entirely uncreated within itself. This is that dignity which we naturally possess in the essential unity of our spirit, where our spirit is naturally united to God. 

However, this unity itself makes us neither holy nor blessed, since it is truly possessed with natural equality by all—both the good and the evil—but it is not experienced equally by all. It is, however, in a certain way the primary cause and a kind of "material" principle <note>The underlying "stuff" or foundation upon which grace is built</note> of all holiness and all goodness. This, then, is the meeting of God and our spirit, and their **natural union**.

<summary>This page describes the "essential unity" of the human soul with God, arguing that every human carries an unchangeable divine image that acts as a mirror for God's light. Ruusbroec distinguishes this "natural union," which is shared by all people, from the "spiritual union" of holiness which must be consciously experienced.</summary>

<keywords>essential unity, Holy Trinity, divine image, unspotted mirror, natural union, creation, mysticism, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

### ¶ On the supernatural meeting of God and our spirit. Chapter 53

<image-desc>Square woodcut decorative initial 'E' featuring foliate and floral patterns.</image-desc>

**E**nd now, pay diligent attention to what I am about to
say. For if these things are understood with a sharp
mind alongside what has already been said, you will
surely easily grasp every divine truth that can be
taught by any creature, and more besides. In
that very unity of which we spoke, besides the
essential relationship, our spirit also exists potentially and oper-
atively within itself, as if subsisting in its own personal being.
For here is the fountain and origin of the soul's supreme
powers, the beginning and the end of every
created work, both natural and supernatural,
performed in the manner of a creature. Nevertheless, unity
insofar as it is unity <note>original: *unitas inquantum unitas est*; Ruusbroec distinguishes between the "still" essence of the soul and its "active" powers</note> does not act; but all the
powers of the soul, in whatever way they act, receive their
strength and power from their origin—that is, from the
unity of the spirit itself, subsisting originally in its
personal existence. In this unity, therefore, it is abso-
lutely necessary that the spirit be either like God
through grace and the virtues, or unlike Him through mortal
sin. Because just as we were made in the image of God,
so too were we made in His likeness <note>original: *similitudinem*; in medieval theology, the "image" is the indestructible nature of the soul, while "likeness" is the state of grace that can be lost through sin</note>—that is,
for the formation of grace. For it is the God-like
light illuminating us and making us like our
Creator; without it, we cannot be super-
naturally united to God. And although we cannot
lose the impression of the image and our natural
unity with God, if we lose the likeness—that is,
grace—we are justly condemned. For this reason,
when God finds in us a fitting aptitude for receiving
grace, He does not delay, by the impulse of His generous
goodness, to bring us to life and make us like Himself through
grace—specifically, as often as we turn to Him with an
undivided will. For at the very moment of our con-
version, CHRIST comes into us, both through a medium and without

<summary>This page defines the distinction between the soul's natural "image" of God and the supernatural "likeness" achieved through grace, explaining that while our essential nature is always tied to God, our spiritual life depends on an active turning of the will toward Him.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 62 regarding the soul's relationship with God. by a means, that is through grace and the virtues, and through himself above all virtues. We also come to him and into him in similar ways. And by imprinting his image and likeness upon us—that is, himself and his gifts—he frees us from sins, making us truly free and conformed to himself. At that very moment in which we are freed from sins, and are made free through grace and <term>charity<note>original: *charitatem*; in this context, it refers to the theological virtue of divine love rather than mere almsgiving.</note>, the spirit pours itself into a <term>fruitive<note>original: *fruitivum*; a technical term in mystical theology meaning a love that finds its final rest and "enjoyment" (fruit) in God.</note> love by a happy immersion. By this very act, there occurs a meeting and a supernatural and immediate union, in which our highest blessedness consists.

Whence, although whatever God bestows from the bounty of his charity and goodness is natural to him, to us—according to the mode of our creation—it is accidental and supernatural. This is because we were previously, by nature, "children of wrath," strangers, and unlike him; and only after being made like him do we obtain this charitable unity with God. 

It is necessary, however, that this unity—which the loving spirit pursues in God and possesses in him without any intermediary—should occur deep within our habitual comprehension. This experience is inaccessible to the grasp of our intellect, unless perhaps it resonates faintly through a habitual and simple understanding according to the way of simplicity. Indeed, in this unity, we must rest above ourselves and above all things. For from it flow all gifts, both natural and supernatural; and yet, in this state, the <term>wayfaring<note>original: *viator*; a traditional term for a human being currently on the journey of earthly life toward eternity.</note> spirit rests above all things. 

Nor is this state anything else but God and the spirit immediately united to God. Truly, we are received into this unity by the Holy Spirit; and in turn, we receive with the Holy Spirit the Father and the Son, and the entire nature of the Godhead, since God is indivisible. Also, the fruitive inclination of the spirit, seeking rest in God above all things, supernaturally attains and possesses in its essential existence everything that the spirit has there naturally acquired. 

Now, these things are held by all lovers of God; but the manner in which they occur is unknown to them as long as they live, if not through an intimate diligence of the spirit...

...they have kept watch and called themselves away from all created things. For in that very instant <note>original: *atomo*; literally "atom," used here in the medieval sense of an indivisible moment or the blink of an eye.</note> in which one turns away from sins, he is received by God into his own essential unity—into the highest peak of his spirit—so that he may rest in God both now and forever. He also receives grace and the divine likeness in the fountain-like origin of his powers <note>original: *potētiarū*; referring to the soul's faculties such as memory, intellect, and will.</note>, so that he may grow perfect in virtues and mature in good character. This rest of unity perseveres as long as the likeness and the virtues remain through <term>charity<note>original: *charitatem*; the theological virtue of divine love.</note>. ### ¶ ON THE EFFECT OF THE LIKENESS OF GRACE.
->Chapter 54.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring interlaced floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>Furthermore, in this consists all holiness and blessedness: that through the likeness and the medium of grace, the spirit is led into the rest of its essential unity. This is because grace is the path through which everyone must pass who wishes to arrive at the naked and simple unity of his own essence, into which God pours himself with all his riches. For this reason, surely, because of the lack of illuminating grace and the lack of that which leads one by the hand to the unity of enjoyment <note>original: *fruitionis*; referring to the soul's "enjoyment" or "fruition" of God's presence.</note>, all sinners and damned spirits are said to be in darkness. 

Nevertheless, the essential existence of the spirit itself is so noble and such a lover of being that, for its own part, the damned cannot wish to be reduced to nothing. Sin, however, interposes such a chaos of darkness and unlikeness between the soul's powers and the essence in which God dwells, that the spirit cannot be united to its own essence. This essence would indeed be its homeland and its own bedchamber of rest, were it not exiled through sin into the region of unlikeness <note>original: *regione dissimilitudinis*; a philosophical term, famously used by St. Augustine, to describe the state of a soul that has lost its resemblance to God.</note>. For he who lives without sin possesses the likeness through grace, and God is his very own. Thus, the supreme necessity of grace is clear: it washes away sins, prepares the way, and makes our whole life fruitful. For this... <note>catchword: *Hanc ob*</note>

...for this reason, Christ comes into us through certain means—that is, through grace and manifold gifts—and we, in turn, meet Him through the mediation of virtues and various spiritual exercises. The more exalted the gifts He bestows, and the more subtly and profoundly He moves us, the more the spirit is made fruitful with higher and more delightful exercises, just as was shown in previous sections. Moreover, these experiences are repeated with frequent renewals. For God is constantly visiting the spirit with new gifts, and the spirit—according to the manner and requirement of this divine visitation—always turns inward and is ever more sublimely renewed to meet the Bridegroom. This ensures that the soul always advances successfully in spiritual exercises and that the life of the affections <note>original: *affectiua vita*; referring to the emotional and volitional side of the spiritual life, as opposed to the purely intellectual.</note> continually grows. This actual meeting is indeed "mediated," occurring as it does through the mediation of God’s gifts and our own virtues, alongside the working of the Spirit. Truly, these intermediaries are necessary, for without the infusion of God’s grace and our own free turning toward Him through <term>charity<note>original: *charitatem*; the theological virtue of selfless, divine love.</note></term>, no creature has ever been saved.

### ¶ ON THE CONTINUAL VISITATION OF CHRIST. Chapter 55.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap letter B featuring floral and foliate motifs.</image-desc>

Looking down from on high with the benevolent eyes of His goodness upon the dwelling and rest which He has established among and within us through His frequent visitations—that is, our unity and likeness to Him—Christ deigns to inhabit that same unity without ceasing, through the arrival of His wondrous eternal generation <note>original: *æternæ generationis*; a theological term referring to the eternal birth of the Son from the Father, which is mirrored in the soul of the believer.</note> and the rich influence of His radiance. Wishing, therefore, to make a permanent home within the spirits of those who love Him, He never ceases to visit us with excellent gifts according to that likeness, so that we may be made more like Him and more illustrious in our virtues. Hence, He desires us to dwell habitually in the essential unity of our spirit, and with Himself...

### ¶ On the continual progress of our spirit. Chapter 56.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'Q' featuring intricate floral and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
How we ought to meet Christ spiritually in each of our actions, and progress into a greater likeness, and possess the unity of <term>fruition<note>original: *fruitionis*; a technical mystical term for the soul's joyful and restful enjoyment of God's presence.</note></term> more nobly: if it pleases your <term>charity<note>original: *charitatem*; a common polite address in medieval religious texts, referring to the reader's spirit of divine love.</note></term>, please attend to this for a little while. For whatever work, however small it may be, when offered to God by the hands of charity and a simple intention, earns a greater likeness and eternal life. Indeed, a simple intention gathers the scattered powers of the soul into the unity of the spirit, joining the spirit to God in the union of love. Simple intention is the beginning and end of all things, and the beauty of the virtues; it offers them to God as a sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving. By its sharp focus <note>original: *acumine*; literally "sharpness" or "point," referring to the concentrated pinnacle of the mind or the "apex of the soul."</note>, it passes through itself, the heavenly bodies, and all created things in a swift flight, and knows how to find God joyfully in the uniform abyss of its very self. That intention is truly "simple" when it embraces nothing outside of God, except in relation to God; for this reason, it naturally drives out all pretense and duplicity of heart. Truly, this intention must be preserved by a person above all things in all their actions, and it must be practiced with the greatest effort. For the divine...

II.

i

always places the soul in God's sight. It provides a continuous light to the intellect; it arouses the virtues with watchful diligence, and it absolves and frees us from all servile and foreign fear both now and in the final judgment. This is the **simple eye** <note>original: *simplex oculus*; a reference to Matthew 6:22. In mystical theology, this "eye" represents the soul's single-minded focus on God.</note> of which **CHRIST** speaks in the Gospel, which makes the whole body—that is, the entire collection of works and the whole life of a person—luminous and pure from the stains of sin. It is the internal **armor of the spirit** <note>original: *spiritus armatura*.</note>, embracing within itself **faith, hope, and charity** <note>These are the "theological virtues" that direct the soul toward God.</note>. Because it trusts in God, it returns to Him a firm and whole faith. Subduing nature, it grants the peaceful possession of the "earth" of the body. It completely expels every murmur of the spirit; and nourishing the virtues with internal vitality, it confers peace and provides hope, acting in the present so that we may truly have confidence on the day of judgment.

Let us remain, therefore, in the **unity of the spirit** <note>original: *unitate spiritus*; the state where the soul's powers are gathered together and aligned with God.</note> through the likeness of grace. Meeting God in continuous turns through the mediation of the virtues, let us offer our whole life and all our works to Him with a **simple intention** <note>original: *intentio simplex*.</note>. Thus, in every work and through every passing hour, we shall be made more like Him.

Finally, in the **abyss of simple intention** <note>original: *abysso simplicis intentionis*; Ruusbroec often uses the metaphor of an "abyss" to describe the infinite and bottomless nature of God's presence.</note>, we shall transcend ourselves; and meeting God more immediately, we shall rest with Him in that very abyss of simplicity—or rather, in that **ocean of simplicity** <note>original: *pelago simplicitatis*.</note>—happily possessing the inheritance prepared for us from eternity. For the life and diligent operation of all spirits consists in "likeness" (by means of a simple intention). Yet their more excellent rest consists in that very simplicity which exceeds all likeness. Nevertheless, each spirit excels another in virtues and likeness, possessing its own essence according to its own nobility within itself. Truly, God provides for each one in a singular way; and conversely, each one in the depths of their own spirit [encounters] God according to the measure of their own charity...

**Q**uomodo—How we ought to meet **CHRIST** spiritually in each of our actions, to advance into a greater likeness, and to possess the **unity of enjoyment** <note>original: *fruitionis unitatem*; "enjoyment" (fruitio) in mystical theology refers to the soul's restful delight in God's presence.</note> more nobly—if it pleases your charity, pay attention for a little while. ### ¶ Concerning the order of both the active and the spiritual life according to the gifts of the Holy Spirit, and first according to the gifts of fear, piety, and knowledge. Chapter 57.

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'C' featuring a stag standing in a wooded landscape with mountains in the distance.</image-desc>

Let us therefore consider the order of the stages of all virtues and all holiness: namely, how we ought to meet God through the **likeness of grace** <note>original: *gratiæ similitudinem*; the idea that through God’s gift, the soul begins to mirror the Divine character.</note>, so that we may be able to rest with Him in **unity** <note>original: *unitate*; a central theme in Ruusbroec’s work, referring to the mystical union where the soul and God become one without losing their distinct identities.</note>. Anyone who has turned from sins to the path of salvation, if they live in the **fear of God** <note>original: *timore dei*; the first of the seven gifts, understood here as a holy awe or reverence that keeps one from sin.</note> and cultivate their life through moral virtues and outward spiritual exercises—and who also remains obedient to the commands of the Lord and the Church—shows themselves to be well-intentioned and ready for every good through a **simple intention** <note>original: *simplici intentione*</note>.

By acting through faith and aligning their own will with the Divine will, and by avoiding **folly** <note>original: *vecordiam*; spiritual senselessness or lack of heart</note>, such a person is made similar to God; and above this likeness, they rest sweetly in God. For through faith of the soul and a simple intention of the mind, each person fulfills the Divine will more or less according to the measure of their likeness; and through love, they rest in their **Beloved** <note>original: *dilecto suo*</note> above that likeness. 

If, however, one exercises themselves in the gift they have received, God soon graciously bestows upon them the spirit of **piety** <note>original: *pietatis*; the second gift, which softens the heart and makes it ready for service</note> and generosity. By this gift, the person is made generous, gentle, and kind, and they stand forth more similar to God and more spiritually alive than before. They feel themselves resting more firmly in God than they did previously—broader in virtues and deeper in spirit. The likeness itself and the rest taste sweeter to them, the more similar to Him they have become.

But if, in this stage, they exercise themselves with the greatest diligence and a simple intention, and have struggled with strong resistance against the opposites of these virtues, they then deserve to receive the third gift: that is, the gift of **knowledge** <note>original: *scientiæ*; the third gift, providing insight into the created world and one's own soul</note> and **discernment** <note>original: *discretionis*</note>. By this gift, one becomes discerning and clear-sighted, knowing by reason what should be done or what should be avoided, and where it is fitting to receive or where to give...

<summary>This page outlines the first three stages of spiritual growth based on the Gifts of the Holy Spirit—Fear, Piety, and Knowledge—explaining how each gift deepens the soul's likeness to God and its ability to find rest in Him.</summary>
<keywords>active life, spiritual life, gifts of the Holy Spirit, fear of God, piety, knowledge, discernment, simple intention, likeness of grace, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>
