II

i ij

...and through a **simple intention** <note>original: *simplicem intentionem*</note> as well as divine love, he rests in God above himself in the **unity of the spirit** <note>original: *unitate spiritus*; the state where the human spirit is joined to God's Spirit.</note>. He possesses himself in a greater **likeness** <note>original: *similitudine*; the process of becoming more like God through grace.</note> than before, and performs all his works with a grander delight. For through the **gift of fear**, he is obedient to the Father. Through the **gift of knowledge**, he is clear in reason and discerning: this he owes to the Son. And through the **gift of piety**, he is generous, holy, and kind: this he owes to the Holy Spirit. Truly, through these things, he bears the likeness of the most holy Trinity.

But through love and the simplicity of his intention, he rests in God (as we have said). And the whole **active life** <note>original: *activa vita*; the life of external moral action and religious duty.</note> consists in these things. Therefore, let everyone exercise themselves with the greatest diligence and follow their simple intention with discernment as a companion. Also, against the opposites of the virtues, let him preserve himself with a combative watchfulness, and let him always lean toward Christ's feet on the stool of humility; thus, he may continually grow in the progress of the virtues and in likeness to God. 

If he governs himself in this way, he cannot go astray; yet, for all this, he will not exceed the limits of the active life. Furthermore, if with a busy and distracted heart he pays more attention to the **foliage of works** and the **flowers of customs** than to the **fruits of virtues** and the cause and end of all virtues—and if he exercises himself more toward the outward signs of the **sacraments** <note>original: *sacramentorum signa*</note> and external traditions than toward the underlying cause and the truth contained within them—then he will labor all the days of his life within the territory of the active life, devoted to outward things. Even so, through good works directed to God with a simple intention, divine mercy will save him.

### ¶ Concerning the STAGE of fortitude. Chapter 58.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'H' embellished with swirling vines and floral patterns.</image-desc>

For this reason, anyone wishing to draw near to God and to turn their exercise and their life toward higher things must necessarily enter from the works to the end, and from the signs to the truth. Thus, having become the lord and master of his own works and an internal knower of truth, he enters the region of the **spiritual life** <note>original: *spiritualis vitae*</note> flowing with milk and honey. Soon, through the fourth gift—that is, the **gift of fortitude** <note>original: *fortitudinis dono*</note>—he is made new by God...

<summary>This page concludes the section on the active life, explaining how the gifts of fear, knowledge, and piety mirror the Trinity, while warning that focusing only on outward rituals keeps a soul in the "active" rather than "spiritual" stage. It then introduces the gift of fortitude as the gateway to a deeper, interior spiritual life.</summary>

<keywords>active life, spiritual life, Holy Trinity, simple intention, gift of fear, gift of knowledge, gift of piety, gift of fortitude, sacraments, Jan van Ruusbroec</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the "active life" into the "spiritual life." It focuses on the gift of fortitude, which allows the seeker to move beyond both earthly distractions and the temptation to linger in spiritual comforts, ultimately aiming for a direct encounter with God.</meta>

->THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-

...the dweller is initiated. Through this, he overcomes and tramples the passions of the soul, driving away all distractions and disruptions. Made free, he is released from all created things. Consequently, purified from the assaults of mental images <note>original: *phantasmatum incursibus*; distractions caused by sensory memories or imaginations that hinder meditation.</note>, he regains mastery of himself. Without effort, he gathers himself into himself and is ignited within by the fire of divine love. With inward devotion, heavenly affection, praise, thanksgiving, and a **simple intention** <note>original: *simplici intentione*; a focused, single-minded aim toward God without ulterior motives.</note>, he turns toward God. 

All his works and his whole life—both interior and exterior—are seasoned for him with a honeyed flavor. For, standing before the throne of the most excellent Trinity, he frequently receives honeyed drops of consolation and sweetness. Indeed, serving at such a table of praise, thanksgiving, and intimate reverence, he is often fed and watered with the remnants of sweet delicacies and wine flavored with spices <note>original: *delibuti pigmentis vini*; refers to "piment," a sweetened, spiced wine considered a luxury in the Middle Ages, here used as a metaphor for spiritual joy.</note>, which fall and drip from the table of so great a Prince. 

Moreover, because of the simplicity of his intention, he rejoices in the sweetness of internal peace. If he remains stable and fixed, standing before the sight of Divine Majesty with praise, thanksgiving, and the service of a right intention, he will merit a **double spirit of fortitude** <note>original: *spiritum fortitudinis... duplicem*; a key concept in Ruusbroec’s thought where the soul must resist both worldly vice and the "spiritual gluttony" of enjoying divine gifts for their own sake.</note> within himself. 

By this means, he will not become intoxicated by divine consolations, sweetnesses, or any gift of God; nor will he cling sleepily to the peace and quiet of his own heart. Rather, it is necessary to pass beyond all gifts and consolations of every kind with a swift ascent, so that he may find the One whom he loves above all things. Thus, one is strong with **simple fortitude** when he overcomes distractions of the heart and earthly concerns by despising them. But he is strong with **double strength** when he transcends divine consolations and heavenly gifts. Through the **gift of fortitude**, therefore, he sets aside all created things and possesses himself with freedom and power.

<summary>This page describes the transition into a deeper spiritual state where the soul, aided by the gift of fortitude, overcomes both earthly distractions and the "drunkenness" of spiritual pleasures. Ruusbroec emphasizes that the seeker must transcend even divine consolations to find God Himself.</summary>

### ¶ ON THE STEP of divine counsel. Chapter 59.

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'N' decorated with scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Therefore, when no creature is able to conquer or hinder the man who wishes to persist—constant and submissive—in his **simple intention** <note>original: *simplici intentione*; a single-minded focus on God alone, without seeking personal gain or distractions.</note> toward divine praise, and who seeks and intends God above all His gifts: he is suddenly advanced to a higher step. Specifically, he is divinely endowed with the fifth gift of counsel. For through this gift, the Father draws His faithful servant inward and calls him, along with His elect, to the right hand of His eternity. The Son, speaking spiritually within him, says, "Follow me to my Father" <note>original: *sequere me... ad patrem meum*</note>. For one thing is necessary <note>A reference to the Gospel of Luke (10:42), suggesting that beyond all various religious activities, only the union with God truly matters.</note>. The Holy Spirit also, expanding the heart with His love, kindles a fire within it.

From this, the man soon begins to seethe within, and he is made "impatient" <note>In this mystical context, "impatience" refers to a holy restlessness or a burning desire for God that cannot be satisfied by anything on earth.</note> by the vehemence of this burning heat. For he who yields to this counsel is spurred on by the impulse of love; nothing is able to satisfy him except the Lord. For this reason, he easily abandons himself and all things to find Him in whom he lives, and in whom all things are one. 

On this step, he continually contemplates God with the eye of simple intention. Restraining the impulse of his heart with the bridle of reason and renouncing his own will, he freely waits for the embrace of the desired unity. He waits until divine benevolence deigns to grant this unity of its own accord. 

Thus, the spirit of counsel <note>original: *spiritus consilij*; one of the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit (Isaiah 11:2), providing supernatural guidance for the soul's choices.</note> becomes "double" <note>The author refers to a "double" spirit to show a progression: first, the soul actively seeks God; second, the soul learns to wait for God’s timing.</note> within him. For he is certainly great, and follows the order of divine counsel by the royal way, who abandons himself and all things; with the vehemence of a seething, impatient love and a raised voice, he proclaims: "Thy kingdom come" <note>original: *adueniat regnum tuum*; from the Lord's Prayer.</note>. 

Yet he is surely even greater and complies better with divine counsel who, overcoming his own will through the increasing strength of love, speaks to God with great reverence and patience: "Not my [will], Lord, not mine," <note>original: *non mea domine non mea*; echoing Christ’s prayer in the Garden of Gethsemane, "not my will, but yours be done."</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition to the "Gift of Counsel," where the soul's intense desire for God is tempered by a total surrender of the self-will. The seeker moves from actively demanding God's presence to patiently waiting for a divinely-initiated union.</summary>

<keywords>Gift of Counsel, Simple Intention, Divine Will, Holy Spirit, Spiritual Despair, Resignation, Contemplation</keywords>

->ON THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE<-

...but let your will be done in all things <note>original: *sed tua in omnibus voluntas fiat*</note>. With such a conclusion, **CHRIST**—praying more at length to the Father in His agony through a humble annihilation of Himself <note>In mystical theology, "annihilation" (*anullatio*) does not mean ceasing to exist, but the total emptying of the ego and self-interest to make room for God.</note>—ended His prayer by the counsel of the Holy Spirit. In doing so, He brought forth a word more lovable to the Father than any He had ever uttered, more delightful and honorable to Himself, more useful to us, and more hateful to the devil, as it was more harmful to him than anything else. For by the renunciation of His human will, all who are truly saved are indeed saved. 

In this way, the divine will becomes the highest joy to the humble lover and the greatest delight in the perception of the spirit, even if—though it were impossible <note>original: *per impossibile*; a common medieval rhetorical device used to emphasize a point by imagining an impossible scenario.</note>—he were to descend into hell. On this step also, nature is supremely surpassed, and God is most highly exalted. The lover of divine counsel is made fit to receive all the gifts of God, because he has inwardly denied himself entirely and renounced his own will. 

Having faithfully surrendered everything he owns, he wants or asks for nothing other than what God wishes to give, for God’s will is his joy. He who transfers himself entirely into God with the hands of love is the freest of all people and lives without any anxiety, because God cannot possibly lose anyone who belongs to Him. 

Truly, although God alone knows the hearts of the children of men, some are nevertheless tested by Him to see whether they have truly learned how to renounce themselves. Thus, having been tested, we may deserve to be divinely enlightened and honored by God, and may live in a way that is truly beneficial. For this reason, God sometimes shifts certain people "to the left" <note>To be "on the left" traditionally signifies a period of spiritual trial, desolation, or the withdrawal of "sensible" or felt consolations from God.</note>, casting them from heaven into hell, and reducing them from great pleasure to the greatest poverty, so that it appears that such a person has been utterly forsaken and despised by both God and all creatures alike. He who previously, while the breeze of prosperity blew with the sweet breath of joy and love, [held] himself and his own...

<summary>This page describes the spiritual "annihilation" of the self-will, modeled on Christ's prayer in Gethsemane, leading to a state of total freedom and trust in God even during intense spiritual trials.</summary>

i iiij

...denied his own will, so that he in no way seeks what was his own, but rather the most pleasing will of God: he will easily renounce himself in times of adversity, in pains and calamities, seeking not what is his own, but the honor of God. For he who is ready to perform any great works for God is also prepared to endure difficult things. However, to suffer adversity with the **mortification** <note>In this context, "mortification" refers to the spiritual practice of "dying" to one's selfish desires or ego to become more aligned with the divine.</note> of one's own will is more sublime, more pleasing to God, and more delightful to our spirit than to perform great works with that same renunciation of the will, because it is more contrary to our nature. For this reason, our spirit is raised up more highly, and nature is more deeply cast down and trampled underfoot in the humble patience of adversity than in the delightful abundance of works—even great ones—provided that an equal **charity** <note>original: *charitate*; referring here to *caritas*, the highest form of Christian love or divine grace.</note> governs in both cases.

# ¶ CONCERNING THE FIRST degree of the gift of understanding, by which the spirit is established in unity. Chapter 60.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring scrolling vine and floral motifs.</image-desc>

**I**f indeed he persists in this renunciation as if wanting or knowing nothing else, he certainly possesses the **spirit of counsel** <note>original: *spiritum consilij*</note> within himself in a twofold way—namely, by satisfying God's will and counsel twice over: both by performing great things and by suffering difficult things, through the vigorous action of good works and the mortification of himself through submissive obedience. Nature also is adorned with the highest beauty, and he himself is made fit to be divinely enlightened according to the spirit. Therefore, he soon deserves to receive the sixth gift, which is the **gift of understanding** <note>original: *intellectus donum*; one of the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit, often described as a deep, intuitive insight into divine truths.</note>. Now, in previous sections, we compared this gift to a spring and a stream. For it both unites our spirit and establishes it in unity; it reveals the truth and brings forth a broad, flowing charity through common benevolence. Nevertheless, this gift is like a ray of the sun, which in the simple air...

<header>THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS</header>

...bathes [the world] in **brightness** <note>original: *claritate*; refers to the spiritual illumination that grants clarity of mind</note> / and explaining the forms of things / manifests the differences of the heavens / and generating life-giving heat, makes the whole world fruitful for the common good. In this way, the first **irradiation** <note>original: *irradiatio*; the "beaming in" of divine light</note> of this gift generates a certain **simplicity** in the spirit: which soon is bathed in a particular chief brightness, just as air is by the light of the sun. For the **grace** <note>original: *gratia*</note> of God, which is the foundation of all gifts, **habitually** <note>original: *habitualiter*; in scholastic theology, this refers to an abiding state of being or a permanent quality of the soul, rather than a fleeting feeling</note> inhabits our **possible intellect** <note>original: *intellectum... possibilem*; a technical term in medieval philosophy for the part of the human mind that has the capacity to receive or be "informed" by new knowledge or divine light</note> as if it were a certain simple light through the gift of understanding. By this simple light, our spirit is stably simplified and clarified within itself, and is filled with God’s grace and divine gifts, having truly become like God through grace and **deifying charity** <note>original: *deificā charitatem*; love that transforms the soul into a likeness of the Divine</note>. 

But even if one is like Him, and loves and intends toward God simply above all gifts, yet he is not content with that likeness and created brightness. Since he is inclined toward a certain supreme and infinite Good from which he emanated, by an inward leaning that is as much natural as it is spiritual. So too the **unity** <note>original: *vnitas*</note> of the divine essence, by an eternal attraction, draws every likeness toward union with itself. And for these reasons, he plunges himself into God as if into his perennial rest, both by actual and **habitual** immersion. For grace relates to God as a ray does to the sun; and by it, as by a straight path, we are led into divine things. And therefore, radiating simply into us, it makes us **god-like** <note>original: *deiformes*; literally "having the form of God"</note>. Moreover, the one conformed to God, striving after its Like, plunges itself into God at every moment; and having been made one with God, it always inhabits that very unity. This indeed is the supreme and singular labor of **charity**. We nevertheless eternally retain a conformity of grace or glory, namely where we possess ourselves as practitioners in the exercises and works of the virtues and of charity: nonetheless, in unity with God in the divine light, above all our own working, we preserve a pure **simplicity** in our spirit.

<summary>This page describes the Gift of Understanding as a divine light that simplifies and "clarifies" the human spirit, drawing the soul into an abiding union with God. It explains how grace acts like a sunbeam, leading the soul back to its source in the divine Unity.</summary>
<keywords>Gift of Understanding, divine light, possible intellect, deification, simplicity, unity, Ruusbroec, scholasticism, grace</keywords>

## II

...where above all virtues we possess God in **perpetual rest** <note>original: *perpetua quiete*; a state of contemplative stillness where the soul is focused solely on the Divine</note>. For it is necessary that **charity** <note>original: *charitatem*; here meaning the divine love that binds the soul to God</note>, by the duty of likeness, should labor everlastingly: so that with God, through the unity of **enjoyable love** <note>original: *profruitiue dilectionis*; "fruition" refers to the soul's ultimate enjoyment of God's presence</note> in leisure, we may eternally rest. This is indeed the <term>twofold business</term> <note>original: *biforme negocium*; the dual task of the soul to both act and rest</note> of charity: namely, in one little hour of time, to love **actually** <note>meaning through specific actions or conscious thoughts</note> and to rest **habitually** <note>meaning as an abiding state of being</note> in the Beloved. These two states are undoubtedly strengthened by one another. For the more sublime the love: the greater the rest. And the deeper the rest: the more intense the love; as if they possess life and health from one another. Whence he who does not love does not rest: and vice versa. 

It appears, however, to some endowed with no small **holiness** <note>original: *sanctitate*</note> that they neither love God, nor rest in Him. But the cause of this appearance is love itself. For they desire to love more intensely than their own human strengths allow: and therefore, falling short in the act of loving, they cry out that they do not love. Yet in this very failing, they feel both love and rest alike. For no one is able to understand how it is simultaneously possible to love **actually** and to rest **enjoyably** or **habitually**, except surely the one who becomes mild through the humble gentleness of the heart, falls silent in the tranquil serenity of the mind, and shines with the clarity of understanding. 

Yet every **lover** <note>original: *amator*; used here in the mystical sense of a soul seeking union with the Divine</note> is one with God in rest, and like God in the activity of love. For God also, in His most excellent nature (whose likeness we bear), according to the **unity of His essence**, rests in eternal enjoyment; and according to the **trinity of persons**, works until now in eternal activity. Each of these is the perfection of the other: for **rest** consists in unity and **activity** consists in trinity; both of which remain equally for eternity. Therefore, he who desires to taste how sweet the Lord is: let him love. For if he loves: without a doubt he shall taste. But if he is indeed content with external things, his soul [will] ne-

<summary>This page explores the mystical paradox of the "twofold business" of the soul: simultaneously laboring in active love and resting in divine contemplation. It explains that this dual nature mirrors God Himself, who rests in His essence while acting through the Trinity.</summary>

<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, mysticism, charity, divine rest, Trinity, unity of essence, fruition, active life, contemplative life</keywords>

...[For as long as one] refuses to be consoled from any other source: he will not be able to taste what God is, nor be delighted by divine sweetness. It is necessary, therefore, that we possess ourselves through the likeness of grace and the virtues, in **simplicity of heart**: and possess God above ourselves through love in the **rest of unity** <note>original: *requie unitatis*; a state where the soul's powers are stilled and focused entirely on the oneness of God</note>. This is the first step of the gift of understanding: by which our spirit is established in unity.

## ¶ ON THE SECOND STEP OF THE GIFT OF UNDERSTANDING, BY WHICH THE SPIRIT IS INTELLECTUALLY ILLUMINATED. Chapter 61.

<image-desc>Large decorative drop-cap 'A' inside a square frame, embellished with intricate foliage and floral scrollwork.</image-desc>
As the air is made bright by the rays of the sun, the beauty of the whole universe is revealed; and a man with enlightened eyes is delighted by the many different forms of colors. In a similar way, while we are simple within ourselves, and our <term>possible intellect</term> <note>original: *intellectus possibilis*; a philosophical term referring to the human mind's capacity to receive and process new information or divine insights</note> is illuminated by the gift of understanding, we are then able to understand both the "proper" and "appropriated" attributes <note>In theology, "proper" refers to things unique to one person of the Trinity (like Fatherhood), while "appropriated" refers to qualities of the whole Godhead usually associated with one person (like Wisdom with the Son)</note> of the most high **Trinity**, which are the cause and origin of every divine work. 

For although all men may understand the "effects" of God—and many who are brilliant with natural reason are led to a knowledge of God through these effects—nevertheless, no one can understand the divine properties and attributes (which are the cause of those effects) in a way that is "savory" or proper to their nature, except through the gift of understanding. For according to the law of eternal truth, this gift teaches one to govern life without error; indeed, it teaches us to contemplate that truth for our own nobility, giving us a **rule of discernment** <note>original: *discretionis*; the ability to judge and choose between different spiritual paths or actions</note> regarding the virtues and their practice. For the one whom it illuminates is surely able to live in the spirit and, with the eye of enlightened reason, to weigh both heavenly and earthly things correctly and to understand them clearly. With the mind, therefore...

<summary>This page describes the transition from the first stage of "Understanding"—internal unity and rest—to the second stage, where the mind is supernaturally illuminated to perceive the inner nature and attributes of the Trinity.</summary>
<keywords>Understanding, Holy Spirit, Trinity, Illumination, Discernment, Divine Attributes, Contemplation, Unity</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Sixth Gift of the Holy Spirit (Understanding), specifically the "Second Step" begun on page 69. It describes the soul’s contemplation of God's infinite nature and how that divine light reflects back onto the soul and all of creation.</meta>

...living in heaven, [the soul] contemplates the nobility and dignity of its beloved alongside all his saints. It marvels at his incomprehensible majesty, unsearchable depth, immeasurable length and width, and his infinite, inscrutable wisdom and truth. It gazes upon his unspeakable goodness and generosity, and countless other such attributes. Although these are expressed with many different names according to the concepts of our mind, they are all one in God: namely, the simplest **nature of the divinity**.

From there, our mind turns the clear eyes of reason back toward itself and all other creatures. It perceives that they were created freely out of the generosity of divine goodness, and naturally adorned with many-layered qualities and characteristics. Furthermore, they are even more generously renewed by the mercy of divine love, and—desiring and seeking salvation—they long to be supernaturally enriched and decorated by the gifts of his grace, and most freely and gloriously blessed <note>original: *beatificandas*; the process of being made perfectly happy or "blessed" through the vision of God</note> through the enjoyment of his goodness.

This kind of rational contemplation, weighing the many benefits of God's riches, fills our spirit with a joyful wonder—provided that through "god-making" love <note>original: *amorem deificum*; a transformative love that makes the soul more like God</note> we have become habitually "dead and buried" in the love of God. While living and moving in the spirit, we should nonetheless savor those eternal things which are above.

This gift also reveals to us that **unity** which we possess as a habit in God through a deep, "fruitive" love <note>original: *fruitiuum amorem*; a technical term for a love that finds its rest and joy in the simple presence of the beloved</note>, as well as that **likeness of God** which we actively hold within ourselves through grace, charity, and the other virtues. This gift also pours into our minds a clarity of intellectual light. By walking in the spirit with a steady step <note>original: *inoffenso pede*; literally "with an un-stumbled foot," meaning a path free of spiritual error</note> in this light, we are able to contemplate God through spiritual images.

It describes the mystical state of being "buried" in divine love while maintaining an active spiritual life through intellectual clarity.</summary>

->ON THE SPIRITUAL NUPTIALS<- <note>original: *DE OR.NUP. SP.*; abbreviation for *De Ornatu Nuptiarum Spiritualium*</note>

...so that we may be able to know ourselves and all other creatures according to the measure of the light that illuminates us, according to the gracious favor of the divine will that inspires us, and according to the nobility of our own contemplating intellect. This is the second step of the gift of understanding, by which our spirit is intellectually enlightened.

### ON THE THIRD step of the gift of understanding, by which the will is inflamed by universal love. Chapter 62.

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'S' set within a square frame, decorated with intricate floral vines and foliage.</image-desc> Just as a greater or lesser heat is generated in the air depending on the quantity of sunlight that makes it clear—and from this heat all fruitfulness is measured—so it is with the soul. If our reason and our understanding are illuminated by God in this way for the discerning knowledge of divine truth, then the loving power—that is, the will—quickly catches fire with an abundance of overflowing love <term>charity; from the Latin *caritas*, referring to a selfless, divine love</term> and a universal goodwill.

For this gift, through the knowledge of truth which we obtain in His light, produces in us a universal love <note>original: *communem charitatem*; this refers to "common" or "shared" love—a key concept in this theology where the soul’s love becomes as broad and inclusive as God's love for all creation</note>. Those who are richer in inward simplicity <note>a state of being focused entirely on God, free from worldly distractions</note> are surely gentler, more peaceful of heart, deeper in the ocean of the Godhead, clearer in their understanding, more abundant in good works, and more universal in their overflowing love. They are also hindered by fewer obstacles because they are most like God, whose essence is simplicity, whose understanding is clarity, and whose influence in action is the most abundant and universal love.

Because we are made most like God in these three things, it is good for us to weave together this unbreakable, threefold cord of divine likeness: namely, that we preserve the simplicity of our mind with the greatest watchfulness, that we consider all things through the light of reason, and that we steep all things in universal love.

<summary>This page describes the final stage of the Gift of Understanding, where divine illumination moves from the intellect to the will, sparking a "universal love" that mirrors God's own nature. The author argues that true spiritual maturity is found in a "threefold cord" of simplicity, clarity of reason, and all-embracing charity.</summary>

<keywords>Gift of Understanding, universal love, divine likeness, simplicity, charity, will, illumination, Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual marriage</keywords>

II

...[we may live]. <note>original: *mus*; likely the suffix of a verb such as *vivimus* (we live) or *possimus* (we may be able) continuing from the previous page</note> Just as this visible sun, remaining simple and unchanged in itself, communicates the rays of its brightness and heat to the whole world: Therefore, let your charity <note>original: *charitas vestra*; a traditional medieval form of address to a devout audience, similar to "your graces" or "dearly beloved"</note> take heed of how we ought—while we have the light of reason—to walk in the business of common charity. 

For the Father, in divine matters, is the principle of the whole divinity according to essence and persons; and therefore, in the sight of the paternal majesty, let us bow ourselves with humble reverence according to the spirit. And so we shall possess humility, which is the most solid foundation of all virtues. 

Let us also adore the Father’s power <term>potentia; refers here to God's attribute of omnipotence</term> with all devotion, all honor, and all veneration of spirit, and thus we shall be spiritually exalted. For by the hand of His power, He creates all things from nothing, and—lest they fall back into nothingness—He preserves the universe. 

To divine faithfulness and most ample <term>charity; selfless, divine love</term>, let us continually offer the sacrifice of praise and thanksgiving; for she has mercifully delivered us both from the chains of the devil and from the dangers of eternal death, and so we shall be truly free. 

To Eternal Wisdom, let us present the blindness and ignorance of human nature with plaintive voices, desiring from the heart that every person be divinely enlightened and attain the knowledge of the truth. And thus God will be known and honored by them. 

Let us also pray with inward devotion for divine mercy for sinners, that they may be converted from vices and progress in virtues; and so God will be more affectionately loved by them. 

Let us generously bestow from the treasury of divine goodness upon all who are in need, so that they may be filled with divine gifts and flow back into the divine goodness; and thus God will be possessed in all things. 

Let us also offer to the Father, as a sacrificial offering <note>original: *hostiam*; literally a victim or sacrifice</note> of praise and veneration, all the deeds of CHRIST—whatever He performed in His humanity at the command of charity; and thus every prayer of ours shall be heard.

<summary>This page describes the practical application of 'universal love' by which the soul honors the Father's power, prays for the enlightenment of all humanity, and offers Christ's earthly deeds back to God as a spiritual sacrifice.</summary>

<keywords>common charity, Father, humility, divine power, eternal wisdom, intercession for sinners, deeds of Christ, spiritual sacrifice</keywords>

->THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE<-

Let us also offer to the Father in CHRIST JESUS golden vials full of perfumes: which are the prayers of the angels and of all the blessed in the heavenly homeland, <note>original: *patria*; literally "fatherland," used here to mean Heaven</note> joined with the humble devotions of all the chosen ones dwelling on earth; and thus, we shall all be united into a single flame of praise. Finally, let us offer to God the Father in the name of CHRIST the entire rational and acceptable service of Holy Mother Church: the sublime sacrifices of the priests and whatever the faculties of our intellect and affection can provide. This is so that we may meet God through CHRIST the Son of God, and be conformed to Him by the likeness of common <term>charity; here referring to *caritas*, the selfless love that connects all members of the Church</term>, and—transcending every likeness through simplicity of mind—we may be united with Him in the essential unity of the spirit. 

For we ought always to remain with God in unity, always to flow out with God and all the saints in common charity, and always to return into that same unity by the flood of praise and thanksgiving, and always, by the weight of penetrating love, to be immersed into the habitual rest of <term>enjoyment; *fruitio*, a technical term for the soul's peaceful delight in God's presence</term>. This is the more abundant life which we know by the witness of our conscience; and through it, we fully possess the gift of understanding.

### ¶ ON THE DEGREE of the gift of wisdom. Chapter LXIII.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring intricate interlaced foliate and vine motifs.</image-desc>
Furthermore, in the <term>reflux; the "ebb-tide" or return of the soul from outward activity back into God</term>, that unity of enjoyment itself behaves like a certain darkness that knows no manner or limit, and a certain incomprehensible immensity. Through love and simple intention, the spirit approaches by actually offering all virtues, and—both habitually and through enjoyment—offers itself above all virtues. Among these amorous offerings, the seventh gift, which is wisdom, arises. This gift makes the simplicity of the spirit, as well as the body and soul...

II

...wisdom, and drenching it with a spiritual flavor. This gift is a certain **divine touch** in the unity of our spirit: it is the fountain-like and original wellspring of all graces, gifts, and virtues. In this touch, every person indeed tastes their own exercise in life, according to the power of that very touch and the measure of their own love. 

And this **divine touch** is the ultimate medium between us and God: between action and rest, between mode and the <term>privation of mode; *modi privationem*, a mystical state where the soul moves beyond specific "ways" or "methods" of relating to God into a formless, direct experience</term>, and between time and eternity. God works this spiritual touch in us primordially, before all other gifts; yet it is known and felt in us last of all, in its own proper nature. For after we seek God in all our exercises, reaching even to the innermost parts of our spirit through the scrutiny of love, it is only then that we feel the inflow of the gift of grace and of all heavenly gifts. 

We feel this touch in the unity of our highest powers—above reason, yet not without reason—because we perceive ourselves being touched. If, however, we attempt to know what it is or from where it comes, our reason and every created consideration are repelled and fail in that very attempt. 

For even if the air shines with the brightness of the sun, and the eyes are powerful with sharp and healthy sight, if anyone wished to stare with a fixed gaze at the wheel of the sun’s rays, the sight of their eyes would surely be beaten back. They would be frustrated in their attempt and blinded, and would be forced to receive the influence of the rays only passively. According to this likeness: the irradiation of incomprehensible light offers itself in its total magnitude within the unity of the highest powers, so that every created operation—which works through discerning consideration—falters and ceases. Our own practice must then simply endure the divine operation, only com-

...beaten back. Yet this is the source of all gifts. For if we were able to comprehend God by our own power of grasp, he would give himself to us without any intermediary coming between; but this is certainly impossible now, because of the narrowness of our capacity. Therefore, he pours into us his gifts in a way we can receive: according to the measure of our grasp and the nobility of our spiritual exercises. For the fruitful unity of the **Godhead**, hanging above the unity of our own powers, demands a likeness of <term>charity; *charitas*, the highest form of divine love</term> and virtue.

And because of this, we are touched at every moment with a **new touch**, so that we also may always be renewed in a greater likeness. And in this renewal of the touch itself, the spirit falls into <term>hunger and into thirst; *esuries et sitis*, a common mystical metaphor for the soul's yearning for God</term>, wishing to penetrate the surpassing abyss of the Godhead with an eager taste through the impulse of love, so that the starving appetite which it endures might be satisfied. 

From this, at last, an eager and insatiable desire is born: to grasp the ungraspable Good, and to measure the highest and the infinite with its greatest strength—but it labors in vain. For every spirit that loves God—each according to the mode of its own nobility and also according to the touch of divine charity—is carried into God with a starving desire. And yet, relative to our practical desire, God remains eternally incomprehensible and immeasurable. Because of this, an **eternal hunger** remains in us, and—together with all the saints—a longing of everlasting desire. 

However, when our effort meets the immensity of the divine abyss, such a great and immense brightness and burning intent is generated in that very meeting that the spirit, beaten back from its own activity, is forced to <term>keep Sabbath; *feriari*, to rest or cease from work</term>. In its own unity, the intent of felt love is liquefied and almost brought to nothing in the face of the divine operation, behaving like a <note>The author is describing the state of "passivity" where the soul stops trying to reach God through its own efforts and instead lets God act upon it.</note> **pure creature** acting only passively. At this stage, the grace of God and our virtues have become a felt love, affecting its own subject <note>The "subject" here is the human person experiencing the love.</note>, but failing regarding the object <note>The "object" is God, who remains beyond the reach of human effort.</note>; because the spirit...

<summary>Jan van Ruusbroec describes the mystical "hunger and thirst" for God, explaining that while the soul constantly seeks to grasp the infinite, it must eventually fail and become "passive" to allow God to act within it.</summary>

# II

...our spirit, exhausted as it were by its final breath, is stripped of every work and becomes love. It is also a simple and suitable vessel for all gifts, and ready for all virtues. In this sensible love, however, there lives a gushing vein: namely, the irradiation or reaction of God <note>original: *irradiatio scilicet vel reactio dei*; the idea that God's light strikes the soul and the soul "reacts" or reflects it back</note>, which, allowing us no rest at all, excites and revives us, drawing us inward and causing us to flow out in new acts of virtue. Let this be shown to you as the origin of all things and the manner of the virtues.

### ¶ OF THE HIGHEST level of the meeting. Chapter LXIIII.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial V with scrolling vine and leaf patterns on a cross-hatched background.</image-desc> But so that we may build the highest level of the **meeting** <note>original: *obuiationis*; refers to the soul's encounter or "coming-to-meet" God</note> at the end of this book, and join together—as if with a cornerstone—the spiritual and contemplative life, let your charity pay heed for a moment. The immense illumination of God, irradiating us with incomprehensible clarity, being indeed the origin of all gifts and virtues: this very incomprehensible charity shines around us and drenches the <term>fruitive; *fruitiuam*, referring to the blissful, quiet enjoyment of God's presence as an end in itself</term> inclination of our spirit with an incomprehensible light, in which the spirit may sink itself in fruitive rest. For that rest knows no measure and has no end; nor is it known except by itself, that is, through rest. For if it were able to be known or grasped, it would then receive a measure and a limit, and it would not sufficiently restrain our **hunger**, but the rest itself would be turned into perpetual restlessness for us. Because of this, therefore, the simple inclination of deepened love causes the fruitive love of our spirit within us. Since fruitive love lacks an end, it is a kind of **abyss**. The abyss of God, truly, "calls unto the abyss" <note>A reference to Psalm 42:7, "Deep calleth unto deep"</note>—that is, all those who are united to the spirit of God in the embrace of fruitive love. Moreover, this calling is an overflowing of a certain habitual clarity, which, embracing us with the arms of immense love, acts as a shroud of its infinity, so that in the vast...

### ¶ Concerning the first mode of this highest meeting. Chapter LXV.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'H' with a floral and architectural scrollwork background inside a square border.</image-desc>

This highest level of spiritual meeting is practiced in three ways. Sometimes, the spiritual person—by the weight of a <term>fruitive; *fruitivę*, referring to a love that rests in the enjoyment of God as the ultimate goal</term> inclination—plunges themselves simply into divine things, rising above all active practice and all virtues. With the simple gaze of fruitive love looking toward what lies ahead, they meet God immediately in a certain vision. From the divine unity, a certain light shines upon them, showing itself in a threefold likeness. 

For it offers itself as a certain darkness <note>original: *caligo*; often used in mystical theology to describe the "dazzling obscurity" where God's light is too bright for the human intellect</note>, as a certain serenity, and—if we may say so—as a certain nothingness <note>original: *nichilū*</note>. By the immensity of the darkness, the person is inwardly overshadowed; in every way, as if empty of activity, they are deprived of their own power. By the formlessness of the serenity, they lose all consideration and distinction of things, being surrounded and drenched by a simple clarity. By the emptiness of that nothingness, they are compelled to <term>keep the Sabbath; *sabbatizare*, meaning to cease from all personal effort or work to rest in God</term> from every labor. 

For by the operation of divine love, the person is conquered; yet, by the penetrating sharpness of their spirit’s fruitive inclination, they in turn "conquer" God and are made one spirit with him. Through this union, the person obtains a fruitive taste, possessing the essence of the deity; and according to the depth of this essential immersion, they are filled with the immense delights and riches of God. From this fullness, a certain embrace and a wealth of affective love flows out in the unity of the highest powers. From this fullness again, the rush of a river of delightful and penetrating savor descends, gladdening the "city of the heart" and the "suburbs" of the sensory powers; and also their tabernacle, namely the spiritual...

<summary>Ruusbroec describes the first way the soul meets God at the highest level: by plunging into a divine light that appears as darkness, serenity, and nothingness, leading to a state of total rest and union.</summary>
<keywords>mystical union, fruitive love, divine darkness, serenity, nothingness, Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual marriage, Sabbath of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "first mode" of the highest spiritual meeting between the soul and God, emphasizing the "Sabbath rest" of the spirit. It then transitions into the "second mode," which focuses on the active fire of charity and the gift of wisdom.</meta>

...ing the man, the Most High sanctifies him. Inwardly, He renders him immobile and stunned, depriving him of the mastery of his own operation and his own strength, to such an extent that in his innermost parts—in soul and body alike—he knows or feels nothing else except a certain singular brightness, with a sensible sweetness and a penetrating flavor. 

This, then, is the first mode of the highest spiritual meeting: namely, a solemn and intimate <term>Sabbatismus; a mystical Sabbath rest where the soul ceases its own labors to rest in God</term> of the spirit. For by lifting the spiritual man above practice and all virtues, it makes him rest from all things. By bringing him to life in God, it builds a stable and firm foundation for the highest spiritual exercise. However, this mode is hindered whenever the spiritual man is intercepted or shaped inwardly by any occupation or by the mediation of any exercise of the virtues; for this mode is (as we have said) an intimate Sabbath rest of the mind, surpassing and leaving behind all things.

## ¶ Concerning the second mode of the highest meeting. Chapter LXVI

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'N' at the beginning of a paragraph. The letter is set against a square background featuring a dense pattern of scrolling vines and leaves in a Renaissance style.</image-desc>

Sometimes, however, the spiritual man turns affectionately and practically toward divine things: namely, so that he may show reverence and honor to God, offering himself and all his own things as a sacrifice of charity <note>original: *charitatis*; divine love</note>, and may burn and be consumed in the fire of love. In this way, he meets God with a certain intermediary. 

This intermediary is the gift of wisdom, the fountain and origin of all virtues, which directs and impels spiritual men into the acts of the virtues according to the measure of their love. Sometimes, it touches the devout mind with such vehemence and kindles such a flame of love that all gifts and all divine things that can be given—aside from God himself—are small to him and satisfy him not at all. Because they are so insufficient compared to the love of the Beloved, they do not mitigate his impatience, but rather increase it. For he feels in the innermost, penetrating part of his spirit the secret and silent whisper of the divine act, which is the end of all virtues.

<keywords>Sabbatismus, Sabbath rest, mystical union, charity, gift of wisdom, fire of love, divine whisper, spiritual exercises</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter LXVI, focusing on the "second mode" of the soul's meeting with God through the active and burning hunger of love, contrasting it with the "Sabbath rest" of the first mode.</meta>

...and beginning. In this state, as if upon an altar, the spirit offers up sacrifices of virtues to God with the hands of sincere affection. Because love lives within like a vital altar fire, the hunger and thirst for divine love grow so intense that through its own excess—continually sacrificing itself—it completely fails in its own works. At last, exhausted in spirit and dissolved by the fire of love, it is in a certain way annihilated <note>In mystical theology, "annihilation" refers to the soul losing its self-centered will to be entirely filled by God, rather than literal destruction.</note>. 

For the soul hungers and thirsts to taste God with full satisfaction, yet it cannot grasp Him through any human effort. But in each of God’s irradiations <note>original: *irradiationibus*; divine beams of light or spiritual enlightenments</note>, the soul is caught up by God and touched by a fresh movement of love. Indeed, it dies by living, and by dying, it lives again. In this way, the affectionate hunger and thirst of love are continually renewed within it. 

This, then, is the second mode of the highest meeting, belonging to the work <note>original: *negocio*; literally "business" or "activity," referring here to the soul's spiritual labor</note> of inward affection. In this mode, the lover—bearing the likeness of grace—seeks with the highest desire to be united to God. This mode is actually more useful and more laudable for us than the one mentioned previously, because this one is the cause of the other. For no one can achieve <term>quiet; a state of mystical stillness or rest in God</term> above practice <note>original: *praxim*; the active exercise of virtues</note> unless they have first practiced this affectionate and active love. 

For this reason, it is necessary for God’s grace to both precede and follow our active love; that is, grace must be exercised both before and after. Without the operation of love, we can earn no merit, God is never reached, nor can we preserve that which we previously achieved through the works of love. Therefore, let no one who is master of themselves grow sluggish in the idleness of sleep, and let no one refuse to persist in the "business" of love. However, this mode is also hindered whenever a spiritual person dwells too much on any specific gift of God or relies on any created thing. For this mode (as we have said) is a certain hunger that nothing except God is sufficient to satisfy.

## II.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'E' with floral and vine patterns begins the text.</image-desc>

**F**rom these two modes <note>referring to the "Sabbath rest" and the "active hunger" described on previous pages</note>, a third arises: which is the spiritual life, governed by a rule of justice. For God, coming into us continually both through means <note>such as sacraments or scripture</note> and immediately, demands both **fruition** <term>fruition; the soul’s joyful, restful enjoyment of God’s presence</term> and **operation** <term>operation; the active labor of virtues and good works</term> at the same time. Neither is hindered by the other; rather, each is strengthened by its opposite. Therefore, in these two—that is, in work and in rest—the spiritual person possesses their life, remaining whole and undivided in both. For they are entirely in God, where they rest in fruition, and entirely in themselves, where they love in active practice. 

However, it is required that both—namely rest and work—be renewed at every moment. And the justice of the spirit, hearing the voice of the inward taskmaster <note>the voice of God or conscience calling for spiritual effort</note>, desires to fulfill what is asked continually. Wherefore, in each of God’s **irradiations** <note>original: *irradiationibus*; divine beams of light or spiritual enlightenments</note>, the spirit is turned toward divine things both in practice and in fruition. By this process, the person is renewed in all virtues and, simultaneously, is absorbed more deeply into the rest of fruition. 

Hence God bestows Himself and His gifts in a single boon; and the spirit, in each of its turnings toward the divine, gives back itself and all its works. For through the simple irradiation of God and through the fruitive inclination and immersion of love, our spirit is united to God; and without the interruption of change, it is led into the rest of the "royal bedchamber" <note>a traditional mystical metaphor for the most intimate union between the soul and God</note>. 

But through the gifts of understanding and wisdom, the spirit is touched in its active practice; it is enlightened and continually set on fire for the **business of love** <note>original: *amoris negocio*; the active, sometimes difficult labor of living out one's faith</note>. Whatever can be desired is shown to the spirit in a spiritual way, and the prize <note>original: *brauium*; the reward at the end of a race</note> of its course stands before its mental eyes. Therefore, the soul hungers and thirsts greedily, because it sees the food of angels and the heavenly drink. It labors mightily in the business of love, because it contemplates its own rest. It is a pilgrim, yet it sees its homeland from afar. Like a champion of love, it strives to obtain victory with all its strength, because it beholds the crown due to the victor. Furthermore...

### THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS OF GOD ###

consolation, joy, peace, beauty, riches, and infinite such things gladdening the human heart, are shown in God to the eyes of **luminous reason** <note>original: *luminosę rationis*; the intellect enlightened by divine grace</note> under spiritual likenesses. Therefore, because of this kind of showing, and because of the touch of God, love does not desert its **activity** <term>operation; the active labor of virtues and good works</term>. For the just man has established a true life—namely, both **fruitive** <term>fruition; the soul’s restful, joyful enjoyment of God’s presence</term> and active—equally in the spirit. This life will indeed remain forever, but after this life, it will be changed into a higher state. Thus, the spiritual man is just, hungering and thirsting for justice. For both by intimate love he approaches God through internal activity, and by a fruitive inclination, he plunges himself into God, possessing eternal rest. Moreover, he remains continually in God, and yet through common **charity** <note>original: *charitas*; the selfless love for God and neighbor</note> in virtues and in justice, he goes out to all creatures. 

This, then, is the highest degree of the whole spiritual life. But whoever does not possess activity and rest in a single exercise has by no means attained this justice. However, the just man cannot be hindered in his turning toward God, since he is freely turned toward divine things both fruitively and practically. 

Furthermore, man is like a **double-faced mirror** receiving the images of objects on each face. According to the upper face, he receives God with all His gifts. According to the lower face, he receives the images of sensible things <note>objects perceived by the physical senses</note> introduced through the windows of the senses. He can therefore, whenever he wishes, turn himself toward interior things and, without hindrance, exercise this justice. But while man lives in this mortal life, he is changeable. And for that reason, he frequently turns to exterior things without necessity and without the command of luminous reason, attending to the business of the senses; and from this, he contracts the stains of **venial sins** <term>venial sins; minor spiritual failings that do not break the soul's relationship with God but require cleansing</term>. But in truth, all venial failings are so utterly consumed in the loving turning of the just man toward God, just as... <meta>catchword: just as (original: queadmo-)</meta>

### ¶ CONCERNING THE ORIGIN of the sect of the spiritually idle, who live in a contrary manner in the first way. Chapter 68.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut initial 'S' featuring scrolling foliage and floral motifs within a square border.</image-desc>It now comes to mind to refute, or rather to lament, certain people who display an appearance of wonderful holiness, yet oppose the healthy labor of prescribed morals and all virtues with the empty **idleness** <term>ocio; a state of inactivity or leisure, here used pejoratively to mean spiritual laziness</term> of their life. Now, therefore, let everyone consider and examine themselves concerning this matter. For whoever among men is not divinely illuminated nor drawn by love is completely untouched; they have neither an active approach to God with affection, nor a simple inclination of love toward **fruitive rest** <note>original: *requiem fruitivam*; the peaceful enjoyment of God's presence</note>. For this reason, they cannot be unified with God. Since nature is universally **reflexive** <note>original: *reflexiva*; the tendency of a created being to turn back or focus upon itself rather than on God</note>, those who are devoid of supernatural and free love inevitably turn back toward themselves in all things, and they seek rest in all external things. For every creature is naturally inclined toward rest; and therefore, rest is sought in many ways by both the good and the bad. 

Do you wish to know the cause and origin of the natural rest of the spirit? Indeed, when all the powers of the soul are **on holiday** <note>original: *feriantibus*; a metaphorical way of saying the soul's faculties have ceased their usual work</note>—namely, the apprehensive powers from the reception of sensible and intelligible images, and the appetitive powers from every operation of affection and will—the soul soon falls into a rest similar to sleep, naturally or even necessarily, like a body with limbs cut off or made immobile. Anyone can easily establish this state for themselves and possess it naturally without **grace** <term>gratia; the free and unmerited favor of God, which Ruusbroec argues is necessary for true spiritual rest</term>, if they remove themselves from the images of things and empty themselves of all activities of their powers. Yet a true lover and worshiper of God can by no means desire to rest in this way; for **charity** <note>original: *charitas*; the highest form of Christian love</note> and the internal touch of grace know nothing of the idleness of empty rest.

<vocab>sect of the spiritually idle, natural rest, soul, grace, love, charity</vocab>

...ceased. And therefore, although the soul of a devoted and fervent lover might sometimes slumber due to weariness, a sudden lapse, or bodily fatigue, it would not allow itself to remain lying within itself for long, as if in the sleepy bed of natural rest. 

Now, the practice of this rest is a quiet sitting, full of **idleness** <term>ocio; here referring to a state of total spiritual and physical inactivity</term>, with every internal and external exercise set aside. This is done so that the removal of activity becomes the discovery of rest, and the continuation of idleness becomes the unimpeded holding of that rest. 

But every practice of this kind of rest is illicit. It causes in a person a kind of blindness toward justice, as it were, and a certain internal **reclining** <note>original: *supinationem*; literally lying on one's back, here used as a metaphor for a passive, self-absorbed spiritual state</note> of the vacant soul, which intends no action at all. This rest is nothing other than a certain spiritual idleness, in which, while the soul’s powers are **on holiday** <note>original: *feriantibus*; a common mystical term for the faculties of the soul ceasing their natural functions</note> from every task, the mind is naturally lulled to sleep, finally forgetting itself in all things regarding any active work. 

Furthermore, this rest is the opposite of the supernatural rest that is possessed in God. For the latter is a loving immersion of the spirit, with a simple gaze into incomprehensible clarity. For that **deifying rest** <term>deifica quies; a peace that transforms the soul to be more like God</term> which is always actively sought with the deepest desires, is continually found through a loving inclination toward **enjoyment** <note>original: *fruitive*; the "fruitio" or delightful experience of resting in God's presence</note>. It is also perpetually possessed through the absorption of love; and even when it has been possessed, it is sought again and again for that very reason. 

This rest, I say, is as much exalted above natural rest as God is exalted above every creature. Therefore, all those are deceived who turn their intention toward themselves and recline inwardly in a natural, idle rest. They do not seek God with inward affection, and for this reason, they never find the rest of **fruitive love**. For the rest they possess consists only in the idleness of the soul and body, toward which they are inclined by both nature and habit. Truly, God cannot be found in this rest; instead, it is a blind guide leading the blind into

## II.

...introduces the **sleepiness of idleness** <term>somnolentiam ocij; a state of spiritual lethargy or passive withdrawal where the mind is blank but not focused on God</term>. Even pagans, Jews, and whatever sinners are wrapped in filth can very easily find this state, provided that the guilt of their conscience does not disturb them with the restlessness of remorse, and they know how to empty themselves of the forms of things and of all the acts of the soul's powers. 

There is, however, in this kind of sleepiness of idleness, a delightful and great rest. Furthermore, this rest is not a sin in itself, because it is naturally implanted equally in all people, if only everyone knew how to empty themselves for this purpose. But when someone tries to use it with affectation <note>original: *affectate*; meaning to deliberately or unnaturally seek this state for its own sake</note> and to possess it without the acts of the virtues, they soon obtain a self-complacency and a sense of grace, and fall into a **supernatural pride** <note>This refers to a spiritual egoism where one believes they have reached the heights of contemplation through their own effort rather than divine gift</note>, from which one very rarely recovers. 

For such a person sometimes thinks they are that very thing, or that they possess that which they may perhaps never actually reach. And so, while any idle person possesses a fictitious rest in this sleepy idleness, they claim that every loving approach to God would be an impediment to their rest. They are not easily awakened from the sleep of their idleness; instead, they settle down entirely quiet within themselves. In doing so, they live in a way that is contrary to the **first mode of the highest meeting** <note>Referring to the author's earlier definition of the soul’s active encounter with the Divine</note> with God who unites the spirit. 

This is indeed the beginning and the root of all spiritual error. We have an example of this in the good and bad angels. For the good angels, having turned toward their Creator with all the gifts they had received and out of a love of **fruition** <term>fruitionis; the joyful, active, and final enjoyment of God's presence</term>, found perpetual happiness. But the bad angels, reflected back into themselves and seeking rest in themselves by pleasing themselves in their own natural light, enjoyed only a brief and illicit rest. Blinded and separated from the **eternal light** <note>original: *aeterno lumine*; the light of God</note> by a great chasm placed between them, they fell irreparably into the outer darkness and into eternal restlessness.

He uses the fall of the rebel angels as a cautionary tale of choosing self-centered rest over active love for God.</summary>

...ly. And therefore, although the soul of a devoted and fervent lover might sometimes drowse because of weariness, sudden intrusion, or bodily fatigue, it would not allow itself to lie back within itself for any length of time, as if in the sleepy bed of **natural rest** <term>naturalis quies; a psychological state of calm or "emptiness" reached by human effort rather than divine grace</term>. 

Now, the practice of this rest is a quiet sitting, full of idleness, with all internal and external activity removed; so that the removal of action is the discovery of rest, and the continuation of idleness is the unimpeded holding of that rest. But every use of this kind of rest is illicit <note>original: *illicitus*; meaning spiritually improper or deceptive in this context</note>. This is because it causes in a person a kind of blindness to justice, and a certain internal reclining of the vacant soul, which intends no action at all. Nor is that rest anything other than a certain **idleness of the soul** <term>ociositas animæ; a state where the soul's faculties are dormant but not engaged with God</term>, in which—with the faculties taking a holiday from all work—the mind is naturally put to sleep, eventually forgetting itself in all things regarding any task whatsoever. 

Furthermore, this rest is the opposite of the **supernatural rest** which is possessed in God. For that higher rest is a loving immersion of the spirit, with a simple gaze into **incomprehensible clarity** <note>This refers to the "Divine Dark" or the blinding light of God's essence which the human mind cannot fully grasp</note>. For that **deifying rest** <term>deifica quies; a state of being transformed by God's presence</term>, which is always actively sought through inward desires, is continually found in an enjoyable way by a loving inclination. It is perpetually possessed through the absorption of love; and even when it has been attained, it is sought no less because of this, again and again. 

That rest, I say, is as much exalted above natural rest as God is exalted above every creature. Therefore, all those are deceived who, turning their intention toward themselves, recline inwardly into a natural, idle rest. They seek God with very little inward affection, and for this reason, they do not find the peace of **enjoyable love** <note>original: *fruitiui amoris*; "fruitive love" is a key term for Ruusbroec, referring to the enjoyment of God's presence for His own sake rather than for personal benefit</note>. ## II

introduces the **sleepiness of idleness** <term>somnolentia ocij; a state of spiritual lethargy or false peace</term>. Which pagans, Jews, and any sinners wrapped in filth can find most easily—provided that the guilt of conscience and the restlessness of remorse does not disturb them, and they know how to empty themselves of the forms of things and of all the acts of the soul's powers. Moreover, in this kind of sleepy idleness, there is a delightful and great rest. Otherwise, this rest is not a sin in itself, because it is naturally implanted in everyone equally, if all knew how to empty themselves equally for this purpose. 

But when someone wishes to use it willfully and possess it without the acts of virtues: soon, having attained self-complacency and a sense of grace, they slide into **supernatural pride** <term>superbia supernaturalis; a spiritual arrogance where one believes they have reached the highest state of holiness through their own psychological calm</term>, from which they very rarely recover. For they sometimes think they are that thing, or have that thing, which they perhaps will never reach. 

Therefore, while any idle person possesses a fictitious rest in sleepy idleness, and claims that every loving approach toward God would be an impediment to their rest, and is not easily awakened from the sleep of their idleness: they settle down entirely quiet within themselves, living in opposition to the first mode of the **highest meeting** <note>original: *summa obuiatio*; likely referring to a specific stage of mystical union discussed earlier in the treatise where the spirit meets God</note> with God, who unites the spirit. This is truly the beginning and the root of every spiritual error. 

We have an example of this in the good and bad angels. Indeed, the good angels, having turned toward their Creator with all the gifts they had received, out of a love for **enjoyment** <term>fruitio; a technical term in medieval mysticism for the blissful, resting experience of God's essence</term>, found perpetual happiness. But the evil ones, reflected back into themselves, and seeking rest in themselves by pleasing themselves in their **natural light** <note>In medieval theology, this refers to the innate intellectual power of a creature, separate from the "supernatural light" of God's grace</note>, enjoyed a brief and illicit rest. And having been blinded and separated from the eternal light by a **great chasm** <note>original: *chao magno*; a reference to the "great gulf" mentioned in the parable of Lazarus and the Rich Man in Luke 16:26</note> placed between them, they fell irreparably into the outer darkness and into eternal restlessness.

<header>OF THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

### ¶On the spiritual vices of those idle ones who are opposed in the second way. Chapter 69.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring intricate foliate patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>
Furthermore, when anyone desires to possess an idle peace without an inward and affective approach toward God, they are already prepared to fall into any error. This is because they have turned away from God and turned toward themselves by **natural love** <term>amor naturalis; a love rooted in one's own psychological needs and self-interest rather than divine grace</term>, desiring and seeking sweetness and comforts, and all other things toward which they are moved by their internal appetite. Hence, such a person is proven to be like a **merchant** <note>original: *mercatori similis*; Ruusbroec frequently uses the "merchant" as a metaphor for those who perform religious duties only to get something back from God, such as peace or status</note>: for in all their actions they are bent back upon themselves, intending and seeking their own rest and profit more than the honor of God. Therefore, living in this kind of natural love, they possess their soul in impatience and in the stubbornness of their own will.

Some, however, performing hard works of penance, lead an austere life so that through this they may be more widely known and broadcast under the name of extraordinary holiness, and furthermore, that they may deserve a sublime reward from God. For natural love is favorable and benevolent toward itself; it gladly receives the little branches of worldly honor in this life, and the rewards of eternal happiness in the future. Some desire and choose many things, and ask many specific favors from God with great passion—and these people are very often seduced. For sometimes, through the hidden workings of the demon, they obtain what they desire. Soon ascribing this to their own holiness, they judge themselves to be more worthy than everyone else. 

For they are proud, not having been touched by God nor spiritually enlightened. And for this reason, they settle down within themselves in an **idle peace** <term>quiete ociosa; a state of stagnant psychological stillness that lacks active love</term>, and they are greatly delighted by a tiny bit of comfort because they are ignorant of the **sober delights** <note>original: *sobrias delicias*; a common mystical term for the refined, non-emotional joy of true spiritual union</note> of true holiness. Moreover, they are inclined toward a taste for internal sweetness according to their own appetite, and toward every spiritual advantage of nature, which

<header>II 78</header>

Not without reason is it called **spiritual luxury** <term>spiritualis luxuria; the seeking of personal emotional or spiritual pleasure for its own sake rather than for God's glory</term>, since it is a certain inclination of **natural love** <term>amor naturalis; an affection rooted in one's own nature and self-interest</term> which, always reflected back upon itself, seeks its own comforts in all things. Idle people of this kind are also always puffed up with **spiritual pride** and are, in all things, masters of their own will. And therefore they are carried by such stubborn affections toward those things they desire, and by such bold petitions for what they wish to obtain from God, that they are frequently deceived, and some are even obsessed by a demon. 

The life of these people—if it should be called life and not rather death—is contrary to **charity** and to that loving approach by which the true lover offers himself and all that is his as a sacrifice for the honor and love of God. Nothing else can satisfy such a lover, and nothing else can suffice for him, except the highest, incomprehensible good: which is God. For charity is the bond of love transporting us into God; through it, by renouncing ourselves, we are united to God, and God to us. 

But natural love is always reflected back upon itself and its own profit, remaining always alone. Nevertheless, it is as similar to charity in its outward acts as two hairs from the same head are similar to one another. The intentions of the two, however, are altogether dissimilar. For the true lover always intends, seeks, and desires the honor of God; but the natural lover always intends himself and his own gain. 

From this it happens that, with natural love outweighing charity in the opposite direction, the natural lover falls into four sins at once. Indeed, he incurs **spiritual pride, greed, gluttony, and luxury**. Through these, in their proper order, Adam fell in the **earthly paradise** <note>original: *paraiso terrestri*; the Garden of Eden</note>, and in him all of human nature sinned. For indeed, by natural love, he loved himself inordinately; and because of this, turning himself away from God, he contemptuously disobeyed His command through pride. By the instinct of greed, he lusted after knowledge and wisdom. By glu- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing with "gulositas" (gluttony) on the following page.</note>

## II

and all just men. The following discourse will now teach how their erroneous and visionary <note>original: "phantasticam"; here meaning an opinion based on delusions or mental imaginings rather than divine revelation</note> opinion can be clearly recognized in words and deeds. Therefore, because of the natural rest which they feel in themselves and which they possess by remaining idle, they say that they are truly free and immediately united to God. They say, moreover, that they are raised by the spirit of God above the whole worship of the Church, above the exercises of the faithful, and above the commands of the Lord; they claim to be above the law as well, and above all works of virtue which can be performed in any way.

For they consider this idleness to be of such great dignity that it ought not to be hindered by any good works whatsoever, because it surpasses all virtues in the nobility of its status. For the sake of this thing, they sink into themselves in a state of pure passivity <term>passibilitate; a state of being acted upon rather than acting, often associated with a controversial "quietist" stillness</term> without any action turning upward or downward. They behave, for instance, like an instrument that is idle in itself, passively waiting for the pleasure of its maker while he wishes to work within it. For (as they say), if they did anything of their own accord, they would hinder God in His action. 

For this reason, they altogether repudiate all virtues. They are so inwardly vacant in their idleness that they wish neither to give thanks to God nor to offer praises. They neither know how, nor do they wish, to understand, to love, to will, to pray, or to desire; for whatever they could pray for or desire, they believe they have already possessed in their own judgment. 

If, therefore, they are "poor in spirit" <note>A reference to the first Beatitude (Matthew 5:3), which these groups interpreted as a total annihilation of the individual will</note> because they have equally left all things by extinguishing their will, they nevertheless remain empty of all judgment of their own will. Because of this inward idleness of the spirit, it seems to them that they have surpassed all things, and that they already possess in the quiet of idleness that very thing for which every ecclesiastical worship was instituted. Furthermore, as they say, no creature—not even God Himself—is able to bestow anything upon them or take anything away; for they hold in their estimation every spiritual and physical exercise, and all virtues...

It explains their rejection of church authority, prayer, and virtuous works, claiming that personal action "hinders" God's work in the soul.</summary>
