# THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE

they have transcended their own <note>original: "propria"; referring to their own actions or individual faculties</note> and have arrived at a certain pure leisure of the spirit in which they are liberated from all virtues. They require (as they say) greater labor to be liberated from virtues in this leisure than to attain them by working. For this reason, they wish to be altogether free and to obey no one at all—not the Pope, of course, nor bishops, nor prelates. Hence, even if in outward appearance they sometimes pretend to obey, they are subject to no one in internal obedience, will, or deed. For from all the rituals and customs which our mother, the Catholic Church, practices with humble devotion, they "sabbatize" <note>original: "sabbatizant"; to keep a Sabbath, here meaning to cease all religious activity</note> in every way through an idle freedom of the spirit. But the enemies of human salvation, seeing them, rightly mock their Sabbaths. In this regard, they falsely and vainly claim that anyone who persists in virtues and desires to do the most pleasing will of God is imperfect; they claim this because such people toil in the harvest of virtues and are ignorant of the dignity of this spiritual idleness. Truly, in their own estimation, they are exalted above all the choirs of angels and saints, and even above every reward of any kind of merit. Because of this, they also say that they can no longer progress in virtues, nor earn any more reward, nor can they ever commit any sins. For they say they are deprived of all will, and that they have returned their spirits to God in idle rest; they say they are united to Him and utterly reduced to nothingness within themselves. And for this reason, they can freely do whatever the nature of the body desires, since they have arrived at the state of innocence <term>status innocentie; a theological term for humanity's condition before the Fall, which these sectarians claimed to have regained</term>, and they say no yoke of the law is imposed upon them. Hence, if it should happen naturally that they are moved toward any desirable thing—the non-attainment of which would hinder the leisure of their spirit—they immediately satisfy the desires of their natur[e] <note>The text cuts off here, continuing with "nature" or "naturally" on the next page.</note>

For this reason, they think nothing of fasts, the celebration of sacred festivals, and other ecclesiastical institutions; at most, they might occasionally pretend to observe them just to keep up appearances before other people. However, according to Holy Scripture, the teaching of CHRIST, and our faith, it is clearly proven that they are deeply deceived.

### CONCERNING A CERTAIN OTHER TYPE
### OF THE SPIRITUALLY IDLE. Chapter 71.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring floral motifs and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>
There is another type of the spiritually idle who are, in part, the opposite of those mentioned before. They also say, as did the others, that they are idle from all works, and that they themselves are nothing other than a certain instrument <term>instrumentum (instrument): the idea that the human person is merely a tool through which God acts, lacking any personal will or responsibility</term> by which God works what He wills and how He wills. Because of this, they claim to be established in a state of pure passivity <note>original: "passibilitate"; the state of being acted upon by an external force rather than acting for oneself</note>, without any action of their own, even while they are personally performing works through grace. For this reason, they call themselves "the passible ones of God" <note>original: "dei passibiles"</note>, claiming that they personally do nothing at all. But whatever they are seen to do, they falsely claim that it is not they acting, but God acting through them as if through an instrument. These people also say that they cannot sin, because it is God who acts and works in them, while they, being vacant in their idleness, do not intend anything for themselves. Therefore, whatever...

# ON THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE

Whatever God has willed is accomplished through them, and nothing else. These people have laid themselves down inwardly into a certain idleness <note>original: "ocium"; here referring to a state of spiritual inactivity or quietism</note>, carrying out the business of doing no work, and exercising no choice of things at all. They also maintain a humble and dejected outward manner; they know very well how to tolerate adverse circumstances with equanimity, for in their own estimation, they are merely an instrument <term>instrumentum: a tool or vessel that has no will of its own</term> by which God operates according to the good pleasure of His will. In many of their ways and actions, these people appear similar to other good and faithful Christians, yet in certain matters, they are the opposite. For they are moved to any action by an internal instinct—whether these actions are similar to those of others or different—and they suspect that these impulses proceed from the Holy Spirit. In this and similar matters, they are entirely deceived. For the Holy Spirit does not will, nor persuade, nor perform those things which are contrary to the doctrine of CHRIST or dissimilar to the Christian religion. 

These people are difficult to recognize, except by a person possessing great discernment of spirits <term>discretio spirituum: the spiritual gift of distinguishing between divine inspiration, human impulse, and diabolical influence</term> and who is enlightened by the light of divine truth. Indeed, some of them are quite subtle; they know how to cloak their errors perfectly and defend them with clever glosses <note>original: "glosis"; interpretive explanations or commentaries used to justify their behavior</note>. They are so stubborn in their own opinions, and they possess themselves with such a powerful mastery of their own will, that they would sooner choose to undergo any kind of death than to revoke even one point they have conceived. For they consider themselves holier than everyone else, and believe they are more fully enlightened by the light of divine truth than all other living people. 

Furthermore, these people are the opposite of the ones described previously in this regard: they claim that they are able to make progress and gain merit. For those others (as we said before) say they cannot progress any further, because they have already possessed themselves in the quiet solitude of their idleness. But where there is no exercise or work at all, there is absolutely no progress. Behold, all of these people are utterly childish and the worst of all living things; therefore, they are to be avoided like the devil. But if one understands well...

<summary>This page analyzes the psychology of a heretical group that claims every internal impulse is a command from the Holy Spirit, making them immune to correction and prone to spiritual pride. Ruusbroec distinguishes them from other quietists by their claim that they can still earn merit despite their supposed passivity.</summary>

<keywords>quietism, spiritual pride, discernment of spirits, Holy Spirit, heresy, Jan van Ruusbroec, merit, stubbornness</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of a group of "spiritually idle" individuals—often identified by historians as the Brethren of the Free Spirit—who claimed that their total passivity made them perfect vessels for God's actions, thereby exempting them from moral effort or religious duty.</meta>

...you have read in the doctrine which we have spoken to you in many ways in the previous sections: you clearly prove these people to be deceived. For by the perversity of their life, they oppose God, justice, and all the saints. They are also the worst precursors of the Antichrist <note>original: "antichristi pessimi percursores"</note>, preparing his way for every heresy. Since indeed they wish to be free, they are empty of virtues and not subject to the Lord's commandments. They are also idle <note>original: "ociosi"; refers to their doctrine of spiritual inactivity or quietism</note>, claiming to be united to God without the "glue" of charity <term>charitas: the theological virtue of selfless, divine love</term>; they claim to be contemplators of the divine without a loving gaze, and the most holy of living beings without any work of holiness. 

Moreover, they say they rest in Him whom they do not love, and that they are raised up into Him whom they neither desire nor will. They also talk nonsense <note>original: "fabulantur"; literally 'to tell fables' or 'to chatter'</note> that they take leisure from all virtues, and also rest from every approach to God, so as not to impede God in His action. Indeed, they confess God as the creator and Lord of all, and yet they wish neither to give Him thanks nor praises. They admit He is of infinite power and wealth, yet they chatter that He can bestow absolutely nothing on them, take nothing away, and that they themselves can neither improve nor merit. 

Yet some of them hold the opposite, saying they merit <term>meritum: the idea that human actions, aided by grace, can deserve a reward from God</term> more than other men because God performs their works, and they themselves—being vacant in idleness—simply endure God's operation. Rather, they claim they are moved by the Spirit of God as "sons of God," in which (as they say) the highest merit consists. 

But all these things are erroneous, indeed impossible: because the operation <term>operatio: the act of working or functioning</term> of God in Himself is both immutable and eternal, seeing as He works Himself and nothing else. And indeed, the perfection or merit of any creature <note>original: "creaturę"; in this context, any created being, specifically a human</note> does not consist in this internal divine operation. For here, there is nothing other than God, who in Himself can neither be brought low nor exalted, neither diminished nor increased. 

Creatures, however, through the flowing power of God, have their own operations, both in nature and in grace <note>original: "grā"</note>, and also even in glory <note>original: "glīa"</note>. Whence, while these works are completed in grace temporally, they persevere in glory eternally. But if it were possible (which is entirely impossible) that a creature, as far as its every mode of operation...

<summary>This page refutes the "Free Spirit" heresy, arguing that true spiritual life requires active virtue and love rather than a total, passive "idleness." The author asserts that God's internal eternal work is distinct from the moral and spiritual actions that humans must perform through grace.</summary>

The previous page established that these individuals claim a state of total passivity to avoid moral responsibility. This page argues that such passivity reduces a human to the status of an inanimate object and likens this state to the condition of the damned, contrasting it with the perpetual, active love of Christ and the saints.</meta>

->DEOR.NVP.SP.<-
<note>original: "De Ornatu Spiritualium Nuptiarum," or "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage," a major work by Jan van Ruusbroec.</note>

...it would reduce [the soul] to nothing; and therefore it would be idle just as it was when it was not yet a creature. That is to say, it would become one with God in every way, and would possess no other being than what it had then. In this state, without a doubt—just as back then—it could merit <term>merit (meritum): the theological concept that human actions, performed through grace, can deserve a reward from God</term> nothing at all. Nor would it be any more holy or blessed than an unfeeling stone or a piece of wood. For without our own active effort, without love <note>original: "dilectio"</note> and the knowledge of God, we cannot be blessed. God, however, would be blessed in Himself, as He was then and always; but no good would come to a creature of this kind. It is clear, therefore, that this idleness <note>original: "ociositatem"; here referring to the "quietist" heresy of total spiritual inactivity</note> is altogether imaginary and erroneous. For these idle people wish to cloak every perversity and every malice under the appearance of good, and to rank this state in dignity above all virtues.

They also strive to hide their wickedness with subtle words, so that they might present an image of better things. All these people are contrary to God and all the saints; instead, they are most like the damned spirits. For the damned spirits lack love and knowledge; they are idle from every praise of God and giving of thanks, as well as from the loving turning toward God. And this is the cause of their eternal damnation. For these idle people, only this remains: that their time be changed into eternity, and God's justice be revealed in their works.

**CHRIST**, however, the Son of God, who according to His humanity is the head of all the righteous and the rule for living: He was, is, and shall be forever with all His members—that is, with all the saints—loving, desiring, giving thanks, and praising His Father who is in heaven. This is true even though His soul is and was united and happy in the divine being. But He never arrived at this sort of idleness, nor will He ever arrive at it in eternity. For His glorious soul and all the blessed have a perpetual approach of love; they are like those who hunger and thirst for the divine sweetness (which they have already tasted) and cannot be satisfied in that regard for all eternity. Yet the same soul enjoys...

<summary>The author argues that true union with God requires active love and knowledge, rather than the "idleness" of the Free Spirit heretics, which he compares to the state of the damned. He cites Christ as the ultimate model of a soul that remains active in love and praise even while being united with the Divine.</summary>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page concludes Book II of "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage." Following the previous page's critique of "idle" or quietist heresies—which claimed a state of total passivity—this text describes the "common life" (vita communis). In this state, the soul is not passive like a stone, but is simultaneously active in love and resting in God’s unity.</meta>

# II
<note>original: "II" refers to the end of Book II of the treatise.</note>

**CHRIST’S** soul and all the saints [dwell] in the surpassing of all desire, where one thing alone exists: namely, the eternal happiness of all God’s chosen. From this, to enjoy and to work at the same time is the blessedness of **CHRIST** and all the blessed. This is also the life of all good people, each according to the measure of their love. Moreover, there is a certain justice that will never perish, but is unfailing and perpetual; therefore, imitating the examples of the saints, let us adorn ourselves with virtues and good works, both inwardly and outwardly. 

Lovingly and humbly, before the sight of the divine majesty, let us sacrifice ourselves with all our works; and thus we shall encounter God through the mediation of His gifts. We shall also be touched by affectionate love <note>original: "dilectio"</note> and filled with a common goodness. By this, it will happen that we flow out and flow back <note>This "ebbing and flowing" is a central image for Ruusbroec, describing the soul's movement out toward others in charity and back into God in contemplation.</note> in truest charity within simple peace, stably, perseveringly, and firmly remaining in the divine likeness. 

Truly, through this likeness, through <term>fruition (fruitio): the mystical experience of "enjoying" God’s presence as an end in itself</term> and divine clarity, in a happy stream we shall be plunged into the deifying unity <note>original: "deificam unitatem"; the process of being "made God-like" through union with the Divine</note>, meeting God directly through Himself in the rest of enjoyment. At last, it will come to pass that, remaining eternally, we may flow out continually; and flowing out continuously, we may flow back into that very same thing without the shadowing of change. For through these things, the true, inward, and spiritual life is possessed most fully in all perfection. Which may He deign to grant us, He who is God, blessed forever.

The End of the second book of 
the Adornment of Spiritual Marriage.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book III of Jan van Ruusbroec's "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage." While Book II focused on the "Interior Life" and the balance between action and rest, Book III elevates the discussion to the "Super-essential Life," the highest state of mystical union where the soul moves beyond its own intellect into the direct light of God.</meta>

<header>THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

### THIRD Book on the adornment of the super-essential and
### contemplative life. Chapter I.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial letter 'A' at the start of the chapter, featuring ornate foliage and vine motifs within a square border.</image-desc>

The lover of God, burning with inward devotion, possessing God in fruitive rest <note>original: "quiete fruitiva"; the specific mystical experience of "enjoying" God as an ultimate end</note>, and possessing himself in a burning, active love, while directing his whole life toward virtues with justice: through these three things, and also through the hidden manifestation of God, the devoted lover is led—as if into the bridegroom’s bedchamber—into the contemplative life. I speak of a lover who is fiery with inward charity and just; one whom God, out of His own generosity, deigns to choose and lift up to super-essential contemplation <note>original: "contemplationem superessentialem"; a state "beyond being" or beyond the reach of human nature and reason</note>. In this state, God is seen under a certain divine light, and according to the manner in which God sees Himself, though still short of the state of perfected heavenly happiness. 

This contemplation, moreover, is preeminent in its purity and clarity. It sets us above our own intellects, because it is a singular kind of adornment, a heavenly crown, and the eternal reward of all the virtues. Indeed, no one can reach this contemplation by depth of knowledge, or by the subtlety of intelligence, or by any human exercises whatsoever. Instead, he whom God wishes to unite to Himself by His own Spirit, and whom He deigns to illuminate Himself—that person will be able to contemplate God, and no other. 

For the hidden nature of the Divinity, according to the mode of the Persons <note>This refers to the Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit</note>, contemplates and loves actively for all eternity. Yet, according to the unity of the essence, it always enjoys a certain embrace of the Persons in the Spirit. In this very embrace, which is celebrated deep within the essential unity of the Godhead, all devoted spirits are made one with God in a deep, overflowing stream of love. They are not just any kind of "one," but the very same thing which is the essence of God in itself, in the manner that deifying happiness <note>original: "deifica beatitudo"</note> requires. And in this sublime and divine unity of God, the Father is the origin and the beginning of every work that is done both in heaven and on earth; and He cries out...

<summary>This page introduces the final stage of the spiritual journey, the "super-essential" contemplative life, where the soul is lifted by God's grace into a direct experience of the Divine essence. Ruusbroec explains that this union is a gift of God rather than a result of human intellect or effort.</summary>

<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, contemplation, super-essential life, Trinity, divine union, deification, mysticism, grace</keywords>

<header>III</header>

...in the very secret sanctuary of the spirit, as if with a cry in the middle of a silent night. And it says: "Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him." This word, in this third short treatise <note>original: "tractaticulo"</note>, we wish to explain and declare (with the Lord’s inspiration) concerning the adornment of the **super-essential** <term>super-essential: a state "beyond being" where the soul transcends its own created nature to meet God's essence directly</term> and contemplative life, which is the end of all holiness and of every life that is lived eternally. 

But very few people arrive at this God-making contemplation <note>original: "deificam contemplationem"</note>, both because of their own unfitness and because of the "hiding place" of the light in which they must then contemplate. Therefore, we believe that no one will fully understand the meaning of the things to be said in this short treatise through the teaching of any science or through innate cleverness. 

For any words at all, and whatever can be understood or taught in a human way, are far removed and far below the truth—which we now intend to stammer in shadowy words rather than to speak out clearly. But he who is united to God and illuminated by the light of this truth can understand it through the truth itself. Since to comprehend and understand God above all likeness, just as He is in Himself, is already to be "God with God" <note>original: "esse deum cum deo"; Ruusbroec refers to a mystical union where the soul is transformed by grace into a divine likeness, though remaining a creature</note> immediately, and without any intervening "otherness" or any hindering obstacle. 

Wherefore, I earnestly ask those who do not understand the things to be said in this short treatise, or who do not feel them in the fruitive <note>original: "fruitiva"; the soul's experience of "enjoying" or resting in the ultimate possession of God</note> unity of their spirit: do not cast down our words through a rash judgment or a sinister interpretation. Instead, wonder at them humbly as if in ignorance, so that by your very humility before the Lord, you may deserve to understand. 

For the things we are now about to say, the Lord favoring us, are true. **CHRIST**, the eternal truth, spoke them Himself in many places in the Gospel, if only we were able to bring forward every one of His testimonies on this matter. For this reason, it is necessary for one who would understand these things...

<header>THE ADORNMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS</header>

...to be dead to oneself and to live in God; and to turn one's face toward the eternal light in the depth of one's spirit. There, the hidden truth itself manifests itself with no mediating means coming between. For indeed the heavenly Father, since he is the Father of light, wants us to be divinely seeing. And therefore, in the secret place of our spirit, he speaks through himself eternally with absolutely nothing mediating, and with no changing shadows <note>original: "nulla vicissitudine obumbrare"; referring to God's unchanging nature compared to the shifting shadows of the physical world</note>, a single inscrutable word and not many, in which word indeed he speaks out himself and all things together. This word indeed sounds as nothing other than "Behold" or "See." It is the coming forth and the generation of the Son of eternal light, in whom all blessedness is known and seen.

### CONCERNING THE THREE requirements for the super-essential theological vision. Chapter II.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop cap 'W' featuring scrolling foliage designs inside a square border.</image-desc>So that the spirit may contemplate God through itself in this divine light, with no mediating means coming between, three things are necessarily required. First, indeed, it is required that one be outwardly well-ordered in all virtues, and inwardly free from all mediating obstacles. One must even be so at leisure in mind during all exterior acts as if one were doing nothing. For if one is inwardly occupied with the image of any particular virtue, it necessarily follows that the mental image <note>original: "imaginem... figuratam"</note> of that act will be shaped in the soul. And as long as one is held by these forms, one will by no means be able to contemplate. 

Second, it is required that one adhere inwardly to God with an intention and a **joining love** <term>joining love: Latin *dilectio coniunctiva*, a love so intense it acts as a glue, binding the soul's will to God's will</term> like an inextinguishable glowing fire, kindled by divine love. At that time, when one feels this way, one can certainly contemplate. 

Third, it is necessary that in an ecstasy that transcends all human ways <note>original: "in excessu omnis modi"</note>, one has lost oneself in a certain **super-intellectual darkness** <term>super-intellectual darkness: the "Divine Dark" (caligo), a state where God's light is so blindingly bright that the human intellect perceives it as darkness because it cannot be understood by reason</term>. Into this darkness all contemplatives have wandered as if in a wasteland, following the abyss of **fruition** <term>fruition: the state of "resting" in God and enjoying His essence, rather than just thinking about Him</term>, never [finding] themselves...

...being humanly able to find <note>The text here completes a thought from the previous page: that human effort and mental images cannot reach this height.</note>. But in the abyss of this darkness, in which the spirit is dead to itself, every lover becomes a seer. This divine light is given in the simple existence of the spirit itself, where the spirit—above all gifts and every created work—receives that clarity <note>original: "claritas"; in mystical theology, this refers to the radiant glory or brightness of God's presence.</note> which is God, within its own restful vacancy. In this very vacancy, through fruitive <term>fruitive: a technical term for love that finds its end in the sheer enjoyment and possession of the beloved, rather than in action or desire</term> love, the spirit loses itself and receives the clarity of God with no intermediary; and without interruption, the spirit itself becomes that clarity which it receives. Furthermore, this clarity—in which everything desirable is perceived according to the spirit’s state of rest—is so great and immense that the contemplative lover, resting in the depths of the divine abyss, sees or feels nothing else except a certain incomprehensible light. Secondly, the spirit finds the simple serenity of divine clarity and feels itself to be that very light by which it sees, and nothing else. Thus, the first part is clear, which says "Behold": namely, how one becomes a seer when placed in the deiform <note>original: "deiformi"; meaning "God-shaped" or "transformed into the likeness of God."</note> light. And blessed indeed are the eyes that see in this way, for they possess eternal life.

### ¶ On the arrival of the Bridegroom into the contemplating spirit. Chapter III.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'N' containing a small landscape scene with buildings and distant mountains, bordered by ornate floral patterns.</image-desc>Now that we see in this way, we are able to contemplate the arrival of the eternal Bridegroom with inward joy. This is the second point of which we now wish to speak. What then is the eternal arrival of our Bridegroom? It is the very new and eternal generation of the Son, and a fresh illumination without interruption. For the abyss from which that clarity radiates—and which is itself that clarity—is living and fruitful. Therefore, the appearance of eternal light is renewed without ceasing in the secret place of the spirit. However, all created works and all practices of virtue are unable to ascend to this level of contemplation; they fall...

<summary>This page describes the pinnacle of mystical vision, where the soul becomes one with the divine light, and begins a new chapter defining the 'arrival of the Bridegroom' as the eternal birth of the Son within the spirit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mystical union, specifically concluding the section on the arrival of the Bridegroom and beginning Chapter 4 on the "exit" of the spirit to meet Him. It follows the description of the spirit becoming deiform light.</meta>

<header>ON THE ADORNMENT OF SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE</header>

...completely compel them. For here God works alone in the highest nobility of the spirit. Nor is there anything else here but an eternal gazing of light in light and through the mediation of light. The arrival of the bridegroom is also so sudden that it can be said both to have always come and to remain always full of inexhaustible and inscrutable riches; and yet personally, with such new clarity <note>original: "claritate"; refers to the divine radiance or light</note>, he arrives anew without intermission, as if he had never arrived before. For his arrival consists in an eternal "now" without time, which is indeed received with new desire and with new joy continually. Furthermore, the abundance of delights and joys which this bridegroom brings in his arrival is inscrutable and immense, for he is his own delight. For this reason, the eyes with which any spirit contemplates the coming bridegroom are so open that they will never be closed; since the gazing and contemplation of the secret manifestation of God remains eternal. Also, the capacity of the spirit is expanded <note>original: "explosa"; in this context, it suggests being opened up or widened into a vast space</note> with such great breadth for the meeting of the coming bridegroom that the spirit becomes that very breadth which it receives. Thus, therefore, God is comprehended and seen through the mediation of God, in which our whole blessedness <note>original: "beatitudo"</note> consists. This is the second point, which says "the bridegroom comes," in which we are taught how we receive the eternal arrival of our bridegroom in our spirit.

### ¶ ON THE EXIT indeed by which the spirit proceeds contemplatively to meet the bridegroom. Chapter IIII.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring ornate interlacing strapwork and foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

The bridegroom is present. Let us hear, therefore, what he might cry out in the secret depth of our spirit. "Go out, therefore," (he says) "by an exit of eternal contemplation and fruition <term>fruition: from 'fruitio', the mystical state of enjoying and possessing God as one's ultimate end</term> toward the steadfastness of God." For all the riches which are naturally in God, we [possess] in him, and he himself [possesses] in us, through the immense charity <note>original: "charitatem"; here used in the sense of divine, self-giving love</note> of love (which is the Holy Spirit)...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spirit’s mystical union with God, specifically focusing on how the soul is drawn into the life of the Trinity and the "eternal outflowing" of the Word and all creatures from the Father.</meta>

<header>III 75</header>

we possess Him by proxy <note>original: "re vicario"; meaning we possess God through Love as a representative of His essence</note>. For in the embrace of that highest love, every desirable thing is tasted with full satisfaction <note>original: "plenis faucibus degustatur"; literally "tasted with full throats," suggesting a hearty or complete spiritual consumption</note>. Through this love, therefore, we are dead to ourselves, and through the overflowing of love in an ecstasy of the mind, we have passed into the darkness. There indeed the spirit is held fast by a certain embrace of the most blessed Trinity, remaining eternally in that <term>super-essential unity</term> <note>a state of being that is beyond all created essence or understanding</note>, enjoying a happy rest. In this very unity, however, according to the mode of fruitfulness, the Father is in the Son, and the Son in the Father, and in both, all creatures are present alike.

Truly, this is above the distinction of the divine Persons. For even in the living fruitfulness of the divine nature, fatherhood and sonship are discerned by the light of reason. But at this level, there arises and begins an eternal outflowing <note>original: "exitus"</note> and an eternal work without beginning; because here is a certain beginning without a beginning. For from the fact that the almighty Father, in the abyss of His own fruitfulness, fully comprehends Himself, the Son has gone forth—the eternal <term>Word</term> from the Father—another Person within the Godhead. 

But also, through the eternal generation of the Word, all creatures have gone forth eternally before they were created in time. God saw and knew them in a certain "otherness" under the distinct <term>archetypal forms</term> <note>original: "rationes idearum"; the eternal patterns or ideas of all things existing in the mind of God</note>; yet they were not in every way other than Himself, because whatever is in God, is God. 

This eternal outflowing and perpetual life—which we have and are eternally in God without any cooperation of our own—is the original cause of our created being in time. Our created being depends upon this eternal being, and according to its existence, it is one with it. Furthermore, this eternal being and life which we have and are in the eternal wisdom of God—as if in the mind of the supreme Craftsman <note>original: "superni artificis"</note> through archetypal forms—is like unto God. For it has an eternal remaining without difference in the divine being, and a perpetual outflowing through the grace of the Word under a distinct "otherness" according to the archetypal pattern of the eternal art. Through these two,

# THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL MARRIAGE

that is, through essential identity and an "ideal" otherness <note>original: "alteritatem idealem"; this refers to how things are distinct from God as "ideas" in His mind, even while their existence is rooted in His one essence</note>. This very life which we possess in the mind of the heavenly Architect is so similar to our Creator, God, that in this very similarity God recognizes Himself without ceasing, as if in His own image—both according to His essence and according to the three Persons. For although there is a distinction and an otherness here defined by reason, nevertheless this similarity is one with the image and the form of the Father’s substance. This is the begotten Wisdom, the <term>Word</term> of the Father, in whom God sees Himself and all things in the indivisible "now" of eternity, without any succession of "before" or "after." Truly, God contemplates Himself and all things with a single, simple vision. 

Furthermore, this eternal Word is the image and likeness of the Father; but for us (who were created in His image and likeness), He is our **exemplar** <note>the original pattern or blueprint after which something is modeled</note>. He is the "spotless mirror of the majesty of God" <note>a reference to the Book of Wisdom 7:26</note> and the "art" of the Almighty, filled with the eternal blueprints of all living things. For in Him, everything that has been made possesses a certain eternal life, as if in its own perpetual model, according to whose image and likeness the Trinity created us. 

Therefore, God commands that we go out from ourselves into this divine light, and that we supernaturally attain this image of God the Father—which is our own exemplar and proper life—so that we may possess it with Him in the glory of active and <term>fruitive</term> <note>original: "fruitiuae"; referring to the quiet, blissful "enjoyment" of God as an end in Himself</note> blessedness. For when we contemplate with the eyes of the mind through divine grace, we perceive that the "bosom of the Father" is the origin and the beginning of our being and our life. From this source, and from all who live within Him, a certain eternal brightness shines forth: that is, the generation of the everlasting Son. In this brightness, the Father Himself becomes known to Himself, along with all those living within Him. For besides the unique property of His Fatherhood, the Father, through this act of generation, bestows upon the Son His own essence along with everything that belongs to Him... 

<meta>The text cuts off at "elargitur," the Latin verb for "bestows" or "grants," which continues on the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page explores the Neoplatonic and mystical idea that all creatures exist eternally as "ideas" or "exemplars" within the Word of God (the Son) before their physical creation. It describes the Trinity's internal life as a "spotless mirror" where God contemplates Himself and humanity simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>Eternal Word, Exemplar, Divine Architect, Image and Likeness, Trinity, Generation of the Son, Fruitive Beatitude, Essential Identity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's relationship to God as its source. It transitions from the idea of the soul being created in God's image to the "contemplative" state where the soul returns to its uncreated essence within the Word.</meta>

III 76

transformed. Therefore, whatever lives in the Father, hidden in unity, also lives in the Son, flowing forth in appearance. But the simple **abyss** <note>original: "abyssus"; in mystical theology, this refers to the bottomless, unfathomable depth of the divine essence</note> of our **exemplar** <note>the eternal blueprint or "idea" of a person existing in the mind of God</note> always "makes darkness its hiding place," <note>a reference to Psalm 18:11</note> utterly excluding every "mode" <note>the specific ways or forms in which things exist or are understood</note>; yet the immeasurable and radiating **brightness** <note>original: "claritas"; often translated as "glory" or "divine light"</note> within it brings forth the hiddenness of God into a mode and makes it manifest. All those who are raised above their created state into the height of the contemplative life are one with this deifying brightness; indeed, they are that brightness themselves. 

Accordingly, they see, and they feel, and they find—through this deifying light—themselves according to their being and their uncreated life; and they find that this life is the very simple abyss of the divinity, from which the brightness itself shines back in a divine way, and in which, according to the simplicity of essence, a simple intellect remains eternally without mode. Therefore, inward contemplatives must go out with an eternal gaze, according to the mode of contemplation, above the distinction of reason and above their own created being, by the power of the uncreated light that is divinely generated. And so, they will be transformed "from glory to glory" <note>original: "de claritate in claritatem"; a reference to 2 Corinthians 3:18</note> as if by the spirit of the Lord; and they shall become one light with that by which they see and that which they see. 

In this way, then, contemplatives attain their **exemplar**, in whose image they were created; they contemplate God and all things without the difference of distinction, using a simple gaze within the divine brightness. For this is the most worthy and most useful contemplation that can be attained in this life, since in it the contemplative remains better master of himself and free. He is able, in any loving inward-turning, to progress into the height of life beyond all grasp of the intelligence, because he remains free and master of himself in the internal fervor of his devotion and the exercise of virtues. Indeed, that simple gaze into the divine light of contemplation is above all...

<summary>This page describes the mystical process of "transformation" where the contemplative soul, through divine light, realizes its uncreated existence within the "abyss" of God. It emphasizes that this state allows the person to see God and themselves as one light while remaining free and virtuous in their earthly life.</summary>
<keywords>contemplation, divine light, abyss of God, exemplar, uncreated life, transformation, mystical union, deification</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the contemplative soul’s ascent. The previous text described how the soul is transformed by divine light; this passage concludes that thought by describing the soul's entry into the "riches of God" and begins a new chapter on the final stage of the mystical journey: the "loving meeting" between the spirit and the Bridegroom.</meta>

-># ON THE ORNAMENT OF THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS<-

...by the fervor of devotion, [the spirit] holds itself above the virtues and every promise. For He Himself is the crown and the prize for which we pant with inward desire: and which, in a certain way, we already have and possess. For the **contemplative life** is clearly a celestial life. If indeed we were liberated from the death of this body, we would surely be more fit—according to the capacity of our creation—to receive **divine brightness** <note>original: "claritatem"; the radiating glory of God</note>. Then the glory of God would be able to penetrate us better and more nobly with its light. This is the mode that exceeds all modes: namely, the way one goes out into divine and continuous contemplation, and the way in which the sublime contemplative is transformed and enveloped by **deifying brightness**. This "going out" <note>original: "exitus"; the soul's ecstatic movement beyond its own limits toward God</note> of the inward contemplative is also formed by **charity**. For through **fruitive love** <note>original: "fruitiuum amorem"; a technical term for the soul's blissful "enjoyment" of God for His own sake</note> he passes beyond his own created state; he finds and tastes the riches and delights that God is Himself, which God causes to flow down into the hidden spirit. There, the spirit holds the highest likeness to divine nobility.

### ¶ ON THE MEETING of the spirit and the bridegroom. Chapter 5.

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter 'C' featuring a stag standing in a forest landscape within a square frame with floral motifs.</image-desc>Therefore, when the inward contemplative has thus attained his **eternal exemplar** <note>the "blueprint" or eternal idea of the soul as it exists in the mind of God</note> and has possessed the bosom of the Father through the Son in this deiform **clarity** <note>original: "lympiditate"; literally a state of being clear or transparent</note> of his spirit, he is already then illustrated by the light of divine truth. He receives the eternal generation of the Son as something new in every single moment. And according to the mode of that light, he goes out into divine contemplation. But here also arises the fourth and final point: namely, the **loving meeting** <note>original: "obuiatio"; the encounter or "coming-against" of the soul and God</note> in which our highest happiness consists above all else. It must be known that the Father, as the living origin of the Trinity, is actively turned toward the Son—as toward His own eternal and divine wisdom—together with all things living in Him.

<summary>This page describes the transition of the soul into "fruitive love," where it tastes the divine essence, and introduces the final stage of the mystical union: the "loving meeting" (obuiatio) between the spirit and God.</summary>
<keywords>contemplative life, deification, fruitive love, divine brightness, eternal exemplar, Trinity, Jan van Ruusbroec, spiritual marriage</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 5 regarding the "meeting" of the spirit and the Bridegroom. While the previous page introduced the concept of the soul's ascent and its transformation by divine light, this page delves into the Trinitarian nature of that meeting, describing the internal relationship between the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit as the source of the soul's ultimate peace.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>III</header>

...and that this same wisdom and all the living beings within it are actually reflected back into the Father as their source (from which they originated). From this encounter of the Father and the Son, there proceeds the third person in the Trinity: namely the Holy Spirit, the love and the bond of both, in the identity of one and the same nature. Indeed, he embraces and penetrates the Father, the Son, and all living things within them with such an immensity of riches and joys—both actively and through enjoyment—that it is necessary for all creatures to remain eternally silent hereafter. For these incomprehensible and wonderful things (which are contained in this most infinite treasury of love) perpetually exceed the understanding of all creatures. 

But where these wondrous secrets are understood and tasted together without any mere astonishment, there the spirit is translated beyond itself and brought to life by God—tasting and seeing, in the unity of the living abyss <note>original: "abyssus"; a common mystical term for the bottomless depth of God's nature</note> (in which the spirit possesses itself according to its own uncreated being), the immense riches which the spirit itself is, according to the way in which God tastes and sees them. 

Furthermore, this kind of divine meeting, most full of pleasure, is actually renewed in us without any shadow of change. For the Father and the Son, with eternal pleasure and an embrace of love, pour themselves into each other—a process which is also continually renewed by the eternal bond of love. For just as the Father contemplates all things anew in the generation of the Son, so also are all things freshly loved by the Father and the Son in the procession <note>original: "processione"; the theological term for the Holy Spirit's origin from the Father and the Son</note> of the Holy Spirit. 

This is the actual meeting of the Father and the Son, in which we are lovingly encircled through the Holy Spirit with perpetual love. But this actual meeting and this lovely embrace is, in its depth, "fruitive" <note>original: "fruitiuus"; relating to *fruitio*, the blissful, direct enjoyment of God as the ultimate end</note> and knows no mode <note>original: "modi nescius"; existing beyond any human method, measure, or limited way of being</note>. For the very vastness of the infinite Deity is so shrouded in darkness <note>original: "caliginosa"; referring to the "Divine Dark" or "Cloud of Unknowing" where the human intellect cannot penetrate</note> and beyond any mode, that it encompasses every mode and every divine work, along with the properties of the divine persons, in that rich embrace of essential unity—generating divine enjoyment in the very abyss of the unnamed Divinity.

<summary>This page describes the inner life of the Trinity as an eternal, loving encounter between the Father and the Son, which produces the Holy Spirit. The author explains that the contemplative soul is drawn into this "abyss" of divine love, where it experiences God's own joy beyond human understanding.</summary>

<keywords>Trinity, Holy Spirit, divine love, abyss, fruitio, contemplative life, Father and Son, divine darkness</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page concludes the third and final book of Jan van Ruusbroec’s *The Spiritual Espousals*. It moves from the Trinitarian encounter described on the previous page to the final state of "fruition"—the total, quiet immersion of the soul into the "dark silence" of the Divine Essence. The page also includes Ruusbroec's concluding remarks regarding the translation of his work from Middle Dutch into Latin.</meta>

# ON THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS

At this level, a certain fruitive excess <note>original: "fruitiuus excessus"; a technical mystical term for an ecstatic state where the soul "goes out" of itself to enjoy God</note> is celebrated, as well as a flowing entrance into the very simplicity of the essence. In this, as if into the unnamed abyss of the deity, all divine names, every mode, and all living reasons—shining in the mirror of divine truth—are absorbed, as if into a whirlpool of all names, modes, and reasons. For in this unsearchable abyss of divine simplicity and enjoyment, all things are embraced in blessedness; yet that very abyss remains utterly incomprehensible in its essential unity. Here, one must believe that the persons <note>referring to the three Persons of the Trinity</note> and everything living in God are present; for here there is nothing else but eternal rest in the fruitive embrace of loving absorption.

This is accomplished in the most simple essence of the divine unity, which utterly excludes every mode <note>original: "modum"; in this context, "modes" are the distinct ways things exist or are perceived; in the ultimate union, these distinctions vanish</note>; it is this which all contemplative spirits have desired above all things. For it is itself a dark silence in which all lovers have lost themselves. If we could thus prepare ourselves with virtues, we would quickly depart from this "body of death" and swim in the clear waves of the depths of the vast sea. No creature would ever be able to find us by following.

But so that we may possess the very essence of divine unity in the rest of fruition, and see the unity in the Trinity with a clear gaze, may divine charity <note>original: "charitas"; the theological term for supernatural love</note>—the giver of all gifts and the merciful responder to every seeker—grant this to us; she who reigns with the Father and the Son for ever and ever. Amen.

**THESE ARE** the most dear words which we previously wrote in a simple manner in the Teutonic tongue <note>original: "teutonico sermone"; referring to Middle Dutch, the language in which the author, Jan van Ruusbroec, originally composed his works</note> for the simple and unlearned laity, according to how the Lord inspired us. Now, at your command, we have translated not the literal Teutonic words, but the sense recollected in the mind into Latin speech—laboriously enough, according to our small ability, yet with God's help—earnestly requesting that you repay our labor with your prayers...

<summary>This concluding page describes the soul's final absorption into the "dark silence" of the divine essence and provides an authorial postscript explaining the text's translation from Middle Dutch into Latin.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruusbroec, mysticism, divine abyss, dark silence, Trinity, fruition, Middle Dutch, Latin translation, spiritual union</keywords>

<meta>This page contains the theological conclusion of Book III of Jan van Ruusbroec’s "The Spiritual Espousals," followed by the beginning of the translator’s epilogue. It leads directly into the final page previously translated, which ends with the printer's colophon.</meta>

# ON THE SPIRITUAL ESPOUSALS

In this stage, a certain fruitful ecstasy <note>original: "fruitivus excessus"; a mystical state of 'going out' of oneself to enjoy the divine</note> is celebrated,
and an entry into the simplicity of the Essence itself flows forth.
In this Essence—as if into the unnamed abyss of the Godhead—all 
divine names, any modes, and all living principles 
shining back in the mirror of divine truth, are absorbed as if into a 
whirlpool of all names, modes, and principles. For in this 
unsearchable abyss of divine simplicity and enjoyment, 
all things are embraced in blessedness; yet that very abyss 
remains utterly uncomprehended in its essential unity. 
Here one must believe in the [Divine] Persons and all things 
living in God; for here there is nothing else 
but eternal rest in the fruitful embrace of loving absorption. 
This takes place in the most simple essence of divine unity, 
which utterly excludes every mode <note>Ruusbroec often describes the highest union with God as "modeless" (sine modo), meaning it transcends all human methods or specific ways of being</note>, which all 
contemplative spirits have desired above all things. 
For it is itself a dark silence <note>original: "caliginosum silentium"</note> in which all 
lovers have lost themselves. If we could so prepare 
ourselves with virtues, we would quickly depart from this 
body of death and swim in the clear waves of the depths 
of the vast sea; nor would any creature ever find us 
by following. But that we may possess the very essence 
of divine unity in the rest of enjoyment, and see the 
unity in the trinity with a clear gaze, may divine charity—
the giver of all things and the merciful hearer of 
every petitioner—grant it to us; she who reigns with 
the Father and the Son for ever and ever. Amen.

¶ THESE ARE the most precious words which we 
previously wrote simply in the Teutonic tongue <note>original: "teutonico sermone"; referring to the Middle Dutch or Flemish in which Ruusbroec originally composed his works</note> for 
simple and unlearned laypeople (just as the Lord inspired). 
Now, at your command, we have translated into Latin—
not the Teutonic words, but the sense gathered 
in the mind—laboriously enough (as we were able 
according to our small ability, yet with God's help), 
asking earnestly that [you repay] our labor with your prayers...

...may you return your prayers. Farewell: or rather, let us together fare well in the Lord.
